# capital_lab — DEEP STRATEGY LIBRARY

> Source-grounded depth across 14 trading-strategy families, produced before the
> build (operator mandate 2026-06-06: load deep from the real literature, not
> retail prompts). Companion to NORTH_STAR_ARCHITECTURE.md, ROADMAP_TO_PARITY.md,
> and STRATEGY_SEEDS_IG_12.md. Every dossier reports HONEST decay/crowding — what
> is genuinely tradeable vs already arbitraged away — and the anti-Profit-Mirage
> validation method. This is the knowledge base R3/R4/R5 build against.

**Reading note:** "Evidence (honest)" separates the headline backtest number from
the net-of-cost / out-of-sample reality. "Decay/crowding" is the verdict that
matters most — a strategy everyone knows is a strategy that no longer pays.

---

## event-sentiment-nlp

**Thesis.** This family monetizes slow human information processing around discrete corporate events. The core economic edge is underreaction: investors fail to fully incorporate the implications of a single earnings number for the FULL path of future earnings (Bernard-Thomas 1989), and they underreact to soft/textual information (tone, language change, news sentiment) that is costly to read and quantify (Tetlock 2007/2008; Cohen-Malloy-Nguyen 2020). Why it persists: (1) limited attention — retail and even institutional investors cannot process every 8-K, transcript, and news wire in real time (Da-Engelberg-Gao 2011; the 'Lazy Prices' no-announcement-effect result is the cleanest proof of inattention); (2) limits to arbitrage — drift concentrates in small, illiquid, high-idio-vol, high-transaction-cost names where arbitrageurs can't cheaply force prices to fundamentals (Mendenhall 2004; Chordia-Subrahmanyam-Tong 2014); (3) gradual information diffusion — text-based fundamentals leak into prices over weeks. The behavioral half (Tetlock's media-pessimism) is partly a temporary-price-pressure / noise-trader effect that REVERTS, distinct from the fundamental-underreaction half (drift that PERSISTS) — conflating the two is the #1 conceptual error. The family DECAYS predictably: post-publication and as arbitrage capital, liquidity, and now LLM news-readers crowd in (McLean-Pontiff 2016: ~58% post-publication decay; PEAD has fallen from ~18-35%/yr in the 1980s toward near-insignificance in large-cap US). The persistent residual lives where arbitrage stays expensive: micro/small-cap, low-liquidity, high-short-interest-cost names — i.e. exactly the capacity-constrained corner.

**Concrete strategies:**
- **Standardized-Unexpected-Earnings (SUE) PEAD — numeric core**
  - *Signal:* SUE_it = (E_it - E_i,t-4) / sigma(seasonal-difference earnings), i.e. actual quarterly EPS minus EPS four quarters ago, scaled by the rolling std of those seasonal differences (8-20 quarters). Alternative analyst-based SUE: (actual - median I/B/E/S consensus just before announcement) / price or / std of forecasts. Rank into deciles each quarter; go LONG decile 10 (positive surprise) SHORT decile 1; enter ~1-2 days AFTER the announcement (avoid the announcement jump you cannot capture); hold ~60 trading days (one quarter), exit before next announcement. Also use the announcement-window 3-day CAR as an alternative surprise proxy ('CAR-based PEAD' — more robust than analyst SUE in some samples).
  - *Params:* Lookback for sigma: 8-20 quarters. Deciles (10) or quintiles. Entry lag: 1-2 days. Holding: ~60 trading days. Rebalance: as earnings arrive (event-time, not calendar). Neutralize size/sector.  ·  *Assets:* US (and global) single-name equities; strongest in small/mid-cap. Adaptable to any market with quarterly/semi-annual reporting.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Bernard-Thomas 1989/1990: ~8-9%/qtr (~35% ann.) PRE-cost, in-sample. Garfinkel-Hribar-Hsiao 2024: ~5.1% risk-adj over 3mo (~20%/yr). Mendenhall 2004 & Chordia et al 2014: drift concentrates in high-arbitrage-cost names; net of realistic transaction costs in liquid large-caps it is small/insignificant today. Honest read: headline numbers are gross, in-sample, pre-cost; tradable residual is small-cap and capacity-limited.
  - *Decay/crowding:* HEAVILY decayed in liquid US large-cap (from ~18-35%/yr 1980s toward near-zero). Chordia-Subrahmanyam-Tong 2014: liquidity & arbitrage capital attenuate it. Survives in illiquid/high-cost names — which is exactly where you cannot trade it cheaply. Treat as a known, crowded, capacity-bound factor, not a standalone edge.
- **Text-based earnings surprise (PEAD.txt style)**
  - *Signal:* Instead of (or alongside) numeric SUE, vectorize the EARNINGS-RELATED text (8-K Item 2.02 press release, MD&A, conference-call transcript) and learn a supervised mapping from text features -> subsequent return / earnings, OR compute a tone/novelty score. PEAD.txt (Zhao et al., JFQA 2023) builds a text-similarity / supervised text-surprise signal and shows text-based drift that is largely ORTHOGONAL to and not subsumed by numeric SUE — combining the two improves the long-short. Construction: TF-IDF or domain-embedding features -> ridge/elastic-net or gradient-boosted prediction of forward CAR -> decile sort -> long-short held ~quarter.
  - *Params:* Feature set: Loughran-McDonald counts + n-grams + embeddings. Model: regularized linear or GBM (avoid deep nets that memorize). Same entry-lag/holding as numeric PEAD (1-2 day lag, ~60-day hold). Strict purged/embargoed CV on event time.  ·  *Assets:* US equities with machine-readable filings/transcripts; extends to any market with electronic disclosure.
  - *Evidence (honest):* PEAD.txt (Philadelphia Fed WP 21-07 / JFQA 2023): text signal generates significant drift incremental to numeric SUE; the two are complementary (text captures qualitative surprise the number misses). Reported as gross/in-sample academic returns — apply costs + deflated-Sharpe before believing. The honest claim is 'text adds orthogonal information,' not a specific net Sharpe you can bank.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Newer and less crowded than numeric PEAD, but the easy version (Loughran-McDonald tone) is now table-stakes and likely arbitraged in large-caps. The orthogonality-to-SUE result is the durable insight; raw tone alpha is decaying as everyone runs the same dictionary.
- **News-sentiment level signal (Tetlock / RavenPack-style)**
  - *Signal:* For each firm-day, aggregate sentiment of firm-specific news: score = sum over articles of polarity * relevance * novelty (RavenPack ESS/event-relevance), or fraction-negative-words (Loughran-McDonald) on news wires. Trade the EXTREMES: long very-positive, short very-negative flow, BUT distinguish horizon — fundamental-news sentiment (Tetlock 2008 'fundamentals-focused stories') predicts earnings and DRIFTS (hold days-weeks); generic media pessimism (Tetlock 2007) produces short-run pressure that REVERTS within ~1 week (fade it / mean-revert, don't chase). Use article relevance and novelty filters to drop stale/duplicate wires.
  - *Params:* Aggregation window: 1-3 days. Decay/half-life on sentiment: ~1-5 days. Relevance threshold (RavenPack >= 75/90). Novelty filter to dedupe. Separate 'fundamental' vs 'noise' stories. Holding: 1-10 days depending on horizon. Neutralize beta/size.  ·  *Assets:* Global equities (RavenPack docs report IRs near 4.0 and >20% ann. in APAC, IR 1.82-2.64 in their insider+sentiment studies — VENDOR studies, treat skeptically); also FX/commodities via macro-sentiment.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Tetlock 2007/2008: statistically robust in-sample but ECONOMICALLY small and partly REVERSAL (noise-trader pressure), not pure alpha. RavenPack vendor studies show high IRs but are self-published, in-sample, and gross — classic vendor-backtest optimism; independently the effect is much weaker and fast-decaying. Net-of-cost edge is fragile because the signal is high-turnover.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Strongly crowded — news-sentiment feeds are commoditized and traded by everyone at low latency. The level signal in liquid names is largely arbitraged; surviving edge is in speed (event-driven, sub-second), in less-covered names, and in the reversal leg of pure-noise pessimism.
- **Merger / risk arbitrage (deal-spread)**
  - *Signal:* On a cash-deal announcement, BUY target at market (price P_t) below offer (P_offer); profit = spread (P_offer - P_t)/P_t annualized over expected days-to-close, IF deal completes. For stock deals, short the acquirer in the exchange ratio. Position size inversely to estimated break probability. The spread compensates for deal-break risk (regulatory, financing, shareholder vote). Screen on spread, time-to-close, hostile/friendly, regulatory overhang, financing certainty.
  - *Params:* Entry: post-announcement. Hold: to close (avg ~3-6 months). Spread threshold vs estimated annualized break-adjusted return. Diversify across many deals (idiosyncratic break risk). Hedge market beta (acquirer short / index).  ·  *Assets:* Equities — announced M&A targets/acquirers, US + global.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Mitchell-Pulvino 2001: 4,750 deals 1963-1998; ~6.2% raw annualized, ~4% EXCESS net of transaction costs. Payoff = SHORT uncovered index put: fine in calm/up markets, severe losses in crashes (deals break en masse). So the '4% excess' is insurance premium, not arbitrage — and it is left-tail-loaded.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Mature, crowded, capacity-constrained by deal flow; spreads are tight in vanilla deals. Edge survives in complex/regulatory-risk deals where pricing the break probability is hard. Crash-correlated — sizing and tail-hedging dominate returns.
- **Document-change (Lazy Prices) drift**
  - *Signal:* Compute quarter-on-quarter textual similarity of each firm's 10-K/10-Q (cosine on TF-IDF, plus Jaccard, min-edit-distance, simple-similarity as robustness). Firms whose filings barely change ('non-changers') outperform; firms with large negative changes underperform. Go LONG non-changers, SHORT changers (especially those adding risk-factor/litigious language). No announcement effect -> you have weeks to build positions.
  - *Params:* Similarity metric: cosine (primary) + 3 robustness metrics. Rank quintiles by change. Long-short. Hold ~1-3 months (filing-to-filing). Section-level (Item 1A Risk Factors, MD&A) often stronger than whole-doc.  ·  *Assets:* US equities with EDGAR filings; extends to any market with standardized periodic disclosure.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Cohen-Malloy-Nguyen 2020: up to 188 bps/month (~22%/yr) alpha long-short, predicts earnings, news, even bankruptcy; NO announcement effect (clean inattention). In-sample/gross academic numbers — net of small-cap transaction costs and post-2020 crowding the live Sharpe is materially lower. Still one of the better-documented document-NLP anomalies.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Published 2020 -> expect McLean-Pontiff-style decay underway as quant funds run EDGAR-diff pipelines. The whole-doc cosine version is now common; section-level and embedding-based change detection (semantic, not lexical) is the less-crowded frontier.
- **LLM/transcript signal done HONESTLY (FinBERT + counterfactual gate)**
  - *Signal:* Use a FROZEN, audited domain model (FinBERT) — not a closed frontier model out-of-the-box — to score earnings-call Q&A tone, analyst-report tone, or news. Build forward-return prediction, then GATE it: (1) only score text DATED after the model's pretraining cutoff (or use DatedGPT-style time-aware models); (2) run the Lookahead-Propensity test (LAP vs accuracy) — if positive, the signal is recall not reasoning; (3) run FactFin-style counterfactual perturbations (swap numbers/sentiment in the text) and require the prediction to MOVE (low Prediction-Consistency, high Input-Dependency-Score). Only deploy the part of the signal that survives all three.
  - *Params:* Model: FinBERT (frozen) or time-controlled LLM. Counterfactual perturbation set: price/earnings/sentiment swaps. Acceptance thresholds: PC < ~0.5, IDS high, Memory-Score low, LAP not correlated with accuracy. Strict post-cutoff out-of-sample window.  ·  *Assets:* US/global equities via transcripts, news, filings.
  - *Evidence (honest):* FinBERT (Huang et al 2023): ~88-89% tone accuracy — but tone accuracy != trading alpha. Profit Mirage 2025: naive LLM agents lose 50-72% of backtest return out-of-window; StockBench 2025: most LLMs (incl GPT-5/Claude-4) fail to beat buy-and-hold and ALL fail in downturns. So the honest expectation is MODEST incremental signal, heavily contingent on leakage control — not the dazzling backtests vendors show.
  - *Decay/crowding:* The hype version (frontier LLM reads news, prints alpha) is mostly leakage/memorization, not edge — it 'decays' the instant you leave the training window (i.e. it was never real). The genuine residual (frozen-model tone orthogonal to LM dictionary, on post-cutoff data) is small but real and currently under-crowded BECAUSE most participants are fooled by leakage in the other direction.

**Canonical sources:**
- *Post-Earnings-Announcement Drift: Delayed Price Response or Risk Premium?* — Victor L. Bernard & Jacob K. Thomas (1989, paper): THE founding PEAD paper. Establishes SUE-decile long-short, documents ~8-9%/quarter (~35% annualized pre-cost) drift, and the 'investors fail to recognize implications of current earnings for future earnings' mechanism. Fama called PEAD the 'granddaddy of underreaction events.' Every event-drift strategy descends from this. — https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/01354e373f23983ac962c8b133e668332ec26da9
- *Evidence that Stock Prices Do Not Fully Reflect the Implications of Current Earnings for Future Earnings* — Victor L. Bernard & Jacob K. Thomas (1990, paper): The mechanism paper: prices behave as if investors model earnings as a random walk when the true process is a seasonal AR; the predictable 'earnings autocorrelation' is the source of drift. Gives you the SUE construction and the structural reason the anomaly exists. — https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/0022-1082.00401
- *Giving Content to Investor Sentiment: The Role of Media in the Stock Market* — Paul C. Tetlock (2007, paper): Founding text-sentiment-and-returns paper. WSJ 'Abreast of the Market' column 1984-1999, Harvard-IV negative-word factor via PCA. High media pessimism -> short-run downward price pressure that REVERTS to fundamentals within days; predicts trading volume. The canonical demonstration that sentiment is a measurable, tradable, but largely temporary (noise/liquidity) signal. — https://www.columbia.edu/~pt2238/papers/Tetlock_Media_Sentiment_JF.pdf
- *More Than Words: Quantifying Language to Measure Firms' Fundamentals* — Paul C. Tetlock, Maytal Saar-Tsechansky, Sofus Macskassy (2008, paper): Distinguishes the FUNDAMENTAL half of sentiment from the noise half. Fraction of negative words in firm-specific news forecasts low earnings; prices briefly underreact; predictability is largest for fundamentals-focused stories. This is the news-sentiment-as-earnings-predictor blueprint and pairs with PEAD. — http://www.columbia.edu/~pt2238/papers/TSM_More_Than_Words_08_06.pdf
- *When Is a Liability Not a Liability? Textual Analysis, Dictionaries, and 10-Ks* — Tim Loughran & Bill McDonald (2011, paper): The domain-specific finance sentiment dictionary (the URL is the free downloadable lexicon + master dictionary). Shows ~3/4 of Harvard-IV 'negative' words (e.g. tax, cost, liability, depreciation) are NOT negative in finance. Six lists: negative, positive, uncertainty, litigious, constraining, superfluous. Mandatory baseline before any LLM signal — your anti-overfit control. — https://sraf.nd.edu/loughranmcdonald-master-dictionary/
- *In Search of Attention* — Zhi Da, Joseph Engelberg, Pengjie Gao (2011, paper): Direct retail-attention measure via Google Search Volume Index (SVI/ASVI). Attention spike -> +price over 2 weeks then REVERSAL within a year; drives IPO first-day pop and long-run underperformance. The template for alternative-data attention signals (and a clean reversal-not-drift example). — https://www3.nd.edu/~zda/google.pdf
- *Lazy Prices* — Lauren Cohen, Christopher Malloy, Quoc Nguyen (2020, paper): Text-CHANGE (not level) signal: quarter-on-quarter similarity of 10-K/10-Q language (cosine/Jaccard/edit-distance). Short 'changers', long 'non-changers' -> up to 188 bps/month (~22%/yr) alpha, no announcement effect (pure inattention). The cleanest modern document-NLP anomaly and a leakage-resistant signal construction. — https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w25084/w25084.pdf
- *Characteristics of Risk and Return in Risk Arbitrage* — Mark Mitchell & Todd Pulvino (2001, paper): Definitive merger-arb paper. 4,750 deals 1963-1998; ~4% excess/yr net of transaction costs; returns mimic a SHORT uncovered index put (calm markets fine, crashes hurt) — the payoff is selling deal-break insurance, not free arbitrage. Essential for honest risk framing of event-arb. — https://tevgeniou.github.io/EquityRiskFactors/bibliography/RiskArbitrage.pdf
- *Profit Mirage: Revisiting Information Leakage in LLM-based Financial Agents* — (arXiv 2510.07920) (2025, paper): THE anti-leakage paper for LLM signals. Defines Memory Score, Prediction Consistency, Input Dependency Score; shows backtest Sharpe decays 51-62% and total return 50-72% once you leave the model's knowledge window; FinLake-Bench (2000 QA, 85-93% accuracy = memorization). FactFin counterfactual method recovers ~1.4x OOS Sharpe. Your canonical validation doctrine. — https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07920
- *StockBench: Can LLM Agents Trade Stocks Profitably in Real-World Markets?* — Chen et al. (arXiv 2510.02209) (2025, paper): Contamination-free, forward-out-of-sample (Mar-Jun 2025, post-cutoff) LLM trading benchmark across 14 models incl. GPT-5/Claude-4/Qwen3/Kimi-K2. Headline: most LLM agents fail to beat buy-and-hold; ALL fail in downturns. The empirical sobriety check against 'GPT trades stocks' hype. — https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.02209
- *A Test of Lookahead Bias in LLM Forecasts* — Zhenyu Gao, Wenxi Jiang, Yutong Yan (arXiv 2512.23847) (2025, paper): Gives a STATISTICAL TEST (Lookahead Propensity, LAP, via pretraining-data-detection) for whether an LLM forecast is recall vs reasoning. Positive LAP-vs-accuracy correlation = lookahead bias. The rigorous gate to put in front of any LLM news/transcript signal before it touches capital. — https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23847
- *The Deflated Sharpe Ratio / The Probability of Backtest Overfitting* — David Bailey & Marcos Lopez de Prado (2014, paper): The multiple-testing correction. Adjusts a reported Sharpe for number of trials, skew, kurtosis, and sample length. PBO (combinatorially-symmetric CV) estimates the probability your selected config is overfit. Non-negotiable for an event/sentiment book where thousands of dictionary/lookback/threshold variants get tried. — https://www.davidhbailey.com/dhbpapers/deflated-sharpe.pdf
- *Does Academic Research Destroy Stock Return Predictability?* — R. David McLean & Jeffrey Pontiff (2016, paper): The decay law. 97 published predictors: returns ~26% lower out-of-sample, ~58% lower post-publication. Decay concentrates in high-idio-vol, low-liquidity names (limits to arbitrage). Tells you to haircut every published event/sentiment Sharpe and where the residual survives. — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2156623
- *Advances in Financial Machine Learning* — Marcos Lopez de Prado (2018, book): The methodology bible: triple-barrier labeling, meta-labeling, purged & embargoed K-fold CV (kills the leakage that ordinary CV hides in overlapping event windows), fractional differentiation, feature importance under multicollinearity. The how-to for building event-ML without fooling yourself. — https://www.wiley.com/en-us/Advances+in+Financial+Machine+Learning-p-9781119482086
- *FinBERT: A Large Language Model for Extracting Information from Financial Text* — Allen Huang, Hui Wang, Yi Yang (2023 (working 2020), paper): The practitioner-grade domain LLM: BERT pre-trained on corporate/analyst reports + transcripts; ~88-89% tone accuracy on AnalystTone. The bridge between dictionary methods and frontier LLMs — frozen weights, auditable, far less leakage-prone than a closed frontier model used out-of-the-box. — https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/1911-3846.12832

**Pitfalls:** (1) LOOK-AHEAD / MEMORIZATION (the family-killer for LLM signals): a frontier LLM scoring pre-cutoff news 'predicts' returns because it MEMORIZED the outcome, not because it reasons. Profit Mirage shows 50-72% return decay and 85-93% memorization accuracy on FinLake-Bench; the Lookahead-Propensity test exists precisely to catch this. Any LLM backtest on pre-cutoff data is presumptively contaminated until proven otherwise. (2) Using a general-purpose sentiment dictionary (Harvard-IV, VADER) on financial text: ~3/4 of Harvard 'negative' words (tax, cost, liability, depreciation, capital) are neutral in finance (Loughran-McDonald 2011). This silently injects sector/leverage tilts and fake sentiment. Always use the finance lexicon as the baseline control. (3) Conflating REVERSAL with DRIFT: Tetlock-2007 media pessimism and Da-Engelberg-Gao attention spikes REVERT (short-lived pressure); Bernard-Thomas SUE drift and Lazy-Prices change PERSIST. Trading them with the same sign/horizon guarantees you fight yourself. Separate the noise-pressure leg (fade) from the fundamental-underreaction leg (follow). (4) Announcement-jump capture illusion: backtests that enter AT the earnings/announcement print capture a jump no live trader gets (you trade after, with slippage). Enter 1-2 days post-event; the drift, not the jump, is the harvestable part. (5) Transaction-cost and capacity blindness: PEAD/Lazy-Prices alpha concentrates in small, illiquid, high-short-cost names (Mendenhall 2004; Chordia et al 2014). Headline academic Sharpes are gross; net of spread+impact+borrow in the names that actually carry the signal, much of it evaporates. The liquid-large-cap version is already arbitraged. (6) Multiple-testing / dictionary-and-lookback mining: trying many sentiment lexicons, aggregation windows, SUE lookbacks, decile cuts, and reporting the best is textbook backtest overfitting. Without deflated-Sharpe / PBO correction the reported t-stat is meaningless (Bailey-Lopez de Prado). (7) Ordinary K-fold CV leaks across overlapping event windows: a 60-day-hold PEAD position straddles fold boundaries, so standard CV trains on the future of an open position. Must use purged + embargoed CV (Lopez de Prado 2018). (8) Vendor-backtest optimism: RavenPack/data-vendor studies (IR ~4, >20%/yr) are self-published, in-sample, gross, and survivorship/selection-tuned. Treat all vendor alpha claims as upper bounds to be independently re-derived out-of-sample. (9) Merger-arb tail risk mispriced as 'arbitrage': the payoff is a short uncovered index put (Mitchell-Pulvino) — calm-market carry with crash blow-ups when deals break en masse. Modeling it as steady arbitrage understates left-tail and correlation-to-crashes. (10) Post-publication decay denial: McLean-Pontiff — ~58% of in-sample return gone post-publication. Every strategy in this family is published; pricing it at its paper Sharpe is a known error. Haircut hard and expect continued decay as LLM news-readers crowd the easy signals.

**Honest validation:** (1) Strict temporal out-of-sample on POST-cutoff data (the anti-Profit-Mirage core): for any LLM/transcript signal, only evaluate on text dated after the model's pretraining cutoff (StockBench's design: Mar-Jun 2025, forward-out-of-sample). If alpha exists ONLY before the cutoff and vanishes after, it was memorization. (2) Run the Lookahead-Propensity (LAP) test (Gao-Jiang-Yan 2025): estimate the probability each prompt was in training via pretraining-data-detection; a positive LAP-vs-accuracy correlation quantifies and rejects the signal as recall, not reasoning. (3) Counterfactual perturbation (FactFin / Profit Mirage): systematically swap the numbers, earnings outcome, and sentiment in the input text and REQUIRE the prediction to move — measure Prediction-Consistency (want LOW), Input-Dependency-Score (want HIGH), Memory-Score (want LOW). A signal that ignores perturbed inputs is leaking. (4) Purged + embargoed K-fold CV (Lopez de Prado 2018) so overlapping event-window labels don't leak future into training; combinatorially-symmetric CV to estimate the Probability of Backtest Overfitting (PBO). (5) Deflated Sharpe Ratio (Bailey-Lopez de Prado): record the NUMBER of trials/variants tested and deflate the reported Sharpe for trials + skew + kurtosis + sample length. Report DSR, not raw Sharpe. (6) Net-of-cost backtest in the names that carry the signal: model spread, market impact (size-scaled), and borrow/short-cost specifically in the small/illiquid universe where the drift lives — not in a clean large-cap universe where it doesn't. (7) Dictionary baseline control: before claiming an LLM/embedding signal, show it beats the Loughran-McDonald lexicon signal out-of-sample. If a frozen 6-list dictionary captures most of it, the expensive model is overfit theater. (8) Decay/crowding stress test: split the sample pre/post publication date of the underlying anomaly and pre/post ~2015 (rise of quant news-readers); McLean-Pontiff predicts ~50-58% haircut. Size to the decayed, not the in-sample, estimate. (9) Sub-sample and regime robustness: separately measure the noise-reversal leg vs fundamental-drift leg, and test in down markets explicitly (StockBench: ALL LLM agents failed in downturns; merger-arb breaks in crashes). A signal that only works in calm up-markets is a hidden short-vol position.

**Data:** "Point-in-time fundamentals & analyst estimates: Compustat/IBES with as-reported, lag-correct EPS and consensus snapshots taken BEFORE each announcement (no restated/look-ahead figures) — for SUE. CRSP/exchange prices with delisting returns and survivorship-bias-free universe (small-caps where signal lives are the ones that delist). Full-text filings: SEC EDGAR 10-K/10-Q/8-K (Item 2.02 press releases) with filing TIMESTAMPS for event-time alignment — for text-PEAD and Lazy Prices. Earnings-call transcripts (e.g. Refinitiv/AlphaSense/Seeking Alpha) for transcript NLP. News with article-level timestamps, relevance, and novelty: RavenPack/Dow Jones/Bloomberg or a raw wire archive (timestamps must be the TAPE time, not the rewrite time — a classic leakage source). Loughran-McDonald master dictionary (free, sraf.nd.edu) as baseline. For LLM signals: documented model pretraining cutoff dates and ideally a pretraining-data-detection harness (for LAP). Corporate-actions/M&A deal database (SDC Platinum / Bloomberg deal terms, offer price, status history, close/break dates) for merger-arb, plus borrow-cost/short-availability data. Critical cross-cutting requirement: everything must be POINT-IN-TIME and event-time aligned — the dominant failure mode in this family is subtle look-ahead via restated data, rewrite-timestamped news, or post-cutoff LLM knowledge."

**Fits our stack:** "R5 (alpha) is the home: these are the event/sentiment alpha generators. But the LOAD-BEARING contribution is to R3 (evidence gate): the Profit Mirage / Lookahead-Propensity / Deflated-Sharpe / McLean-Pontiff machinery IS the anti-Profit-Mirage acceptance test that R3 should enforce on EVERY signal, not just NLP ones — it operationalizes the lab's 'honest about proven vs speculated' ethos and the Profit-Mirage/StockBench skepticism the operator named. R4 (data): defines the point-in-time, event-time, post-cutoff data contracts (EDGAR timestamps, IBES snapshots, news tape-time, model-cutoff metadata) the data layer must guarantee. Risk: merger-arb-as-short-put and 'fails in downturns' (StockBench) feed the left-tail/regime module. Execution: the small-cap/illiquid capacity constraint and announcement-jump-vs-drift distinction set realistic cost/slippage and entry-lag assumptions. Maps onto the IG-12 prompts that cover (a) alpha-signal generation and (b) backtest-honesty / overfitting-defense — it deepens the evidence-gate prompt most, by supplying concrete, citable acceptance tests (LAP test, FactFin counterfactuals, purged-CV, DSR, post-cutoff OOS) rather than generic 'avoid overfitting' advice."

**Go deeper:** • Read the PEAD.txt full paper (Zhao et al., JFQA 2023 / Philadelphia Fed WP 21-07) directly — the philadelphiafed PDF 403'd; get it via the Cambridge/JFQA version or SSRN to extract the exact text-surprise ML construction and its orthogonality-to-SUE numbers. • Lopez de Prado, 'Advances in Financial Machine Learning' (2018) chapters on triple-barrier labeling, meta-labeling, purged/embargoed CV, and fractional differentiation — the implementation core for event-ML without leakage. Pair with his 'The 10 Reasons Most ML Funds Fail.' • Engelberg-Reed-Ringgenberg and the short-interest / limits-to-arbitrage literature; Mendenhall 2004 'Arbitrage Risk and PEAD' and Chordia-Subrahmanyam-Tong 2014 for the precise capacity/decay mechanics. • Ke-Kelly-Xiu (2019) 'Predicting Returns with Text Data' (SESTM) — a leakage-aware supervised-sentiment method that out-of-samples cleanly; the rigorous alternative to dictionary tone and to black-box LLMs. • DatedGPT / time-aware pretraining (arXiv 2603.11838) and 'Evaluating LLMs in Finance Requires Explicit Bias Consideration' (arXiv 2602.14233) and MemGuard-Alpha (membership-inference filtering of contaminated signals) — the 2026 frontier of leakage-controlled LLM finance. • RavenPack/Bloomberg event-taxonomy methodology papers and the Heston-Sinha 'News vs Sentiment: Predicting Stock Returns from News Stories' (2017) for the horizon structure (intraday vs weekly drift) of news signals — and to calibrate how much vendor IRs shrink out-of-sample. • Frazzini-Israel-Moskowitz 'Trading Costs' and AQR's capacity/implementation work — to convert gross academic Sharpes into net, capacity-aware expectations for the small-cap names where these signals actually live. • Earnings-call paralinguistics / vocal-tone literature (Mayew-Venkatachalam 'The Power of Voice') and Q&A-evasion NLP — soft-information event signals beyond text that are less crowded. • Hansen-McMahon and the macro text-sentiment thread (central-bank communication, FOMC) for the event-sentiment family applied to rates/FX, where crowding differs from equities.

---
## Execution, Transaction Costs & Capacity — the gross-to-net bridge (Almgren-Chriss optimal execution, square-root impact law, implementation shortfall, TCA, slippage/spread/borrow modeling, capacity & alpha decay with AUM)

**Thesis.** This is not an alpha family — it is the family that determines whether every OTHER alpha family survives contact with reality. The economic edge here is not a market inefficiency to harvest; it is a *correctness constraint*: gross alpha is a coordinate-free quantity, but net alpha is the only thing that compounds in the bank account, and the wedge between them is a deterministic, model-able function of trade size, speed, liquidity, and AUM. The reason this 'edge' (cost discipline) persists and never crowds out is structural: trading is physically a liquidity-demanding act, and demanding liquidity costs money — you pay the spread, you move the price against yourself (impact), you pay borrow to short, you pay commissions/financing. Almgren-Chriss formalize the irreducible tension: trade fast and pay impact, or trade slow and eat volatility risk (the efficient frontier of execution). The square-root impact law (impact ∝ √(Q/ADV)) is one of the most robust empirical regularities in all of finance — universal across asset classes, eras, and venues — and its concavity is *why capacity is finite but not zero*: doubling size only increases per-share cost by √2, but total cost still grows faster than linearly, so for every strategy there exists an AUM at which marginal impact cost equals marginal gross alpha and net P&L is maximized. The brutal corollary, documented by Novy-Marx & Velikov and McLean-Pontiff: a large fraction of published 'anomalies' are gross-alpha artifacts that go to zero net-of-cost, and most lose >50% of their edge post-publication as capital crowds in. A fund that does not model these costs honestly is not running a strategy; it is running a Profit Mirage.

**Concrete strategies:**
- **Almgren-Chriss IS-targeting execution schedule (the workhorse)**
  - *Signal:* Liquidate X shares over horizon T in N slices. Price: S_k = S_{k-1} + σ·τ^(1/2)·ξ_k − τ·g(n_k/τ). Temporary impact per slice h(n_k/τ)=ε·sgn(n_k)+(η/τ)·n_k; permanent g(v)=γ·v. Minimize U(x)=E(x)+λV(x) where E(x)=½γX²+ε·Σ|n_k|+(η̃/τ)·Σn_k², V(x)=σ²·Σ τ·x_k², η̃=η−½γτ. Closed-form optimal holdings: x_j = [sinh(κ(T−t_j))/sinh(κT)]·X, with κ²=λσ²/η̃ (so κ≈√(λσ²/η) for small τ). Trade list n_j = [2·sinh(½κτ)/sinh(κT)]·cosh(κ(T−t_{j-½}))·X. Trade HALF-LIFE θ=1/κ is the intrinsic execution timescale, independent of imposed T. High risk-aversion λ → large κ → front-loaded (sell fast, eat impact to cut variance); λ=0 → naive linear TWAP (n_k=X/N).
  - *Params:* λ (risk aversion, e.g. 1e-6 to 2e-6 per the paper's Fig.1), σ (per-period vol), η (temporary impact slope), γ (permanent impact slope), ε (fixed cost ≈ half-spread + fees), T (horizon), N (slices). κ=√(λσ²/η). Rule of thumb: if T≫θ trade looks min-variance/spread-out; if T≪θ trade is impact-dominated/front-loaded.  ·  *Assets:* Equities (native), futures, FX, liquid crypto. Any asset with a definable ADV and a microstructure spread. Multi-asset extension (Appendix A) uses the covariance matrix — correlated baskets liquidate jointly.
  - *Evidence (honest):* The MODEL is a normative optimization, not an alpha claim — no Sharpe attaches. Its value is COST REDUCTION vs naive execution; ITG/Citigroup-style TCA studies show IS-targeting beats TWAP/VWAP on impact-dominated orders. HONEST CAVEAT: the closed form assumes LINEAR impact, which Almgren himself (2005) showed is empirically wrong (true exponent ~0.6 temporary, √ for metaorders). So the sinh trajectory is a first-order approximation; for large orders use nonlinear impact and lose the closed form (numerical solve).
  - *Decay/crowding:* Does not decay — it is execution machinery, not alpha. But the impact PARAMETERS drift with market structure (η, γ in 2005 ≠ 2026 post-HFT). Must be re-calibrated on your own fills continuously.
- **Square-root impact cost model (the backtest's honest cost engine)**
  - *Signal:* For a parent order of Q shares (one side), expected impact cost in price terms: I(Q) = Y·σ_daily·√(Q/ADV), where Y≈0.5–1.0 (use ~0.8 as a prior; mean ⟨c⟩≈0.842 in TSE survey), σ_daily = daily return vol, ADV = average daily volume. Per-dollar cost ≈ I(Q)/2 for the metaorder on average (impact accrues progressively). Add half-spread (s/2) per share for crossing, plus commission c_bps. Total per-trade cost(bps) ≈ (s/2)_bps + commission_bps + 1e4·Y·σ_daily·√(participation_fraction) where participation_fraction = Q/ADV. For the Almgren-2005 refinement substitute the 3/5 law: temporary ≈ η·σ·(Q/(ADV·T))^0.6, permanent ≈ γ·σ·(Q/ADV)·(Θ/ADV)^0.25.
  - *Params:* Y≈0.8 (impact prefactor, RE-FIT on your fills), σ_daily, ADV (trailing 20–60d median, NOT mean — avoid spikes), half-spread s (time-of-day & vol dependent), commission (asset/venue specific, ~0.5–2 bps US equities, higher emerging/crypto), participation cap (keep Q/ADV per day below ~5–10% or impact estimate is unreliable). For 0.6-law: η≈0.142, γ≈0.314 as PRIORS only.  ·  *Assets:* Universal — equities, futures, FX, options (√ law shown to hold for options too), crypto. The √ law is the single most asset-class-robust regularity here.
  - *Evidence (honest):* The √ law is empirically among the most validated relationships in finance: exponent 0.5±<0.01 across stocks/eras/venues (Bouchaud et al., TSE complete survey 2024). HONEST CAVEAT: holds for the BULK of metaorder sizes but (a) crosses over to LINEAR for very small orders (Kyle regime) and to faster-than-√ at very high participation (>~10% ADV), and (b) the prefactor Y is the fragile part — varies 0.5–1.0 by market and is what crowding inflates (co-impact). A backtest using Y too low manufactures phantom alpha.
  - *Decay/crowding:* The √ FORM is stable; the PREFACTOR Y rises with crowding (co-impact: you pay for correlated flow). In a crowded factor your effective Q is the aggregate crowd's Q, so realized impact >> your-own-Q estimate. This is the dominant hidden capacity tax.
- **Strategy capacity / maximum-AUM estimation (where alpha = cost)**
  - *Signal:* Capacity is the AUM at which net P&L is maximized: gross alpha grows ~linearly with capital deployed, but impact cost grows super-linearly (because cost-per-share ∝ √(Q/ADV) ∝ √AUM, so total impact cost ∝ AUM^1.5). Net P&L(A) = α·A·turnover − Σ_names [Y·σ·√(w·A·turnover/ADV)]·(w·A·turnover). Maximize over A: optimal capacity A* scales like A* ∝ (α/Y)²·(aggregate ADV·liquidity). For the n=1.5 impact convention the optimum P&L P* = M̃³/(3Q²) and capacity I* = M̃²/(T·Q²) (M̃=alpha budget, T=turnover, Q=impact coef) per the alpha-streams literature. Practical recipe: build a per-name capacity map — for each holding, max tradable = (target participation, e.g. 5% ADV) × ADV × horizon; aggregate to portfolio AUM under weight constraints. Net alpha at AUM A = gross_alpha − cost_drag(A); plot the curve, A* is the peak, and you run at ~50–70% of A* for safety margin.
  - *Params:* α (gross alpha, bps/turnover), annual turnover T, per-name ADV distribution, σ, impact prefactor Y, max participation cap (5–10% ADV/day), holding weights w_i, rebalance frequency. Decay half-life of the alpha (faster decay → forced higher turnover → lower capacity).  ·  *Assets:* All. Capacity is most binding in small/mid-cap equities, less liquid futures, single-name credit; least binding in large-cap index futures, major FX, on-the-run treasuries.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Real-world capacity caps are publicly observable: Helikon hit capacity at ~$8B, Kintbury introduced constraints near ~$3B (HedgeCo 2026). Renaissance Medallion famously capped at ~$10B and returns capital. HONEST CAVEAT: capacity estimates are HIGHLY sensitive to the impact prefactor Y and to whether you model co-impact/crowding — a 2x error in Y is a 4x error in A*. Most blow-ups come from over-estimating capacity by ignoring crowding and using mean (not median) ADV.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Capacity itself shrinks as a strategy crowds: more correlated capital → higher effective Y → lower A* for everyone. Capacity is a shared, depleting commons. McLean-Pontiff: ~58% return decay post-publication is largely this mechanism.
- **Implementation Shortfall TCA + buy/hold-spread cost mitigation**
  - *Signal:* Measure every strategy's true cost as IS = Σ_filled (P_exec − P_decision)·shares + Σ_unfilled (P_cancel − P_decision)·shares + commissions, all signed by trade direction, benchmarked to the DECISION price (not VWAP). Decompose: IS = delay cost (P_arrival−P_decision) + execution/impact cost (P_exec−P_arrival) + opportunity cost (unfilled alpha) + explicit fees. MITIGATION (Novy-Marx-Velikov): apply a BUY/HOLD SPREAD / banding — enter only names in the extreme signal band, but do NOT exit until the signal crosses a WIDER exit band; keep holding 'stale' names you wouldn't newly buy. This decouples entry turnover from exit turnover and roughly halves round-trip cost. Combine with: trade-size capping at X% ADV, scheduling around liquidity (avoid open/close unless capturing close-auction liquidity), and netting offsetting orders across sub-strategies before sending to market.
  - *Params:* Entry band (e.g. top/bottom decile), exit band (e.g. top/bottom tercile or quartile — wider), participation cap, one-sided monthly turnover (KEEP <~50% to survive costs per N-M-V), commission schedule, half-spread by liquidity bucket.  ·  *Assets:* Systematic equities (native), extends to any rebalanced factor portfolio (futures, ETFs).
  - *Evidence (honest):* Novy-Marx & Velikov (RFS 2016): low-turnover anomalies (<~50% one-sided monthly turnover) retain significant NET spreads; high-turnover ones (short-term reversal) are consumed by costs. Buy/hold spread is the single most effective simple mitigation. HONEST CAVEAT: their cost estimates are from effective-spread models on historical TAQ data; for SMALL/illiquid names true costs may exceed their estimates, and for a real fund the achievable mitigation depends on infrastructure (smart order routing, internalization).
  - *Decay/crowding:* The mitigation TECHNIQUE doesn't decay, but the underlying anomalies it's applied to do (post-publication crowding). Banding extends a strategy's net-profitable life by lowering the cost hurdle the (decaying) gross alpha must clear.

**Canonical sources:**
- *Optimal Execution of Portfolio Transactions* — Robert Almgren & Neil Chriss (2000, paper): THE foundational optimal-execution paper. Defines temporary impact h(v)=ε·sgn(n)+(η/τ)·n and permanent impact g(v)=γ·v, the implementation-shortfall mean-variance objective min E(x)+λV(x), and derives the closed-form sinh trajectory x_j=sinh(κ(T-t_j))/sinh(κT)·X with κ²=λσ²/η̃ and trade half-life θ=1/κ. Every modern execution algo (TWAP/VWAP/IS-targeting) descends from this. Note it ASSUMES linear impact, which the same author later refutes empirically. — https://www.smallake.kr/wp-content/uploads/2016/03/optliq.pdf
- *Direct Estimation of Equity Market Impact* — Robert Almgren, Chee Thum, Emmanuel Hauptmann, Hong Li (2005, paper): The empirical companion that calibrated impact on ~700k Citigroup orders (Dec 2001–Jun 2003). REJECTS the linear assumption of the 2000 paper: temporary impact follows a 3/5 (=0.6) power law on participation rate, not linear and not exactly √. Gives the usable engineering form: permanent ≈ γ·σ·(X/V)·(Θ/V)^(1/4) with γ≈0.314, temporary ≈ η·σ·|X/(V·T)|^(3/5) with η≈0.142. This is the model you actually plug into a backtest. Caveat: pre-2005 market structure (decimalization just done, pre-HFT-saturation) — constants have drifted; treat the functional form as durable, the constants as needing re-fit. — https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/Direct-Estimation-of-Equity-Market-Impact-Almgren-Thum/00777edc168f26633de9f5b9ff4c4f74bd9790e3
- *The Square-Root Law of Market Impact (and Trades, Quotes & Prices, Ch.12)* — Jean-Philippe Bouchaud, Julius Bonart, Jonathan Donier, Martin Gould (2018, book): Definitive synthesis of the square-root impact law: I(Q) = Y·σ·√(Q/V), with exponent δ stubbornly 0.5 (error bars <0.01) and prefactor Y of order unity (~0.5–1.0, mean ⟨c⟩≈0.842 in TSE survey). Crucially shows impact is ~independent of execution schedule and time — it depends on TOTAL volume Q, not how you slice it — and argues the origin is MECHANICAL (latent-liquidity / locally-linear order book), not informational, which is why it's universal. Contrasts directly with Kyle (1985) linear λ. This is the impact model for capacity analysis. — https://bouchaud.substack.com/p/the-square-root-law-of-market-impact
- *Continuous Auctions and Insider Trading* — Albert S. Kyle (1985, paper): Origin of 'Kyle's lambda' — the linear price-impact coefficient λ = σ_v/σ_u (informed-trade volatility over noise-trade volatility), derived from an informational equilibrium where price impact reveals private information. λ is still the standard intraday liquidity metric (regress Δprice on signed order flow). The tension between Kyle-linear (small orders, informational) and square-root (large metaorders, mechanical) is the central unsolved-ish debate in impact modeling. Linear holds in the small; √ holds in the large. — https://www.jstor.org/stable/1913210
- *The Implementation Shortfall: Paper versus Reality* — André Perold (1988, paper): Coined 'implementation shortfall' = paper-portfolio return minus real-portfolio return = the single number capturing ALL leakage between decision and fill. Decomposes into explicit costs (commissions/fees/taxes), execution/impact cost, delay cost (drift while waiting), and opportunity cost (alpha on shares never filled). The opportunity-cost term is the genius: it penalizes UNFILLED orders, which naive VWAP benchmarks ignore. This is the honest accounting frame — the backtest must measure shortfall vs the decision price, not vs some achievable VWAP. — https://jpm.pm-research.com/content/14/3/4
- *A Taxonomy of Anomalies and Their Trading Costs* — Robert Novy-Marx & Mihail Velikov (2016, paper): The canonical net-of-cost reality check. Studies ~23 anomalies after realistic trading costs; finds low-turnover anomalies (one-sided turnover <~50%/month) survive net, while high-turnover ones (short-term reversal, etc.) are largely consumed by costs. Establishes that the single most effective cost-mitigation is the buy/hold spread (banding: trade into the extreme decile, but keep holding until a wider exit band, rather than rebalancing on every signal flip). This is THE anti-Profit-Mirage paper for systematic equity strategies. — https://rnm.simon.rochester.edu/research/ToAatTC.pdf
- *Does Academic Research Destroy Stock Return Predictability?* — R. David McLean & Jeffrey Pontiff (2016, paper): Out-of-sample + post-publication decay quantified: anomaly returns fall ~26% out-of-sample (data-mining component) and a further ~32% post-publication (crowding/arbitrage component) — total ~58% decay. This is the empirical backbone of 'alpha decays with crowding,' and the reason a backtested Sharpe must be discounted before it's believed. Directly relevant to capacity: publication = free capacity-eroding capital. — https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/jofi.12365
- *Co-impact: Crowding Effects in Institutional Trading Activity* — Bucci, Mastromatteo, Eisler, Lillo, Bouchaud, Lehalle (2018, paper): Shows that when multiple institutions trade the SAME direction simultaneously, impact is driven by aggregate (co-)order-flow, and the square-root law holds at the aggregate level — i.e. you pay for OTHERS' correlated trading, not just your own. Quantifies the crowding tax on impact. Critical for honest capacity estimation in a crowded factor: your effective Q is the crowd's Q. — https://arxiv.org/pdf/1804.09565
- *Active Portfolio Management (Ch. on transaction costs & the Fundamental Law)* — Richard Grinold & Ronald Kahn (2000, book): Fundamental Law of Active Management (IR = IC·√Breadth) plus the formal treatment of how transaction costs and signal decay (half-life) interact to bound turnover and capacity. The dynamic-portfolio-management framework derives optimal turnover as a function of alpha half-life and cost — the bridge from signal decay to net IR. The sinh execution trajectory of Almgren-Chriss was constructed earlier here. — https://joim.com/wp-content/uploads/emember/downloads/richard_grinold.pdf
- *Trading Costs (and related quant-blog treatments of backtest cost modeling)* — Hudson & Thames / BSIC / QuantStart practitioner literature (2020, blog): Practitioner-grade implementations of the cost stack a backtest must include: fixed commission, proportional half-spread, and a square-root/linear impact term tied to participation rate. The operative practitioner heuristic: any order >1% of ADV should expect ~1–2% slippage; commissions are the easy/small part, impact and spread are the dangerous part. Useful as the code-level reference, not theory. — https://github.com/hudson-and-thames/backtest_tutorial/blob/main/Intro_Transaction_Costs.ipynb

**Pitfalls:** (1) PHANTOM ALPHA FROM ZERO/LOW COSTS: the single most common backtest lie. A strategy with 200%+ annual turnover can show Sharpe 2.0 gross and Sharpe 0.0 net. If the backtest doesn't subtract spread + commission + impact, it is not a strategy, it is a Profit Mirage. Rule: NEVER report a gross Sharpe without the net Sharpe beside it. (2) USING MEAN ADV INSTEAD OF MEDIAN / TRAILING ADV: ADV spikes (earnings, index events) inflate apparent liquidity; you size to the spike, then can't get out on a normal day. Use trailing median ADV and cap participation. (3) IGNORING THE IMPACT PREFACTOR'S FRAGILITY: Y (square-root prefactor) varies 0.5–1.0 and is exactly what crowding inflates. A backtest with Y=0.3 manufactures capacity that doesn't exist. Worse: own-Q impact ignores CO-IMPACT — in a crowded factor you pay for the whole crowd's correlated flow, so realized impact can be 2–5x your single-name estimate. (4) LINEAR-IMPACT OVER-OPTIMISM AT SIZE: Almgren-Chriss closed form assumes linear impact; Almgren-2005 shows true temporary exponent ~0.6 and metaorders follow √. Linear UNDER-states cost for large orders. The clean closed-form sinh trajectory is seductive but wrong in the tails where it matters most. (5) BENCHMARK GAMING IN TCA: measuring execution vs interval-VWAP makes a slow, opportunity-cost-bleeding execution look 'good' because it ignores the alpha lost on delay and unfilled shares. Perold's IS vs the DECISION price is the honest benchmark; VWAP flatters. (6) OVERFITTING THE COST MODEL ITSELF: tuning Y, η, γ until the backtest looks good is double data-snooping. Calibrate cost params on an INDEPENDENT dataset (your own fills, or a TCA vendor) and freeze them before testing the alpha. (7) POST-PUBLICATION / OUT-OF-SAMPLE DECAY UNMODELED: McLean-Pontiff ~58% total decay. A backtested gross Sharpe on a known factor should be haircut heavily (≥50%) before it's believed, because the world has already crowded in. (8) SHORT-SIDE COST BLINDNESS: borrow fees (50–200+ bps/yr, far higher for hard-to-borrow), rebate dynamics, recall risk, and short-squeeze tail risk are routinely omitted. A long/short backtest that doesn't subtract realistic borrow on the short book overstates net alpha — and the high-borrow names are often exactly the ones the short signal wants. (9) CAPACITY ESTIMATE SENSITIVITY: A* ∝ (1/Y)², so a 2x error in the impact coefficient is a 4x error in capacity. Most fund blow-ups in this dimension come from over-estimating capacity, scaling AUM, and discovering impact costs were 2–3x assumed. (10) SURVIVORSHIP & LOOK-AHEAD IN ADV/SPREAD DATA: using current spreads/ADV for historical periods (markets were far less liquid pre-2010, decimalization 2001) flatters historical net returns. Costs must be period-appropriate.

**Honest validation:** (1) DUAL-LEDGER REPORTING (the core anti-Profit-Mirage test): every backtest result MUST report gross AND net side by side, with the cost stack itemized (commission, half-spread, impact). If net Sharpe collapses, the strategy was a cost illusion. This is non-negotiable. (2) COST-MODEL STRESS / Y-SWEEP: re-run net P&L across impact prefactor Y ∈ {0.5, 0.8, 1.0, 1.5} and spread assumptions ±50%. If the strategy is only profitable at the optimistic end, it is fragile. The strategy must survive the PESSIMISTIC cost assumption to be deployable. (3) TURNOVER-HURDLE TEST (Novy-Marx-Velikov frame): compute one-sided monthly turnover; if >~50%, the bar for gross alpha is very high and the strategy likely dies net unless banded. Test the buy/hold-spread variant and confirm it materially improves net Sharpe. (4) CAPACITY CURVE: plot net P&L vs AUM (gross α·A minus impact cost ∝ A^1.5). Identify A* (peak) and confirm intended AUM is comfortably below it (run at 50–70% of A*). If the curve peaks below intended AUM, the strategy cannot hold the target capital — reject or downsize. (5) PARTICIPATION-RATE SANITY: for each name, check Q/ADV per day stays under the cap (~5–10%). Any holding requiring >10% ADV/day to enter or exit has UNRELIABLE cost estimates and tail liquidation risk — flag it. (6) OUT-OF-SAMPLE + POST-PUBLICATION HAIRCUT: discount any in-sample gross Sharpe by the McLean-Pontiff empirical decay (~26% OOS, ~58% if it's a published factor) BEFORE believing it. Then subtract costs. Survives both? Maybe real. (7) CALIBRATE COSTS ON INDEPENDENT FILLS: fit Y, η, γ, and realized spread on your OWN execution data (or a TCA vendor), frozen and independent of the alpha backtest. Compare backtest-assumed cost vs realized IS post-deployment — the gap is your model error. (8) PAPER-VS-REALITY (Perold) RECONCILIATION POST-LAUNCH: once live, compute actual implementation shortfall vs the backtest's decision prices. If live IS systematically exceeds modeled cost, the cost model is too optimistic and capacity/expected-return must be revised down immediately. (9) BORROW/FINANCING REALISM ON SHORTS: pull actual or proxied borrow fees (e.g. from a securities-lending feed) for the short book; subtract 50–200+ bps where applicable, exclude or penalize hard-to-borrow names, and add a short-squeeze tail scenario. Re-check net Sharpe of the short leg in isolation.

**Data:** "Per-symbol daily: trailing ADV (median, 20–60d), daily return volatility σ, bid-ask spread (ideally intraday/time-of-day resolved; minimum daily effective spread from TAQ-style data or a vendor), commission/fee schedule per venue/asset class. For impact calibration: your own historical fills (parent + child orders, arrival/decision/exec prices) OR a TCA vendor dataset OR the Almgren-2005 priors (Y≈0.8, η≈0.142, γ≈0.314) as a STARTING point only. For shorts: securities-lending borrow fees / rebate rates and hard-to-borrow flags (S3, IBKR, or broker feed). For capacity: full universe ADV distribution + intended weights. For honest historical backtests: PERIOD-APPROPRIATE spreads/ADV (pre-2010 liquidity ≠ today; decimalization 2001 break). Intraday tick/quote data needed only if doing intraday execution research; daily bars + spread + ADV suffice for net-of-cost strategy validation. Critically, costs must be applied at the CHILD-order/participation-rate level, not as a flat bps haircut, or large-order impact is understated."

**Fits our stack:** "This is the R3 EVIDENCE GATE made quantitative — it is the family that decides whether any R5 alpha candidate is real net-of-cost or a Profit Mirage. Concretely: (1) R3 evidence gate — institute the DUAL-LEDGER rule: no alpha advances past R3 without gross-AND-net Sharpe and an itemized cost stack; the Y-sweep and turnover-hurdle become hard gates. (2) EXECUTION layer — Almgren-Chriss sinh schedule + square-root impact model are the execution engine spec; the impact params are the calibration target. (3) RISK layer — capacity curve (A*) is a position-sizing/AUM constraint; participation caps are pre-trade risk checks. (4) PORTFOLIO layer — buy/hold-spread banding is a turnover-reduction primitive that belongs in the optimizer's cost term (optimize net, not gross). (5) R4 data — defines the mandatory data feeds (ADV, spread, σ, borrow). It DEEPENS the IG-12 'transaction cost / realistic backtest' and 'capacity & decay' prompts: replaces the retail 'add 5 bps slippage' hand-wave with participation-rate-dependent √-impact costing, the Perold IS accounting frame, and an explicit AUM capacity ceiling. It is the load-bearing honesty constraint for the whole fund — the bridge from coordinate-free gross alpha to the only number that compounds: net."

**Go deeper:** • Almgren, Thum, Hauptmann, Li (2005) 'Direct Estimation of Equity Market Impact', Risk 18(7) — read the FULL paper for the exact 0.6-power functional form and the daily-vol/turnover scaling; re-fit the constants on post-2015 data since η,γ have drifted with HFT. • Bouchaud, Bonart, Donier, Gould (2018) 'Trades, Quotes and Prices: Financial Markets Under the Microscope' (Cambridge) — Ch.11–12 on impact and metaorders; the definitive microstructure text. Pair with the 'complete survey of the Tokyo Stock Exchange' (arXiv 2411.13965) for the strict-universality evidence and the ⟨c⟩≈0.842 prefactor. • Novy-Marx & Velikov (2016) 'A Taxonomy of Anomalies and Their Trading Costs' (RFS 29:1) FULL paper at rnm.simon.rochester.edu — the net-of-cost survival table by anomaly; and Velikov's follow-up work + the open-source 'Anomalies' cost-aware factor library. • Kyle (1985) 'Continuous Auctions and Insider Trading' (Econometrica) + Obizhaeva & Wang 'Optimal Trading Strategy and Supply/Demand Dynamics' (resilient order book) + Gatheral (2010) 'No-Dynamic-Arbitrage and Market Impact' — for the impact-decay/transient-impact propagator models (impact is not instantaneous; it decays — Gatheral's no-arbitrage constraint links permanent and transient impact). • Bucci et al. (2018) 'Co-impact: Crowding Effects in Institutional Trading' (arXiv 1804.09565) + 'Impact is not just volatility' (arXiv 1905.04569) — for honest crowding-adjusted capacity; this is where own-Q estimates go wrong. • McLean & Pontiff (2016) 'Does Academic Research Destroy Stock Return Predictability?' (J. Finance) — the OOS + post-publication decay numbers; haircut priors for any known factor. • Grinold & Kahn 'Active Portfolio Management' (2000) Ch.16 (transaction costs) + Grinold's 'Dynamic Model of Portfolio Management' (JOIM) — optimal turnover as a function of alpha half-life and cost; the signal-decay-to-capacity bridge. • Practitioner code: Hudson & Thames backtest_tutorial (Intro_Transaction_Costs.ipynb), QuantRocket's borrow-fee-as-alpha studies, and S3 Partners borrow-fee data methodology — for the short-side cost stack (borrow 50–200+ bps, hard-to-borrow flags). • Gârleanu & Pedersen (2013) 'Dynamic Trading with Predictable Returns and Transaction Costs' (J. Finance) — the closed-form dynamic portfolio that trades partway toward a moving 'aim portfolio,' optimally balancing alpha decay against quadratic costs; this is the modern successor to Almgren-Chriss for PORTFOLIO (not single-name) execution and is what a serious fund actually uses to fuse signal + cost. • Frazzini, Israel, Moskowitz (AQR) 'Trading Costs of Asset Pricing Anomalies' / 'Trading Costs' — AQR's real-money estimates from $1T+ of live trades showing realized costs are MUCH lower than academic effective-spread models for large patient traders; the crucial counterpoint to Novy-Marx-Velikov (capacity is larger than naive TAQ models imply IF you trade patiently). Read both sides — the truth on capacity sits between the pessimistic-TAQ and the optimistic-AQR estimates.

---
## momentum-trend (time-series momentum / trend-following / managed futures + cross-sectional equity momentum)

**Thesis.** Two related but distinct edges. (1) TREND / TIME-SERIES MOMENTUM (TSM): an asset's own past return predicts its future return at 1-12 month horizons, then partially reverses at 2-5 years. The persistent economic driver is behavioral — under-reaction to news (anchoring, slow information diffusion, disposition effect) followed by delayed over-reaction/herding — plus a structural flow argument: trend-followers (speculators) take the other side of hedgers who pay a premium to offload risk (Moskowitz-Ooi-Pedersen show speculators are positioned WITH the trend, hedgers against). It persists because it is a behavioral bias that requires conviction to arbitrage (you must hold losing positions through whipsaws) and because the marginal hedger keeps paying. (2) CROSS-SECTIONAL MOMENTUM (XS): relative winners beat relative losers (Jegadeesh-Titman). Same under-reaction story but RELATIVE, so it is dollar-neutral and loads on a crash risk: it implicitly shorts beaten-down high-beta names, so it behaves like a written call option in market rebounds and crashes catastrophically off market bottoms (1932, 2009). WHY IT DECAYS: XS equity momentum is the most-published, most-crowded factor on earth; post-2000 decimalization cut the transaction-cost moat, hedge-fund AUM 10x'd, and Ehsani-Linnainmaa (2022) showed it is not even a distinct factor — it is factor-momentum timing other factors. TSM/trend has decayed far less (it lives in deep, cheap futures markets with high capacity) but its realized Sharpe fell from ~1.0+ in backtests to a hard 2010s stretch as AUM and correlation across CTAs rose. The honest punchline: trend's REAL edge after costs is a Sharpe of ~0.3-0.5 with crisis-alpha convexity — valuable as a diversifier, not as a standalone money-printer.

**Concrete strategies:**
- **Time-Series Momentum (TSM / MOP12) — the canonical trend signal**
  - *Signal:* For each instrument, signal = sign(past-12-month excess return). Go long if 12m excess return > 0, short if < 0, 1-month holding period, rebalance monthly. Position size each instrument inversely to its ex-ante volatility so every position targets the same risk: weight_i = (vol_target / sigma_i) * sign(r_i,t-12,t). MOP regression spec (their Fig.1): r_st/sigma_{s,t-1} = a + b_h * sign(r_{s,t-h}) + e ; b_h positive and significant for lags h=1..12, negative for h=13..48 (the reversal).
  - *Params:* Lookback: 12 months (primary). Holding: 1 month. Vol target per instrument: 40% annualized in the paper (size = 40%/sigma_i; the 40% is 'inconsequential', just a scaling convention). sigma_i = exponentially-weighted std of daily returns with center-of-mass = 60 days (delta chosen so sum (1-delta)*delta^i weights have COM 60 days; ~equivalent to ~0.94 daily decay). Diversified portfolio realized ~12% annual vol.  ·  *Assets:* 58 liquid futures/forwards: country equity indices, FX forwards, commodity futures, sovereign-bond futures. The deeper, more diversifying the cross-section, the higher the portfolio Sharpe (single-instrument Sharpes ~0.2-0.4; diversified ~1+).
  - *Evidence (honest):* IN-SAMPLE / GROSS in the original paper: diversified vol-scaled TSM gross Sharpe >1 (annualized), 1985-2009, large alpha vs Fama-French + carry, t-stats clustered by month. Per-instrument 12m TSM annualized Sharpe ~1.1 average. CRITICAL CAVEAT: Kim-Tse-Wald (2016) show much of this is the vol-scaling overlay, not the signal. Net-of-cost, realistic diversified trend Sharpe is ~0.4 (Hurst-Ooi-Pedersen, out-of-sample 1880-2013, after 2/20 + costs).
  - *Decay/crowding:* Trend has decayed LESS than XS equity momentum (lives in deep cheap futures, high capacity, billions in CTA AUM ~$300B+). BUT 2010s was a documented hard decade: rising cross-CTA correlation, central-bank vol suppression killed trends, realized Sharpe well below backtest. Capacity is high but the Sharpe is structurally modest (0.3-0.5 net) — treat as a convex diversifier, not a Sharpe engine.
- **Multi-horizon trend ensemble (AQR 'Century' construction) — the production CTA signal**
  - *Signal:* Equal-weight three TSM sub-signals on 1-month, 3-month, and 12-month lookbacks. Each sub-signal = sign (or a smoothed/tanh version of the intra-period Sharpe) of the past-k return. Size every position to a constant per-market volatility, then scale the whole portfolio to a fixed target. This is the actual signal that defines the managed-futures/CTA index.
  - *Params:* Lookbacks: 1m, 3m, 12m equal-weighted (fast captures reversals, slow captures persistent trends, diversifying across speeds). Per-market vol-sizing; PORTFOLIO target vol = 10% annualized. Monthly (or weekly) rebalance. Robert Carver's practitioner analog: EWMAC crossovers (e.g. 16/64, 32/128, 64/256-day fast/slow EWMA pairs) as continuous forecasts, capped at +/-20, forecast-diversification-multiplier applied.  ·  *Assets:* 67 markets back to 1880 in the paper; a live CTA runs 50-150 futures across equity indices, bonds/rates, FX, commodities (energy, metals, ags). Breadth IS the alpha — more uncorrelated markets raises the diversified Sharpe.
  - *Evidence (honest):* OUT-OF-SAMPLE, the gold standard: positive average return in EVERY decade since 1880, positive in 8 of 10 largest 60/40 drawdowns (crisis alpha / 'long volatility' convexity). HONEST net number: Sharpe ~0.4 after simulated transaction costs AND 2-and-20 fees. Gross of fees/costs the diversified Sharpe is higher (~1) but you do not capture that.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Best-documented durability of any momentum variant (140 years OOS). Crowding manifests not as dead returns but as MORE-correlated CTA drawdowns and shallower trends in the QE era. Edge is real but small and convex; you are paid for holding through whipsaws and for providing crisis liquidity, not for a high Sharpe.
- **Cross-Sectional Equity Momentum (Jegadeesh-Titman 6-1-6 / UMD)**
  - *Signal:* Rank stocks by cumulative return over the formation window, SKIPPING the most recent month (months t-12 to t-2 — skip avoids 1-month reversal/microstructure bid-ask bounce). Long top decile (winners), short bottom decile (losers), equal- or value-weighted, hold 1-6 months, monthly rebalance and overlap. Dollar-neutral (WML / UMD).
  - *Params:* Formation: 11 months, t-12 to t-2 (skip most recent month — load-bearing). Holding: 1-6 months. Deciles (or quintiles). Original 6-6: ~1%/month gross. Industry/residual variants exist (industry momentum, momentum on factor-residuals).  ·  *Assets:* US equities originally (NYSE/AMEX), then global equities, and 'everywhere' (AMP 2013) — equity indices, bonds, FX, commodities.
  - *Evidence (honest):* IN-SAMPLE 1965-1989: ~1%/month, ~16.5%/yr top-minus-bottom decile, Sharpe ~0.8 post-WWII through 2008. BUT: the most decayed variant. 2002-2012 long-short annualized spread ~ -2.1%; 2014-2024 averaged just ~2.23%/yr; -82% in 2009. Net of realistic shorting/turnover costs the standalone US equity momentum Sharpe today is fragile-to-negative in many windows.
  - *Decay/crowding:* HEAVILY crowded and decayed. Drivers: post-2000 decimalization removed the transaction-cost moat; 10x hedge-fund AUM; Ehsani-Linnainmaa (2022) show it is subsumed by factor momentum (not even a distinct factor). McLean-Pontiff: ~58% post-publication decay base rate. Treat naive UMD with extreme skepticism; if used, must be risk-managed and likely combined with value.
- **Risk-Managed / Constant-Volatility Momentum (Barroso-Santa-Clara; Daniel-Moskowitz dynamic)**
  - *Signal:* Take any momentum signal (XS UMD or TSM) and scale gross exposure inversely to a forecast of the strategy's OWN realized variance: w_t = (target_vol / sigma_hat_t) * base_signal, where sigma_hat_t is forecast from recent daily WML returns (e.g. realized vol of past ~126 days). Daniel-Moskowitz refinement: scale by forecast Sharpe (mean/variance), w_t proportional to mu_hat_t / sigma_hat_t^2 — lever up when expected return high AND vol low, cut when 'panic' state (post market-decline, high VIX).
  - *Params:* Vol forecast window: ~6 months daily realized vol (Barroso). Constant-vol target: e.g. 12% annualized on the strategy. Daniel-Moskowitz dynamic adds a conditional-mean forecast (bear-market indicator + variance). Rebalance monthly.  ·  *Assets:* Originally US equity momentum; the technique generalizes to TSM and all-asset momentum.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Barroso-Santa-Clara: constant-vol scaling NEARLY DOUBLES the Sharpe and virtually eliminates the crash (skewness fixed). Daniel-Moskowitz dynamic: all-asset Sharpe 1.18 (~4x the static ~0.5-0.7), significant alpha vs static WML, robust across asset classes and out-of-sample eras. The single most reliable improvement to any momentum sleeve.
  - *Decay/crowding:* The vol-scaling 'edge' is partly mechanical (Kim-Tse-Wald): it is risk-parity, not new alpha — be honest that you are buying a better risk profile, not necessarily more raw predictability. But the crash-avoidance is real and persistent because it exploits the predictability of VOLATILITY (very stable) not of returns.
- **Donchian Channel Breakout / MA-Crossover (the classic discretionary-systematic trend rules)**
  - *Signal:* Donchian/turtle: go long when price breaks above the N-day high, short when it breaks below the N-day low; exit on an opposite M-day breakout (M<N). MA-crossover: long when fast MA > slow MA (e.g. 50d>200d 'golden cross'), short/flat when fast<slow. Position-size by ATR so each unit risks a fixed % of equity; stops at k*ATR.
  - *Params:* Turtle System-1: 20-day entry breakout, 10-day exit. System-2: 55-day entry, 20-day exit. ATR-based sizing: 1 unit = ~1% equity per N (N=20-day ATR), stop at 2*ATR, pyramid up to 4 units, add every 0.5*ATR. MA pairs: 50/200, or EWMAC 16/64, 32/128, 64/256.  ·  *Assets:* Futures (the original turtle universe: commodities, FX, rates, indices). Equivalent in spirit to TSM — a breakout is a discretization of the same trend signal.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Mostly PRACTITIONER / vendor-backtest evidence (weak by academic standards; heavy survivorship & overfit risk in published CAGR numbers like '29-57% CAGR' — treat as marketing, NOT validated). The ACADEMIC justification is that breakout and MA-crossover rules are monotone transforms of past-return sign, so they inherit TSM's ~0.4 net Sharpe — no better. Use as a robustness/ensemble member, not a holy grail.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Same capacity/decay profile as TSM (it IS TSM in disguise). Parameter-specific rules (exactly 20/10) are overfit-prone; ensembling many lookbacks is the honest version. Beware vendor CAGR claims — they are almost always gross, single-path, and cherry-picked.

**Canonical sources:**
- *Returns to Buying Winners and Selling Losers: Implications for Stock Market Efficiency* — Narasimhan Jegadeesh & Sheridan Titman (1993, paper): The origin paper for cross-sectional momentum. Defined the J-month-formation / K-month-holding decile methodology (J,K in {3,6,9,12}). 6-1-6 winners-minus-losers earned ~1%/month abnormal return on NYSE/AMEX 1965-1989. Broke weak-form EMH; spawned the entire factor-momentum literature. — https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/j.1540-6261.1993.tb04702.x
- *Time Series Momentum* — Moskowitz, Ooi & Pedersen (AQR/NYU) (2012, paper): The canonical TSM/trend paper. 58 liquid futures/forwards, 1985-2009. Showed an asset's own past 12-month return predicts its next-month return (t-stats positive lags 1-12, negative 13-48), the vol-scaled diversified portfolio has gross Sharpe >1 (~2.5x equity market), little factor exposure, and pays best in market extremes (the 'TSM smile'). Provided the exact vol-scaling recipe the whole CTA industry uses. — https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0304405X11002613
- *Value and Momentum Everywhere* — Asness, Moskowitz & Pedersen (2013, paper): Proved momentum (and value) is a universal phenomenon across 8 markets/asset classes (US/UK/Europe/Japan stocks, equity-index, bond, FX, commodity futures). Key: value and momentum are negatively correlated (~ -0.5 to -0.6), so a 50/50 combo has a much higher Sharpe than either alone. Funding-liquidity risk is a common driver. The intellectual basis for multi-asset 'everywhere' factor portfolios. — https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/jofi.12021
- *Momentum Crashes* — Kent Daniel & Tobias Moskowitz (2016, paper): The definitive treatment of momentum's fat left tail. WML's up-market beta in bear states (-1.51) is double its down-market beta (-0.70): it behaves like a written call option. Worst crashes: July-Aug 1932 and Mar-May 2009 (loser decile +163%). Their dynamic strategy scaling weights by forecast mean/variance lifts the all-asset Sharpe to 1.18 (~4x static). Essential reading for risk management. — https://www.nber.org/papers/w20439
- *A Century of Evidence on Trend-Following Investing* — Hurst, Ooi & Pedersen (AQR) (2017 (orig. 2012), paper): Out-of-sample acid test: built the canonical equal-weighted 1m/3m/12m TSM signal on 67 markets back to 1880, vol-scaled to 10% target. Positive every decade since 1880, positive in 8 of 10 worst 60/40 drawdowns (crisis alpha). The honest number lives here: net Sharpe ~0.4 after realistic transaction costs and 2/20 fees. The reference for trend capacity and durability. — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2993026
- *Momentum Has Its Moments* — Pedro Barroso & Pedro Santa-Clara (2015, paper): Showed momentum risk is highly predictable from its own recent realized variance; scaling exposure inversely to forecast variance (constant-vol targeting) nearly doubles the Sharpe and virtually eliminates crashes. The simplest, most robust momentum risk-management technique — strictly easier to implement than Daniel-Moskowitz dynamic scaling and nearly as good. — https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0304405X14002566
- *Time Series Momentum and Volatility Scaling* — Kim, Tse & Wald (2016, paper): The crucial skeptical counterpoint: much of TSM's headline Sharpe comes from the VOLATILITY-SCALING overlay, not the momentum SIGNAL per se. Without vol-scaling, TSM and buy-and-hold deliver similar cumulative returns. Forces honesty about WHAT is actually generating the edge — a risk-parity/vol-targeting effect vs. genuine return predictability. — https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1386418116301379
- *Factor Momentum and the Momentum Factor* — Sina Ehsani & Juhani Linnainmaa (2022, paper): Devastating for naive XS equity momentum: momentum in high-eigenvalue PCA factor returns SUBSUMES individual-stock momentum, industry momentum, intermediate momentum, and Sharpe-ratio momentum. Momentum is not a distinct risk factor — it times other factors (avg factor earns 51bps after a positive year vs 6bps after a losing year). Means a stock-momentum sleeve may be redundant if you already run factor-timing. — https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/jofi.13131
- *Does Academic Research Destroy Stock Return Predictability?* — R. David McLean & Jeffrey Pontiff (2016, paper): The canonical post-publication decay study: 97 characteristics' returns decline ~58% after publication (~26% from in-sample overfit, ~32% from arbitrage/learning). The base-rate prior every backtested Sharpe must be discounted against. Momentum decays less than most because it is costly to arbitrage, but the prior still bites. — https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/jofi.12365
- *Expected Returns / Systematic Trading (practitioner books)* — Antti Ilmanen (AQR) ; Robert Carver (2011 / 2015, book): Ilmanen's 'Expected Returns' is the practitioner bible on momentum/trend risk premia, decay and harvesting costs. Carver's 'Systematic Trading' gives the actual implementation: forecast scaling to a target, the EWMAC (exponentially-weighted MA crossover) trading rule family, position sizing by volatility target, and forecast diversification multipliers — the bridge from paper to running CTA book. — https://www.systematicmoney.org/systematic-trading

**Pitfalls:** (1) VOL-SCALING MASQUERADING AS ALPHA (the #1 trap): Kim-Tse-Wald show much of TSM's headline Sharpe is the volatility-targeting overlay, not return predictability. If you backtest TSM-with-vol-scaling and attribute the Sharpe to 'momentum', you are fooling yourself. ALWAYS decompose: run the raw signal at constant gross exposure vs. the vol-scaled version, and report both. The signal and the risk overlay are separate edges. (2) CROSS-SECTIONAL MOMENTUM IS LARGELY DECAYED & CROWDED: naive US equity UMD has had a brutal 2002-2024 (spreads near zero or negative, -82% in 2009). Do NOT build a standalone equity-momentum book expecting 1990s Sharpes. McLean-Pontiff ~58% post-pub decay + Ehsani-Linnainmaa subsumption mean the honest forward prior is low. (3) MOMENTUM CRASHES / NEGATIVE SKEW: XS momentum has a fat left tail — it is short a call option on the market in bear-market rebounds (1932, 2009). An un-risk-managed momentum book WILL eventually take a -50% to -90% monthly hit off a market bottom. Never run momentum without constant-vol or dynamic scaling. (4) THE SKIP-MONTH IS LOAD-BEARING FOR XS, ABSENT FOR TSM: cross-sectional equity momentum MUST skip the most recent month (t-12 to t-2) to avoid the 1-month short-term reversal and bid-ask bounce. TSM/trend in futures does NOT skip (uses the full 12m incl. last month). Getting this backwards corrupts the signal. (5) TRANSACTION COSTS & TURNOVER DOMINATE NET RESULTS: gross-to-net is enormous. Hurst-Ooi-Pedersen's honest number is ~0.4 net vs ~1.0 gross. Equity momentum's high turnover + shorting costs + decimalization-era thin spreads can erase the entire premium. Any backtest without realistic, asset-class-specific costs (incl. futures-roll costs and market impact scaled to AUM) is a Profit Mirage. (6) OVERFIT LOOKBACKS / VENDOR CAGR CLAIMS: '20/10 Donchian gives 29% CAGR' style numbers are single-path, gross, and parameter-mined. The robust approach is ENSEMBLING many lookbacks/speeds (1m+3m+12m, multiple EWMAC pairs) and accepting a lower, stable Sharpe. Distrust any single-parameter 'optimal' rule. (7) CTA CROWDING & CORRELATED DRAWDOWNS: the 2010s showed trend-followers increasingly correlated; crowding shows up as synchronized drawdowns and shallower trends (QE vol-suppression), not as a clean dead-signal. Position sizing must assume your trend book correlates with the rest of the CTA industry in a squeeze. (8) SURVIVORSHIP & DATA SNOOPING IN THE UNIVERSE: backtesting trend on currently-liquid futures ignores delisted/dead contracts. Building XS momentum on a survivor equity universe inflates the premium. Use point-in-time universes. (9) REGIME DEPENDENCE: momentum/trend pays in trending, extreme markets and bleeds in choppy, mean-reverting, low-vol regimes. A flat 12-18 month stretch is NORMAL and not evidence the edge is gone — but it is also not distinguishable in real time from genuine decay, which is the hardest live problem.

**Honest validation:** (1) DECOMPOSE SIGNAL vs. RISK-OVERLAY (anti-Profit-Mirage core test): run the raw momentum signal at CONSTANT gross exposure AND the vol-scaled version separately. Report Sharpe of each. If nearly all the Sharpe comes from vol-scaling, you have a risk-parity strategy, not a momentum-prediction edge — label it honestly (Kim-Tse-Wald discipline). (2) REALISTIC, ASSET-CLASS-SPECIFIC NET-OF-COST BACKTEST: model bid-ask, market impact (scaled to intended AUM), futures roll costs, and shorting/borrow for equities. Target replicating Hurst-Ooi-Pedersen's ~0.4 NET Sharpe for trend — if your net Sharpe is >0.7 on a diversified trend book, suspect a costing or look-ahead bug. (3) OUT-OF-SAMPLE & DEEP-HISTORY TEST: the strongest validation in this family is temporal OOS. Reserve a holdout (e.g. post-2010, the hard decade) and a deep pre-sample (pre-1985 / pre-WWII via extended datasets). A signal that only works 1985-2009 is suspect; trend's credibility comes from working every decade since 1880. (4) CRASH / TAIL STRESS TEST: explicitly compute performance in Mar-May 2009, 1932 (if data), Aug 2007 quant-quake, Q4 2018, Mar 2020. Measure max monthly drawdown and skewness. If an un-risk-managed momentum book does NOT show a severe left tail, your backtest is wrong (the tail is a known feature). (5) DEFLATED SHARPE / MULTIPLE-TESTING ADJUSTMENT: because this family invites lookback/parameter mining, apply Bailey-Lopez de Prado deflated Sharpe or Harvey-Liu-Zhu (t-stat hurdle ~3.0, not 2.0) to penalize the number of configurations tried. Report the haircut. (6) FACTOR-SPANNING / REDUNDANCY TEST: regress your momentum returns on existing factors (market, value, carry, and especially FACTOR momentum per Ehsani-Linnainmaa). If the alpha vanishes against factor-momentum or against an existing trend sleeve, the strategy is redundant, not additive. (7) WALK-FORWARD PARAMETER STABILITY: re-estimate any chosen lookbacks/vol-windows in rolling walk-forward; the edge must survive without re-optimization. Ensemble across parameters rather than picking the in-sample best. (8) CAPACITY / TURNOVER SANITY CHECK: estimate turnover and the AUM at which market impact eats the premium. XS equity momentum has high turnover and low capacity; trend in liquid futures has high capacity — quantify before sizing. (9) LIVE PAPER-TRADE LAG CHECK: confirm the signal uses only information available at decision time (skip-month for XS, no same-bar look-ahead, vol estimated on lagged data) — re-run with an enforced 1-day execution lag and confirm the Sharpe survives.
