**Data:** "Trend/TSM: daily settlement prices for a broad futures/forward cross-section (50-150 markets: equity indices, sovereign bond/rate futures, G10+EM FX, energies, metals, ags), continuous back-adjusted series WITH roll-yield/roll-cost tracked, point-in-time (incl. delisted contracts to avoid survivorship). Need ~12+ months history per instrument to form the slowest signal; deep history (decades) for credible OOS. Cross-sectional equity momentum: point-in-time CRSP-style daily/monthly total returns (incl. delisted), shares-outstanding for value-weighting, sector/industry codes (GICS) for industry-momentum & neutralization, borrow/short-availability data for realistic shorting costs. Both: asset-class-specific transaction-cost estimates (spreads, market impact curves), risk-free/financing rates for excess returns, and a daily-return panel to estimate ex-ante volatility (EWMA, ~60-day center-of-mass). For risk-managed variants: daily strategy-level returns to forecast WML/strategy variance. Corporate-action-adjusted prices essential for equities; FX needs interest-rate differentials for the carry/excess-return component."

**Fits our stack:** "R3 (evidence): this family is the CLEANEST fit for an honest evidence ledger because the literature itself is split gross-vs-net and in-sample-vs-OOS. Encode each claim with provenance: 'TSM gross Sharpe >1 (MOP 2012, in-sample 1985-2009)' vs 'trend net Sharpe ~0.4 (HOP 2017, OOS 1880-2013, after 2/20+costs)' vs 'XS equity momentum decayed to ~2%/yr 2014-2024'. R3 should store the Kim-Tse-Wald and Ehsani-Linnainmaa REFUTATIONS as first-class evidence, not footnotes — this is the anti-Profit-Mirage discipline operationalized. R4 (data): drives the futures cross-section + point-in-time equity panel requirements above; the breadth of the futures universe is itself an alpha input, so R4 should prioritize MARKET COUNT and survivorship-free history. R5 (alpha): implement the signal library — TSM (12m sign), multi-horizon ensemble (1/3/12m or EWMAC pairs), XS UMD (t-12 to t-2 skip-month), and the risk-overlays (Barroso constant-vol, Daniel-Moskowitz dynamic) as SEPARATE composable modules so signal and risk-scaling are independently testable (this is what Kim-Tse-Wald demands). RISK: momentum's negative skew / written-call behavior must be a hard risk-model input — size with constant-vol targeting by default, never raw signal. PORTFOLIO: exploit the value-momentum negative correlation (AMP 2013) for a higher-Sharpe combo; treat trend as a convex DIVERSIFIER/crisis-alpha sleeve, not a Sharpe maximizer. EXECUTION: turnover and roll-cost modeling is make-or-break (gross-to-net gap is the whole story); execution layer must model market impact scaled to AUM and futures roll. This family DEEPENS the IG-12 prompt on systematic factor/trend strategies — specifically the sub-prompt distinguishing time-series vs cross-sectional momentum, and the 'is the Sharpe from the signal or the risk overlay?' decomposition that should be a standard IG-12 interrogation of any momentum backtest."

**Go deeper:** • Read the actual MOP 2012 (Time Series Momentum) Section 2-3 for the exact pooled-regression t-stat plot (positive lags 1-12, reversal 13-48) and the 40%/sigma vol-scaling with 60-day-COM EWMA — the literal recipe to code first. • Daniel-Moskowitz 2016 'Momentum Crashes' Section 4 + Table 7: the dynamic-weight formula (scale exposure proportional to conditional Sharpe = mu_hat/sigma_hat^2) and the spanning tests showing it isn't explained by constant-vol momentum — implement BOTH the Barroso constant-vol and the DM dynamic and compare. • Robert Carver 'Systematic Trading' + his blog (qoppac.blogspot.com): the practitioner bridge — EWMAC forecast construction, forecast scaling/capping, the forecast-diversification-multiplier, instrument-diversification-multiplier, and vol-target position sizing. This is how you actually turn the papers into a running book. • Kim-Tse-Wald 2016 in full: the precise decomposition of how much TSM Sharpe survives WITHOUT vol-scaling — run their experiment on your own data before trusting any momentum Sharpe. • Ehsani-Linnainmaa 2022 'Factor Momentum and the Momentum Factor' (JF) + Gupta-Kelly 'Factor Momentum Everywhere' (2019): if you run multiple factors, test whether a stock-momentum sleeve adds anything beyond timing your existing factors — likely redundant. • Asness-Moskowitz-Pedersen 2013 AMP 'Value and Momentum Everywhere' Tables III-V: the value-momentum negative correlation and combo Sharpes — the basis for the portfolio-construction win. • AQR's 'You Can't Always Trend When You Want' (2019, Babu-Hoffman-Levine-Ooi-Pedersen-et al): the honest post-mortem on WHY trend struggled in the 2010s (lower number/size of trends, not a broken signal) — essential for calibrating live expectations vs declaring decay. • Antti Ilmanen 'Expected Returns' (2011) chapters on momentum and trend: the risk-premia framing, harvesting costs, and decay — the strategic context for sizing this family in a real book. • Hurst-Ooi-Pedersen 'A Century of Evidence' full PDF (SSRN 2993026) Exhibits 1-5: per-decade and per-asset Sharpes and the crisis-alpha table — the durability evidence and the ~0.4 net number. • Moskowitz-Ooi-Pedersen + later 'Trend-Following: Equity and Bond Crisis Alpha' and the 'TSM smile' / convexity literature: to model the option-like payoff for the portfolio/risk layer (trend is long volatility / long gamma in crises).

---
## mean-reversion-statarb

**Thesis.** Mean-reversion stat-arb monetizes the gap between the price of *immediacy* and the price of *information*. Two distinct economic engines sit underneath, and conflating them is the single biggest source of false confidence. (1) RELATIVE-VALUE CONVERGENCE: two (or N) assets share a common stochastic trend — same factor exposures, same cash-flow drivers — so the spread between them is stationary even though each leg is a random walk. When idiosyncratic supply/demand shocks (index rebalances, fund flows, sentiment, one-sided order flow) push the spread away from its equilibrium, an arbitrageur who can hold the convergence trade earns the gap as prices re-couple. The edge persists only to the extent that (a) the cointegrating relationship is economically real and not spurious, and (b) capital willing to bear *divergence risk* (the spread can widen arbitrarily before it converges — Shleifer-Vishny limits-to-arbitrage) is scarce. (2) LIQUIDITY PROVISION / SHORT-TERM REVERSAL: at daily-and-faster horizons, mean reversion is mostly compensation for absorbing the price impact of liquidity-demanding order flow (Lehmann 1990, Lo-MacKinlay 1990, Khandani-Lo 2007 reframe contrarian profits as the *return to market-making*). You buy what was just sold hard and sell what was just bought hard; you are paid the bid-ask/impact premium for providing immediacy. This engine decays fastest because it is a capacity-constrained, crowding-sensitive service business — once enough capital provides the same liquidity, the premium compresses, and in a deleveraging cascade (Aug 2007) the crowd all demands liquidity at once and the strategy's worst-case becomes catastrophic and correlated. DECAY THESIS: both engines have materially decayed since ~2003 (Avellaneda-Lee's own Sharpe halved 1997-2003 → 2003-2007; GGR distance-method profits faded post-2002) because falling commissions/spreads, decimalization, faster execution, ETF arbitrage, and the entry of dozens of stat-arb desks competed away the easy convergence and compressed the immediacy premium. What survives in 2026 is NOT the textbook recipe: it is (a) cointegration with regime-awareness and rigorous net-of-cost discipline, (b) residual stat-arb on harder/less-arbitraged universes (mid-cap, international, crypto, intraday), and (c) reversal only where you genuinely have the lowest execution cost in the room. The edge is real but thin, regime-dependent, capacity-limited, and lethal under crowded deleveraging — treat it as a liquidity/risk-premium harvest, not a free lunch.

**Concrete strategies:**
- **Distance-method pairs (GGR) — BASELINE / DECAYED, build it to benchmark, not to trade**
  - *Signal:* Formation (12 mo): normalize each stock to a cumulative-total-return price index starting at 1. For every candidate pair compute SSD = sum over formation days of (P_i,t - P_j,t)^2; rank, keep the smallest-SSD pairs. Trading (next 6 mo): let D_t = P_i,t - P_j,t; let sigma be the std of D over the formation window. OPEN when |D_t| > 2*sigma (long the cheap leg, short the rich, dollar-neutral). CLOSE on next crossing of D_t through 0 (convergence), or force-close at period end. One-day-wait rule: open at next day's price to avoid bid-ask bounce inflating returns.
  - *Params:* formation=12mo, trade=6mo, divergence trigger=2.0 std, top-5 and top-20 portfolios, overlapping 6-mo cohorts started each month, return computed on COMMITTED capital (number of pairs available) not just employed (the honest, lower number).  ·  *Assets:* US equities (liquid common stock, same exchange); also tested in FX, commodities, intl equities, crypto.
  - *Evidence (honest):* GGR (RFS 2006): ~11%/yr annualized excess return, top-20, 1962-2002, low beta, in-sample-ish (rule fixed ex ante but tested on history). Bootstrap shows it's not generic reversal. HONEST CAVEAT: risk-adj return on top-20 fell from ~0.67%/mo (1963-1988) to ~0.42%/mo post-1989; post-2002 out-of-sample the simple distance rule is widely reported as NOT robustly profitable net of modern costs. Treat published 11% as a non-replicable historical artifact.
  - *Decay/crowding:* HEAVILY DECAYED. Harbourfront 2026 review: 'simple mechanical versions that worked in the 1990s/early 2000s no longer deliver robust returns.' Crowding + decimalization + commission collapse competed it away. Build it ONLY as a sanity/benchmark baseline.
- **Cointegration pairs (Engle-Granger / Johansen + OU spread) — STILL WORKS with discipline**
  - *Signal:* Selection: for candidate pairs of LOG prices, Engle-Granger 2-step — regress log P_y on log P_x (and const) to get hedge ratio gamma and spread z_t = log P_y - gamma*log P_x - mu; ADF-test z_t for stationarity (reject unit root). For N>2 baskets use Johansen trace/max-eigenvalue test to get the cointegrating vector. Half-life filter: fit Δz_t = lambda*z_{t-1} + e_t (AR(1)/OU), half-life = -ln(2)/lambda; KEEP only pairs with economically sensible half-life (e.g. ~1-30 trading days) AND an economic rationale (same sector/factor). Signal: z-score = (z_t - rolling_mean)/rolling_std. Enter at |z|≈2, scale, exit near 0; hard stop at |z|≈3-3.5 or on cointegration breakdown (rolling ADF p-value rises / hedge ratio drifts).
  - *Params:* rolling estimation window 60-252 d; entry z≈1.5-2.5, exit z≈0-0.5, stop z≈3+; half-life acceptance ~1-30 d; RE-ESTIMATE gamma on a rolling/Kalman basis (static gamma is a classic failure); demand p_ADF strongly significant AFTER multiple-testing correction.  ·  *Assets:* equities (same-sector), ETFs, futures (calendar/crack/crush spreads), rates, crypto majors. Best where the cointegration is structurally forced (dual-listings, ETF-vs-basket, futures-vs-spot).
  - *Evidence (honest):* Mixed but positive when costs modeled: cointegration methods are the 'most robust' family in the 2026 Harbourfront review and survive net-of-cost in several markets; but performance is regime-dependent and Sharpes decline in low-vol subperiods. Most published edge is in-sample / pre-cost — demand net-of-cost, out-of-sample, multiple-testing-corrected evidence before believing any pair.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Moderately decayed in saturated US large-cap; survives better in mid-cap, international, futures spreads, and crypto. Main risk = SPURIOUS cointegration from scanning thousands of pairs (multiple testing) and structural breaks where the relationship simply ends (M&A, regime change).
- **Avellaneda-Lee residual stat-arb (PCA or ETF, OU s-score) — the systematic workhorse**
  - *Signal:* Daily: estimate factor structure on a trailing window of returns. EITHER (a) PCA on the empirical correlation matrix → keep top ~15 eigenportfolios (or enough for ~55% variance), regress each stock's returns on eigenportfolio returns to get residuals; OR (b) regress each stock on its sector ETF return. Cumulate the residuals X_k(t)=Σ residual; fit OU: dX=kappa(m-X)dt+sigma dW via the AR(1) auxiliary regression. Compute s-score_k = (X_k - m)/sigma_eq where sigma_eq=sigma/sqrt(2*kappa). Trade contrarian: OPEN LONG if s < -sbo(=-1.25), OPEN SHORT if s > sso(=+1.25); CLOSE LONG if s > -ssc(=-0.50), CLOSE SHORT if s < sbc(=+0.75). Hold the whole book market/factor-neutral by construction (residuals are factor-orthogonal). Avellaneda-Lee also subtract the estimated drift to avoid trading non-mean-reverting names.
  - *Params:* estimation window 60 trading days; require FAST reversion: kappa large enough that mean-reversion time 1/kappa << window (their filter ~ kappa > 252/30, i.e. half-life under ~30 cal days, ~ a week or two); ~15 PCs; thresholds sbo=sso=1.25, sbc=0.75, ssc=0.50; equal-dollar long/short, daily rebalance, ~ leverage 2-4x historically (DANGER — see Khandani-Lo).  ·  *Assets:* US equities (the canonical universe); generalizes to any large cross-section with a clean factor model — international equities, crypto cross-sections.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Avellaneda-Lee (2010): PCA Sharpe ~1.44 net of cost 1997-2007, BUT only ~0.9 in 2003-2007; ETF-with-volume variant ~1.51 in 2003-2007. So even the seminal paper documents its own ~halving of Sharpe as the strategy aged. Sharpes are gross-of-financing/borrow and pre-crowding-2008.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Decayed in US large-cap residual space (most arbitraged corner of the market). The Aug-2007 quant quake hit exactly this strategy. Edge migrates to harder universes, intraday horizons, and better cost/borrow modeling. Capacity-limited.
- **Short-term reversal / daily liquidity provision (Lehmann/Lo-MacKinlay/Khandani-Lo) — high-Sharpe, high-fragility**
  - *Signal:* Cross-sectional contrarian: each day (or week) rank the universe by prior 1-day (or 1-week) return; go long the biggest losers, short the biggest winners, dollar- and beta-neutral, weights ∝ -(r_i - mean_r). You are being paid to ABSORB the order flow that pushed those names. Refinements that matter post-2010: residualize the reversal signal against industry/size/short-term-momentum, exclude the bid-ask-bounce component (skip the most-recent tick or use mid-quotes), condition on high-volume/illiquidity (reversal is stronger where immediacy was scarcer), and avoid earnings/news names (those moves are information, not liquidity, and DON'T revert).
  - *Params:* lookback 1 day to 1 week; hold 1 day to 1 week; universe = liquid names (illiquid names show fake reversal from microstructure); decile or rank-weighted long-short; turnover is EXTREME (~100%+/day) so net edge lives or dies on per-trade cost.  ·  *Assets:* equities primarily; intraday reversal in futures/FX/crypto.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Lehmann/Jegadeesh (1990): ~1-2%/month gross in early samples. Khandani-Lo: the daily contrarian return shrank from ~1.4%/day mid-90s to a small fraction by 2007 → only attractive levered. Modern decomposition (FRBNY SR513): the liquidity-provision slice persists; the overreaction slice decayed. Gross numbers are wildly misleading — this is the single worst family for the in-sample/pre-cost mirage.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Most crowded and most dangerous. Aug 2007 IS this strategy's tail: synchronized deleveraging → 4-7% loss in 3 days then +24% bounce. The profit is a liquidity premium that vanishes precisely when everyone needs liquidity. Only run it if you are a genuine low-cost liquidity provider with real (un-levered) cash buffers.

**Canonical sources:**
- *Pairs Trading: Performance of a Relative-Value Arbitrage Rule* — Gatev, Goetzmann & Rouwenhorst (GGR) (2006 (working paper 1999, NBER w7032), paper): The foundational academic pairs-trading paper. Defines the 'distance method': 12-month formation, 12 m rank pairs by sum of squared deviations of normalized cumulative-total-return price series, trade in the next 6 m, open at 2-sigma divergence, close on convergence. Reports ~11%/yr annualized excess return on top-5/top-20 self-financing portfolios over 1962-2002, low market beta, profits exceeding conservative costs. Crucially BOOTSTRAPS random pairs to show the effect is NOT generic mean-reversion. Every later study is measured against it — and it is the canonical example of a result that DECAYED post-publication. — https://academic.oup.com/rfs/article-abstract/19/3/797/1646694
- *Statistical Arbitrage in the U.S. Equities Market* — Avellaneda & Lee (2010 (SSRN 2008), paper): The canonical *systematic* (not pairwise) stat-arb framework. Two ways to extract residuals: PCA eigenportfolios (~15 factors, or enough to explain ~55% variance) or regression on sector ETFs. Model cumulative residual as Ornstein-Uhlenbeck, compute the dimensionless s-score = (X-m)/sigma_eq, trade contrarian on s-score thresholds. The blueprint for modern equity market-neutral residual trading and the right mental model for our R5 alpha layer. Honest about decay: Sharpe ~1.44 (1997-2007) but ~0.9 in 2003-2007 sub-period. — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=1153505
- *What Happened to the Quants in August 2007?* — Khandani & Lo (2007/2011 (NBER w14465), paper): The indispensable RISK paper for this family. Reframes daily contrarian/mean-reversion profits as the *return to liquidity provision*, shows that return had shrunk dramatically by 2007 (from ~1.4%/day in 1995 to a fraction by 2007 → required leverage to stay attractive), and documents the 'unwind' deleveraging cascade: a 4-7% loss over Aug 7-9 then +23.67% bounce Aug 10. The proof that this family's tail risk is endogenous, correlated, and crowding-driven. Read BEFORE sizing any reversal book. — https://web.mit.edu/Alo/www/Papers/august07.pdf
- *Optimal Mean Reversion Trading: Mathematical Analysis and Practical Applications* — Tim Leung & Xin Li (2016 (book; core paper arXiv:1411.5062, 2015), book): The rigorous treatment of WHEN to enter/exit an OU spread. Formulates entry+exit as an optimal double-stopping problem with transaction costs and a stop-loss; proves the optimal entry region is a bounded interval strictly above the stop-loss and that take-profit decreases as stop-loss rises. Moves you beyond arbitrary '2-sigma in, 0 out' to cost- and risk-aware thresholds. Directly informs our risk/execution layer. — https://arxiv.org/abs/1411.5062
- *Pairs Trading: Quantitative Methods and Analysis* — Ganapathy Vidyamurthy (2004, book): The practitioner bridge that popularized the COINTEGRATION (vs distance) approach: Engle-Granger error-correction framing for pairs, spread construction via the cointegrating vector, and the operational pipeline. The reference most desks actually built v1 from. — https://www.wiley.com/en-us/Pairs+Trading%3A+Quantitative+Methods+and+Analysis-p-9780471460671
- *Advances in Financial Machine Learning (Ch. 7, 11-12) + The Deflated Sharpe Ratio / Prob. of Backtest Overfitting* — Marcos López de Prado (with Bailey, Borwein) (2018 / 2014-2016, book): The anti-Profit-Mirage canon. Purged + embargoed cross-validation, Combinatorial Purged CV (CPCV), Deflated Sharpe Ratio (DSR) and Probability of Backtest Overfitting (PBO). Mandatory because stat-arb research = thousands of candidate pairs/parameter combos = a multiple-testing machine that manufactures false Sharpes. This is the validation spine for our R3 evidence gate. — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2460551
- *Decomposing Short-Term Return Reversal* — Cheng, Hameed, Subrahmanyam, Titman (FRBNY SR513) + Lehmann (1990), Lo-MacKinlay (1990) (1990 / 2017, paper): Origin + modern decomposition of the short-term reversal effect. Lehmann/Jegadeesh: contrarian weekly/monthly returns ~1-2%/month in early samples. Modern work decomposes reversal into a liquidity-provision component (persists, capacity-limited) and an overreaction/information component (weaker, decays), telling you which part of the reversal premium is still harvestable and which is a mirage. — https://www.newyorkfed.org/medialibrary/media/research/staff_reports/sr513.pdf
- *arbitragelab documentation (OU optimal mean reversion, cointegration, PCA/ETF residual approaches)* — Hudson & Thames (2020-2023, repo): The most complete open implementation reference tying the papers to code: OU MLE calibration, half-life = ln(2)/theta, Engle-Granger & Johansen pair selection, Avellaneda-Lee PCA/ETF s-score, Leung-Li optimal levels. Use as the spec for our R4/R5 code, not as a black box (verify formulas against the source papers — notation differs, e.g. they swap theta/mu). — https://hudson-and-thames-arbitragelab.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/optimal_mean_reversion/ou_model.html

**Pitfalls:** (1) In-sample / pre-cost mirage is the dominant failure: turnover is high, so a strategy that looks like Sharpe 2 gross routinely goes to <0.5 or negative after realistic spread+impact+borrow+financing. ALWAYS quote net-of-cost, and model impact as a function of your own size, not a flat bps. (2) Multiple-testing / spurious cointegration: scanning thousands of candidate pairs guarantees some pass ADF/Johansen by chance. A 5% test on 10,000 pairs yields ~500 'significant' spurious pairs. Without DSR/PBO correction you are mining noise. Demand an economic rationale per pair, not just a p-value. (3) Look-ahead and survivorship bias: using current index membership, post-hoc tickers, or full-sample normalization leaks the future. GGR-style normalization must use ONLY formation-window data; delisted/merged names must be in the universe (M&A is where shorts get crushed). (4) Static hedge ratio: cointegrating vector gamma drifts. A pair that was cointegrated breaks (regime change, M&A, business-model divergence). Use rolling/Kalman gamma and a cointegration-breakdown stop, or you ride a 'converging' spread to infinity. (5) Divergence risk / no convergence guarantee (Shleifer-Vishny limits to arbitrage): the spread can widen far past your entry and stay wide longer than your capital/margin lasts. 2-sigma entry with no stop is a short-vol trap that blows up on the fat tail. (6) Endogenous, correlated tail (the Quant Quake): every stat-arb desk holds near-identical books. In a deleveraging cascade they all sell the same names at once; your 'market-neutral' book takes a coordinated 4-7%+ hit (Aug 2007) and your diversification across pairs evaporates exactly when you need it. (7) Bid-ask bounce manufactures fake reversal: at daily/intraday horizons, measuring returns trade-to-trade (not mid-to-mid) creates a microstructure reversal that is pure illusion and uncapturable. The one-day-wait rule and mid-quote pricing exist to kill this — skip them and your backtest lies. (8) Information vs liquidity confusion: reversal works for liquidity-driven moves and FAILS (continues) for information-driven moves (earnings, M&A, guidance). Trading reversal on news names = systematically buying falling knives. (9) Borrow cost and short availability: the short leg may be hard-to-borrow (high rebate cost) or unavailable precisely for the most-divergent names — exactly the trades the backtest loved. Unmodeled borrow turns positive gross into negative net. (10) Leverage as the hidden dependency: Khandani-Lo show the raw edge became so thin that historical Sharpes only look good levered 2-8x. Leverage is what turned a bad week into an existential event in 2007. Report UNLEVERED economics first; treat leverage as a separate, risk-managed decision. (11) Parameter overfitting of thresholds/lookbacks: '2-sigma in, 0 out, 60-day window' optima are themselves a fitted choice across a grid — re-optimizing entry/exit/half-life cutoffs per backtest is textbook overfitting. Fix them ex ante or use CPCV.

**Honest validation:** (1) Net-of-cost from the first backtest, never gross: charge realistic half-spread + a size-dependent impact model (e.g. square-root impact) + commissions + borrow/financing. Re-run the headline Sharpe after costs and report BOTH; the gap is the honesty gap. For high-turnover reversal, also report break-even cost (the per-trade cost at which edge = 0). (2) Walk-forward / purged + embargoed cross-validation (López de Prado): never evaluate on the same window you selected pairs or tuned thresholds on. Purge overlapping labels and embargo a gap to kill leakage. Prefer Combinatorial Purged CV (CPCV) to get a distribution of out-of-sample Sharpes, not one lucky path. (3) Deflated Sharpe Ratio (DSR) + Probability of Backtest Overfitting (PBO): adjust the Sharpe significance threshold for the NUMBER OF TRIALS (every pair, window, threshold combo counts), plus skew/kurtosis. Report PBO; if PBO is high the 'best' config is expected to underperform its own median out-of-sample — discard it. (4) Bootstrap against random/sham pairs (GGR's own control): regenerate the strategy on randomly matched pairs (or sham factor residuals) thousands of times. The real strategy's return must lie far in the right tail of the random-pair distribution; if it doesn't, you have generic mean-reversion or noise, not a relative-value edge. (5) Sub-period and regime split: report Sharpe by year and by vol regime (e.g. VIX terciles). A single full-sample Sharpe hides the Avellaneda-Lee pattern (1.44 → 0.9). If the edge lives in one pre-2008 sub-period, it is decayed, not real. (6) Stress the crowding tail explicitly: simulate a forced-deleveraging shock (Aug-2007-style) by adding correlated impact across all positions; measure max drawdown and time-to-recover UNLEVERED and at intended leverage. If the strategy only survives because of leverage assumptions, it is not validated. (7) Capacity / turnover realism: estimate strategy capacity (AUM at which your own impact eats the edge) and check turnover is fundable. A Sharpe-2 strategy at $5M capacity is a different object than one at $500M — report capacity-adjusted economics. (8) Live paper-trading / out-of-sample holdout before sizing: keep a true OOS period (most recent N months) untouched during all research; the strategy must clear net-of-cost, DSR-significant hurdles there before any capital. Treat the holdout as one-shot — re-peeking burns it.

**Data:** "MINIMUM: split-and-dividend-adjusted daily total-return prices (NOT just close prices — cumulative TOTAL return for distance/cointegration), point-in-time index membership and a survivorship-bias-free universe INCLUDING delisted/merged tickers (CRSP-quality or equivalent; Sharadar/Norgate for retail-grade), and per-name sector/industry classification + ETF mappings for the Avellaneda-Lee ETF variant. For cointegration you need long histories (252+ d per estimation window, multi-year to test stability). FOR HONEST COSTS: historical bid-ask spreads (or TAQ/quote data) to model the half-spread, average daily volume + market cap for impact and capacity modeling, and a borrow-cost / short-availability feed (e.g. IBKR rebate data) — without borrow data the short leg is fiction. FOR RESIDUAL STAT-ARB: a clean returns panel wide enough for stable PCA (hundreds of names) and a factor/risk model (or roll your own PCA). FOR REVERSAL/INTRADAY: tick or minute bars with quotes (mid-prices), since trade-to-trade returns inject fake reversal. CORPORATE-ACTION cleanliness (splits, spinoffs, ticker changes, M&A dates) is load-bearing — most blow-ups trace to a missed action. Earnings/news calendars to flag information-driven (non-reverting) moves. All data must be point-in-time / as-reported to avoid look-ahead."

**Fits our stack:** "R3 (evidence): This family is the canonical anti-Profit-Mirage testbed — wire DSR + PBO + CPCV + random-pair-bootstrap as MANDATORY gates before any pair/parameter set is promoted. The R3 gate should reject any stat-arb signal whose net-of-cost, multiple-testing-corrected Sharpe isn't significant on a true holdout, and should require sub-period/regime breakdown (auto-flag the Avellaneda-Lee '1.44→0.9' decay pattern). R4 (data): drives concrete requirements above — survivorship-free universe, point-in-time membership, corporate actions, bid-ask + ADV + borrow feeds; flag that close-only price data is INSUFFICIENT. R5 (alpha): three implementable signal generators — (a) cointegration-pairs (Engle-Granger/Johansen + OU half-life filter + z-score), (b) Avellaneda-Lee residual s-score (PCA or ETF), (c) short-term reversal (residualized, liquidity-conditioned). All emit a continuous score (z-score / s-score) → clean interface to a sizing layer. RISK: market/factor neutrality by construction (residual orthogonality or dollar/beta-neutral legs), per-pair cointegration-breakdown stops, |z| hard stops, AND a portfolio-level CROWDING/deleverage stress test (correlated-impact shock) — Khandani-Lo says the dangerous risk is at the book level, not the pair level. Cap leverage explicitly and report unlevered economics. PORTFOLIO: aggregate many thin, decorrelated convergence bets; size by half-life (faster reversion = more capital-efficient), and constrain gross/net by capacity estimates. EXECUTION: this family is execution-bound — the entire edge can be the difference between paying and earning the spread. Need a passive/liquidity-providing execution model (post, don't cross, where possible), per-trade cost attribution, and turnover budgeting. IG-12 PROMPT THIS DEEPENS: the mean-reversion / statistical-arbitrage strategy-family prompt — replace any 'ask-the-LLM-for-a-pairs-strategy' shallowness with this explicit pipeline (selection test → half-life filter → OU/z-score thresholds → net-of-cost CPCV/DSR validation → crowding stress → capacity-bounded sizing), and hard-code the decay/crowding warnings so the system never ships the textbook 11%/yr number as a live expectation."

**Go deeper:** • Read the Avellaneda-Lee (2010) PDF directly (math.nyu.edu/faculty/avellane) and reconcile the s-score thresholds + the 'modified s-score' (drift-subtracted) and the kappa>252/30 fast-reversion filter against the arbitragelab implementation — verify, don't trust, since notation (theta/mu/kappa) differs across sources. • Khandani-Lo (web.mit.edu/Alo/www/Papers/august07.pdf) full text: extract the exact decay of the daily contrarian return 1995→2007 and the leverage/cash-buffer survival lesson; use it to parameterize the crowding stress test. • López de Prado, Advances in Financial Machine Learning ch. 7 (CV), 11 (backtest dangers), 12 (CPCV) + Bailey-López de Prado 'Deflated Sharpe Ratio' (SSRN 2460551) and 'Probability of Backtest Overfitting' (SSRN 2326253) — implement DSR/PBO as code, not concept. • Leung & Li, Optimal Mean Reversion Trading (arXiv:1411.5062 + book): derive the cost/stop-loss-aware optimal entry interval and take-profit level to replace arbitrary 2-sigma rules; compare expected value vs the naive threshold rule. • Do & Faff (2010, 2012), 'Does Simple Pairs Trading Still Work?' / 'Are Pairs Trading Profits Robust to Trading Costs?' — the definitive empirical decay study on GGR distance method net of costs; quantify exactly how much of the 11% survives. • Krauss (2017) 'Statistical Arbitrage Pairs Trading Strategies: Review and Outlook' (the ResearchGate review) — the single best literature map of the five method families (distance, cointegration, time-series, stochastic-control, ML) with their relative evidence. • Palomar, Portfolio Optimization (portfoliooptimizationbook.com ch.15) — clean, code-backed treatment of cointegration discovery, half-life, Kalman-filtered hedge ratios, and spurious-cointegration multiple-testing cautions; good basis for our R5 cointegration module. • Modern/regime-aware + ML directions: graph-clustering multi-pair stat-arb (arXiv:2406.10695), regime-switching pairs, and deep/RL stat-arb (arXiv:2403.12180, 2203.03179) — evaluate skeptically as capacity/cost-realistic, not as Sharpe-chasing; the 2026 Harbourfront review is the honest 'what still works' synthesis. • FRBNY SR513 'Decomposing Short-Term Return Reversal' + Nagel (2012) 'Evaporating Liquidity' — to split reversal into liquidity-provision (harvestable) vs overreaction (decayed) and to time reversal exposure with VIX/liquidity conditioning.

---
## factor-zoo (equity cross-sectional factor investing)

**Thesis.** Factors are systematic, diversified exposures that earn a premium for one of two reasons, and the distinction is everything for an autonomous fund: (1) compensated risk — the factor pays you for bearing a risk others won't (CMA/RMW have a q-theory/discount-rate story; HML a distress story; BAB a leverage-constraint story), in which case the premium should NOT decay because nobody is arbitraging away a genuine risk premium; or (2) mispricing — a behavioral or limits-to-arbitrage error (momentum underreaction, low-vol lottery-preference), which CAN and DOES decay once published and traded. McLean-Pontiff (2016) is the load-bearing empirical fact: post-publication, anomaly returns fall ~58% (26% out-of-sample from data-mining + a further ~32% from publication-informed arbitrage), and the decay is LARGER for cheap-to-arbitrage, high-liquidity signals. So the family's edge persists only where (a) the economic story is a real risk premium, (b) the signal is ambiguous enough to resist crowding ("what is cheap?" has many answers; "buy winners" has one — so momentum crowds hardest), or (c) limits-to-arbitrage (microcaps, shorting frictions, capacity) protect it. The honest 2026 posture: treat published single-name equity factors as DECAYED priors, not free money. The surviving edge is in implementation (cost-aware construction, signal combination, factor timing on valuation spreads) and in not fooling yourself (multiple-testing-corrected t-hurdles), not in re-discovering value or momentum.

**Concrete strategies:**
- **Value (HML / book-to-market) — and its modern repairs**
  - *Signal:* Long high book-to-market (cheap) minus short low B/M (expensive), value-weighted, within size groups. Construction (Fama-French): at end of June, sort on B/M = book equity (fiscal year t-1) / market equity (Dec t-1); form 2x3 size x B/M portfolios on NYSE breakpoints (size: NYSE median; B/M: 30th/70th NYSE percentiles). HML = avg(SmallValue, BigValue) - avg(SmallGrowth, BigGrowth). Rebalance annually. Modern repairs to address the 2007-2020 drawdown: (1) intangibles-adjusted book value (capitalize R&D + SG&A) per Israel-Laursen-Richardson; (2) composite value (B/M + earnings/price + sales/price + cashflow/price) to reduce accounting noise; (3) value within-industry to neutralize sector bets.
  - *Params:* B/M breakpoints 30/70 NYSE; size split at NYSE median; annual June rebalance; 6-month accounting lag (June t uses fiscal-year t-1 book). Composite-value: equal-weight z-scores of 4-5 valuation ratios. Intangibles: capitalize R&D over ~3-5yr, ~20%/yr of SG&A.  ·  *Assets:* US + international developed equities (well-replicated across 23+ countries); weaker/absent in some EM. Also works cross-asset (value in currencies, bonds, commodities — AQR 'Value and Momentum Everywhere').
  - *Evidence (honest):* In-sample (FF 1993): HML ~0.40%/mo, strongly significant. HONEST FLAG: in FF5 (2015) HML is statistically REDUNDANT (its return absorbed by RMW+CMA). HONEST FLAG: HML suffered its longest/deepest drawdown in history 2007-2020 (~55% as of mid-2020). Israel-Laursen-Richardson (AQR 2020) attribute the entire drawdown to a widening value-growth valuation spread (i.e. value got cheaper, not broken) + intangibles mismeasurement, not premium death. Net-of-cost: value is LOW turnover, so it survives costs better than momentum. Verdict: not dead, but plain B/M HML is the weakest classic factor; use composite/intangibles-adjusted value.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Crowds LEAST among classic factors (the alpha-decay literature finds value/quality/investment have minimal hyperbolic-decay fits, R-sq<0.10) because 'what is cheap?' is an ambiguous signal with many definitions — hard to crowd a signal nobody agrees on. Low turnover = high capacity. The risk is not crowding but regime (long growth-led bull markets).
- **Momentum (UMD / WML, 12-1)**
  - *Signal:* Rank stocks on cumulative return over months t-12 to t-2 (SKIP the most recent month t-1 to avoid short-term reversal/microstructure bounce). Long top decile/tercile, short bottom. Carhart UMD construction: 2x3 size x prior-return sort, UMD = avg(SmallHigh,BigHigh) - avg(SmallLow,BigLow). Rebalance monthly. Variants: residual/idiosyncratic momentum (momentum on FF-residual returns) has higher Sharpe and lower crash risk; factor momentum (time-series momentum on factor returns) per Ehsani-Linnainmaa.
  - *Params:* Formation 12-1 (skip 1 month); holding 1 month with monthly rebalance OR overlapping portfolios; deciles on NYSE breakpoints. For 52-week-high variant: proximity to 52wk high. Short-term reversal: 1-month, separate signal.  ·  *Assets:* US + international + cross-asset (one of the most universal factors — 'Value and Momentum Everywhere'). Weak/negative in Japan equities (the famous exception).
  - *Evidence (honest):* In-sample (Jegadeesh-Titman 1993): ~1.49%/mo gross, t very high. HONEST FLAG: highest GROSS Sharpe of the classic factors but also highest TURNOVER (~monthly, ~200%+ annual), so net-of-cost returns are dramatically lower; transaction-cost-aware studies (Novy-Marx-Velikov, Frazzini-Israel-Moskowitz) show momentum's net premium is much smaller than gross but survives at institutional scale with smart trading. HONEST FLAG: subject to rare violent 'momentum crashes' (2009 rebound: short leg of losers rockets). Negatively skewed.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Crowds HARDEST. The alpha-decay paper finds momentum has the strongest hyperbolic decay fit (R-sq 0.65); returns fell from ~10%/yr (1990s) to ~2% recently, correlated with momentum-ETF volume growth. Reason: 'buy recent winners' is an UNAMBIGUOUS signal everyone computes identically — maximally crowdable. Mitigant (counterintuitive): crowded momentum showed LOWER crash risk (0.38x) in the crowding study. Treat momentum as a decayed, capacity-limited, cost-sensitive factor — the opposite of value.
- **Profitability (RMW / gross-profitability / ROE)**
  - *Signal:* Three flavors, increasingly refined: (a) Novy-Marx gross profitability = (REVT - COGS)/AT (gross profits to total ASSETS, not equity — the denominator is the innovation; 'cleanest' accounting profitability, least polluted by SG&A/accruals). (b) FF5 RMW operating profitability = (revenues - COGS - SG&A - interest expense)/book equity. (c) q-factor ROE = income before extraordinary items / one-quarter-lagged book equity, updated MONTHLY with the latest quarterly earnings (this monthly update is why the q-ROE factor captures much of momentum). Long robust/high, short weak/low.
  - *Params:* Novy-Marx: GP/AT, annual. FF RMW: 2x3 size x OP, 30/70 breakpoints, annual June. q ROE: 2x3x3 sort, MONTHLY rebalance using most recent quarterly earnings announcement; this update frequency is load-bearing.  ·  *Assets:* US + international developed (Novy-Marx international evidence positive). Strong in large/liquid stocks (rare for a factor — most live in small caps).
  - *Evidence (honest):* In-sample: q-factor ROE ~0.58%/mo, t~4.8 (one of the most robust t-stats in the zoo). Novy-Marx GP/AT has 'roughly the same power as book-to-market.' HONEST FLAG: profitability is one of the BEST-replicating factors (survives HXZ-2020 and Chen-Zimmermann), works in LARGE caps (so capacity is high), and has a clean q-theory mechanism — this is arguably the highest-conviction factor in the zoo. Controlling for profitability also strengthens value (they're negatively correlated, so combine them).
  - *Decay/crowding:* Crowds LITTLE (ambiguous signal — many profitability definitions; the alpha-decay study groups it with value/quality as low-decay). Main risk: definition sensitivity (gross vs operating vs ROE give different portfolios). Robust enough that FF added it to their own model.
- **Investment / Asset Growth (CMA / I/A)**
  - *Signal:* Long low-investment (conservative) minus short high-investment (aggressive) firms. FF CMA investment variable = growth in total assets: (AT_t - AT_{t-1})/AT_{t-1}. q-factor I/A = annual change in total assets / lagged total assets (same idea). Economic story (q-theory): firms invest heavily when their cost of capital (discount rate) is LOW, so high-investment firms have low expected returns by construction — this is a discount-rate factor, not pure mispricing. Closely related: Cooper-Gulen-Schill asset-growth anomaly, accruals, net-share-issuance (all 'investment/financing' family).
  - *Params:* CMA: 2x3 size x Inv, 30/70 NYSE breakpoints, annual June rebalance, total-asset growth over prior fiscal year. I/A (q): same annual measure.  ·  *Assets:* US + international developed. Concentrated in smaller firms but present in large.
  - *Evidence (honest):* In-sample: both FF CMA and q I/A are significant; the investment factor is central to q-theory's success at subsuming anomalies. HONEST FLAG: highly correlated with value (low-investment firms tend to be value firms) — much of HML's redundancy in FF5 is because CMA captures the same thing. HONEST FLAG: weaker as a standalone net-of-cost trade; best used as part of a multi-factor blend, not alone.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Low crowding (ambiguous, low-turnover, accounting-based). The risk is overlap/collinearity with value and profitability, not crowding. In a combined model it adds modest incremental breadth.
- **Quality (QMJ)**
  - *Signal:* Composite z-score of four pillars, each itself a composite, derived from a Gordon-growth valuation identity (price should rise with quality): PROFITABILITY (gross profits/assets, ROE, ROA, gross margin, accruals-quality/low-accruals), GROWTH (trailing 5yr growth in those profitability measures), SAFETY (low beta, low leverage, low bankruptcy risk/Ohlson-O, low ROE volatility), PAYOUT (net equity+debt issuance, payout ratio — rewards shareholder-friendly capital discipline). Rank stocks by composite quality; long top 30%, short bottom 30%, within size groups (6 value-weighted size x quality portfolios). QMJ = avg of the two high-quality minus avg of the two junk.
  - *Params:* Equal-weight the four pillar z-scores; each pillar is the equal-weight z-score of its components; cross-sectional ranking; 30/70 within large and within small separately; monthly or quarterly rebalance.  ·  *Assets:* US + 24 countries (AQR maintains downloadable QMJ factors globally). Works in large caps.
  - *Evidence (honest):* In-sample: significant risk-adjusted (alpha) returns in US and 24 countries; NEGATIVE market beta (does well in recessions/crises) — a genuine portfolio diversifier, not just another long-only beta. HONEST FLAG: 'quality' is the most definitionally elastic factor — easy to data-mine a good backtest by picking components ex-post. Use the AQR-published definition, don't reinvent. HONEST FLAG: overlaps heavily with profitability and low-vol; check incremental alpha after controlling for RMW + BAB.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Low-to-moderate crowding (ambiguous composite). The 'flight-to-quality in crises' property is structural (defensive), unlikely to fully arbitrage away. Main risk is that its premium is partly a relabel of profitability + safety you already own.
- **Low-Risk / Betting-Against-Beta (BAB) and low-volatility**
  - *Signal:* Two cousins. BAB (Frazzini-Pedersen): rank all stocks by estimated beta, form a long low-beta / short high-beta portfolio, then LEVER the low-beta leg up to beta 1 and DE-LEVER the high-beta leg down to beta 1 so the portfolio is ex-ante market-neutral and the bet is purely on the beta-return relationship being too flat (CAPM failure). Beta estimated from 1-yr daily (vol) + 5-yr (correlation), shrunk toward 1. Low-volatility (simpler cousin): just long low-realized-vol or min-variance, long-only, no shorting/leverage. Mechanism: leverage-constrained investors overpay for high-beta lottery exposure, so low-beta is underpriced.
  - *Params:* BAB: rank-weighted (not value-weighted) within high/low groups; beta shrinkage (Vasicek/0.6*beta+0.4*1); monthly rebalance; the leverage step is essential and is also the cost/turnover problem. Low-vol: 12-36 month realized vol or covariance-matrix min-var, quarterly rebalance.  ·  *Assets:* US + global equities + cross-asset (BAB shown in 20 markets, bonds, credit, futures). Robust internationally.
  - *Evidence (honest):* In-sample (FP 2014): BAB Sharpe ~0.78 (1926-2012), significant after controlling for mkt/size/value/momentum/liquidity. HONEST FLAG — this is the most CONTESTED construction in the zoo: Novy-Marx-Velikov 'Betting Against Betting Against Beta' (2018) shows BAB's premium depends on (a) the non-standard RANK-weighting (overweights microcaps), (b) the leverage application, and (c) is heavily eroded by the high turnover of beta-rebalancing — net of realistic costs much of it disappears. Low-vol long-only is more robust net-of-cost (low turnover) but is essentially a defensive/quality+rates bet and underperforms in rate-rising regimes.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Moderate-to-high crowding concern — low-vol/min-vol ETFs took huge AUM 2012-2018, pushing low-vol stocks to rich valuations (Arnott's 'factor valuation' warning). Watch the valuation spread of the low-vol leg; when low-vol is expensive vs history, the premium is compromised. BAB specifically also carries funding/leverage risk in drawdowns (the leverage that creates the premium amplifies losses).
- **Size (SMB) — included for honesty, not conviction**
  - *Signal:* Long small-cap minus short large-cap, value-weighted within. FF SMB = avg of the three small-stock portfolios (from B/M, OP, Inv sorts) minus avg of the three big-stock portfolios. Mechanism originally framed as a risk premium for illiquidity/distress.
  - *Params:* NYSE-median size breakpoint; annual June rebalance; the modern fix (Asness et al. 'Size Matters If You Control Your Junk') is to run size NEUTRALIZED for quality — raw small-caps are full of junk that masks the size premium.  ·  *Assets:* US + international, but weakest and least stable of the classics.
  - *Evidence (honest):* HONEST FLAG — size is the WEAKEST classic factor: the standalone small-firm premium largely VANISHED out-of-sample after Banz (1981), is concentrated in January and microcaps, and is not robust in HXZ-2020. It revives mainly as an interaction (size matters once you control for quality/junk) or as a beta you get for free, not an alpha. Do NOT build a fund on standalone size.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Effectively decayed/never-robust as a standalone. Its main role now is as the conditioning variable that makes OTHER factors (especially profitability, value) stronger in the small-cap segment — and as a capacity/liquidity constraint to respect, not a signal to chase.

**Canonical sources:**
- *Common Risk Factors in the Returns on Stocks and Bonds (the 3-factor model: Mkt, SMB, HML)* — Eugene F. Fama, Kenneth R. French (1993, paper): Founding document of the entire factor-investing field. Established the 2x6 size/B-M sort methodology, NYSE breakpoints, value-weighting, and the long-short mimicking-portfolio construction that every later factor copies. The data library (mba.tuck.dartmouth.edu/pages/faculty/ken.french) is the canonical benchmark dataset against which any in-house factor must be sanity-checked. — https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-405X(93)90023-5
- *On Persistence in Mutual Fund Performance (the Carhart 4-factor model: adds UMD/momentum)* — Mark M. Carhart (1997, paper): Canonized momentum (Jegadeesh-Titman 1993) as a fourth factor (UMD/WML) and made the 4-factor model the workhorse benchmark for fund-performance attribution for ~20 years. If you can't beat Carhart-4 alpha, your 'edge' is just known factor exposure. — https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1540-6261.1997.tb03808.x
- *A Five-Factor Asset Pricing Model (adds RMW profitability, CMA investment)* — Eugene F. Fama, Kenneth R. French (2015, paper): Added profitability (RMW) and investment (CMA) motivated by the dividend-discount/valuation identity. Critically, the authors themselves found HML becomes REDUNDANT in the 5-factor model (its return is absorbed by RMW+CMA loadings) — a rare case of authors demoting their own famous factor. Also notable: the model still fails to price small stocks that invest aggressively despite low profitability. — https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jfineco.2014.10.010
- *Digesting Anomalies: An Investment Approach (the q-factor model)* — Kewei Hou, Chen Xue, Lu Zhang (2015, paper): The strongest competitor to FF5. A 4-factor model (Mkt, ME size, I/A investment, ROE profitability) grounded in neoclassical q-theory of investment, NOT a fishing expedition. Empirically subsumes a large swath of the anomaly zoo (including momentum, via the ROE factor) better than FF3/Carhart. Later augmented to q5 (adds expected-growth Eg). Maintained as a live, downloadable factor library at global-q.org — practitioner-grade. — https://global-q.org/uploads/1/2/2/6/122679606/houxuezhang2015rfs.pdf
- *...and the Cross-Section of Expected Returns* — Campbell R. Harvey, Yan Liu, Heqing Zhu (2016, paper): The multiple-testing reckoning. Catalogued 316+ published factors and argued that with so many tests, the conventional t>2.0 hurdle produces rampant false discoveries; the multiple-testing-adjusted hurdle is t>3.0 (rising over time as more factors are tested). This is the single most important methodological constraint on a factor fund: a 'significant' single-test t-stat near 2 is, in expectation, noise. Companion: Harvey-Liu (2020) 'A Census of the Factor Zoo'. — https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/hhv059
- *Does Academic Research Destroy Stock Return Predictability?* — R. David McLean, Jeffrey Pontiff (2016, paper): The decay/crowding bible. Studied 97 published predictors: returns are ~26% lower out-of-sample and ~58% lower post-publication. The ~32% gap between OOS and post-pub decay is the 'arbitrage learns from publications' effect. Decay is larger for cheap-to-arbitrage, liquid, low-idio-risk signals. Mandatory prior for any fund deploying a published factor: assume the live premium is roughly half the paper's. — https://doi.org/10.1111/jofi.12365
- *Replicating Anomalies* — Kewei Hou, Chen Xue, Lu Zhang (2020, paper): The pessimistic replication audit. Of 452 anomalies, 65% fail to clear even t>1.96 once microcaps are controlled (NYSE breakpoints + value-weighting); 96% of the 'trading frictions' category fail; at the t>2.78 multiple-testing hurdle, 82% fail. The core methodological lesson: equal-weighting and including microcaps (which are ~3% of market cap but ~60% of the stock count) inflates anomaly t-stats — most of the 'zoo' is a microcap artifact. — https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w23394/w23394.pdf
- *Open Source Cross-Sectional Asset Pricing* — Andrew Y. Chen, Tom Zimmermann (2022, repo): The optimistic counter-replication AND the most useful practical asset here. Replicated 200+ (319 catalogued) published signals with open code/data; for clearly-significant originals, 98% replicate at t>1.96, with a 0.90 slope and 83% R-squared vs original t-stats. Resolves the HXZ-pessimism vs Chen-optimism debate: anomalies DO replicate in-sample, but the disagreement is about microcap weighting and post-pub decay. The downloadable signal+portfolio data is a turnkey R4/R5 input — do not rebuild this from scratch. — https://www.openassetpricing.com/
- *Quality Minus Junk (QMJ)* — Clifford S. Asness, Andrea Frazzini, Lasse H. Pedersen (2019, paper): Operationalizes 'quality' rigorously from a Gordon-growth valuation identity: profitability + growth + safety + payout. Practitioner-grade (AQR maintains the QMJ factor dataset for 24 countries, downloadable). QMJ has a negative market beta and does well in crises — a genuine diversifier. Pairs with Novy-Marx (2013) gross-profitability as the academic root. — https://www.aqr.com/Insights/Research/Working-Paper/Quality-Minus-Junk
- *Betting Against Beta (BAB)* — Andrea Frazzini, Lasse H. Pedersen (2014, paper): The canonical low-risk/low-vol factor with an actual economic mechanism (funding/leverage constraints, not just mispricing). Beta-neutral construction (lever up low-beta leg to beta 1, de-lever high-beta leg to beta 1) is a distinctive recipe. BUT it is also the most-attacked factor: Novy-Marx-Velikov (2018) 'Betting Against Betting Against Beta' shows it relies on the specific rank-weighting and microcap exposure, with high turnover that erodes net returns. Study both papers together. — https://pages.stern.nyu.edu/~lpederse/papers/BettingAgainstBeta.pdf
- *Active Portfolio Management / Quantitative Equity Portfolio Management* — Grinold & Kahn; Qian, Hua & Sorensen (2000 / 2007, book): The implementation bridge. Grinold-Kahn gives the Fundamental Law of Active Management (IR ~ IC x sqrt(breadth)), factor-risk-model construction, and transfer coefficient — the machinery for turning a factor signal into a risk-managed portfolio (your R-portfolio/risk layers). Qian-Hua-Sorensen covers signal-combination, IC decay, and the gap between paper alpha and net alpha. These are the books that connect 'a factor exists' to 'we can trade it.' — https://www.mhprofessional.com/active-portfolio-management-9780070248823-usa
- *The Deflated Sharpe Ratio: Correcting for Selection Bias, Backtest Overfitting and Non-Normality* — David H. Bailey, Marcos Lopez de Prado (2014, paper): The anti-self-deception tool for the R3/validation layer. The Deflated Sharpe Ratio adjusts an observed Sharpe for the NUMBER of trials run, the variance of those trials' Sharpes, sample length, skew and kurtosis — directly operationalizing the Harvey-Liu-Zhu multiple-testing critique at the strategy level. Plus 'Minimum Backtest Length' (the more independent strategies you try, the longer your backtest must be to avoid selecting an overfit). This IS the anti-Profit-Mirage method. — https://www.davidhbailey.com/dhbpapers/deflated-sharpe.pdf

**Pitfalls:** (1) MICROCAP CONTAMINATION is the #1 zoo artifact. Microcaps are ~3% of total market cap but ~60% of the NYSE+AMEX+NASDAQ stock COUNT. Equal-weighting and including microcaps inflates anomaly t-stats massively. HXZ-2020 showed 65% of 452 anomalies fail t>1.96 once you use NYSE breakpoints + value-weighting. ALWAYS use NYSE breakpoints (not all-stock breakpoints) and value-weight (or cap the microcap allocation). If your backtest shines only equal-weighted, you have a microcap mirage, not an edge. (2) SINGLE-TEST t-STATISTICS LIE. Harvey-Liu-Zhu: with 300+ factors tested, the multiple-testing-adjusted hurdle is t>3.0 (and rising), not t>2.0. A factor at t=2.1 is, in expectation, a false discovery. Your validation layer must apply a multiple-testing correction (BHY/FDR or the Deflated Sharpe Ratio) that accounts for HOW MANY signals you tried — including the ones you discarded. The trials you don't report are exactly what inflates the survivor. (3) ASSUME ~50% POST-PUBLICATION DECAY ON ANY PUBLISHED FACTOR (McLean-Pontiff: ~58% lower post-pub, ~26% lower even out-of-sample pre-pub). The premium in the paper is NOT the premium you'll earn. Decay is WORSE for cheap-to-arbitrage, liquid, low-idio-vol signals (i.e. exactly the capacity-friendly ones you'd want to scale). Build the decay haircut into the prior, not as an afterthought. (4) GROSS-vs-NET IS THE WHOLE GAME for high-turnover factors. Momentum and BAB have spectacular GROSS Sharpes and crippling turnover (momentum ~200%+/yr, BAB beta-rebalancing similar). Net of realistic transaction costs + market impact at scale, much of the premium evaporates (Novy-Marx-Velikov; Frazzini-Israel-Moskowitz 'Trading Costs'). Any factor backtest without explicit, scale-dependent cost modeling is a Profit Mirage. Value/profitability/investment are low-turnover and survive costs far better — turnover is a first-class factor-selection criterion. (5) FACTOR REDUNDANCY / COLLINEARITY: HML is redundant in FF5 (absorbed by RMW+CMA). Value, investment, and profitability are mutually entangled. Stacking 'value + quality + investment + low-vol' often buys far less diversification than the names suggest because the long/short legs overlap. Always run a spanning regression: regress each candidate factor on the others and keep only those with significant ALPHA (incremental), not significant raw return. (6) DATA-SNOOPED CONSTRUCTION CHOICES: breakpoints (30/70 vs deciles vs quintiles), weighting (value vs equal vs rank), formation lag, rebalance frequency, winsorization, the exact accounting definition — each is a researcher degree of freedom. The same 'factor' can be significant or not depending on these. Multiverse/specification-curve analysis (run ALL reasonable specs, report the distribution) is the honest defense; a single hand-picked spec that 'works' is overfit. (7) LOOK-AHEAD / POINT-IN-TIME BIAS: Compustat data is restated; using restated fundamentals or insufficient reporting lag (FF use a 6-month lag for annual, the q-model is careful about quarterly-earnings announcement dates) injects future information. Survivorship bias (dropping delisted/bankrupt firms) and delisting-return omission (especially for the short legs of distress/low-quality factors) systematically inflate long-short returns. You MUST use point-in-time data with correct delisting returns. (8) SHORT-SIDE INFEASIBILITY: many zoo 'long-short' premia live disproportionately in the SHORT leg (overpriced junk/lottery/high-investment stocks), which is exactly where shorting is hard/expensive (high borrow cost, recall risk, hard-to-borrow microcaps). A long-short paper Sharpe is often unattainable as a tradeable strategy. Decompose long-leg vs short-leg alpha; for a real fund, a factor whose alpha is mostly on the unshortable short side is far less valuable than the headline number. (9) FACTOR VALUATION / CROWDING TIMING RISK: Arnott (Research Affiliates) warns that much of a factor's recent return can come from the factor getting EXPENSIVE (valuation spread compressing), which is borrowed from future returns. A factor trading rich relative to its own history (e.g. low-vol post-2016) is a worse forward bet. BUT factor-timing on valuation is itself fragile and easily overfit (Asness pushback) — use it as a risk overlay/de-risking signal, not an aggressive timing engine. (10) REGIME DEPENDENCE & LONG DRAWDOWNS ARE NORMAL, NOT BROKEN: value had a ~13-year, ~55% drawdown (2007-2020) that looked like death and then partially reversed. Factors require multi-year patience and a pre-committed conviction framework, or you will capitulate at the bottom and harvest only the losses. The autonomous fund needs a rule for distinguishing 'temporary valuation-spread widening' (hold/add) from 'genuine structural decay' (cut) — and that rule must be set BEFORE the drawdown, not during it.

**Honest validation:** (1) MULTIPLE-TESTING-CORRECTED SIGNIFICANCE (the anti-Profit-Mirage core): never accept a factor on a single-test t>2. Apply EITHER (a) the Harvey-Liu-Zhu / Benjamini-Hochberg-Yekutieli FDR adjustment using a realistic count of how many signals/specs you actually tried, with a hurdle of t>3.0 as the default; OR (b) the Deflated Sharpe Ratio (Bailey-Lopez de Prado), which deflates the observed Sharpe by the number of trials, the variance of trial Sharpes, sample length, skew, and kurtosis. Track N_trials honestly in the R3 evidence layer — the discarded specs are the multiplier. (2) PURGED, EMBARGOED WALK-FORWARD / COMBINATORIAL CROSS-VALIDATION (Lopez de Prado 'Advances in Financial ML'): standard k-fold CV leaks because of overlapping labels and serial correlation. Use purged k-fold + embargo (drop train observations whose label window overlaps the test set; embargo a buffer after each test fold). Report the distribution of out-of-sample Sharpes across CV paths, not a single backtest number. Also compute Minimum Backtest Length: given how many strategies you tried, is your sample even long enough to distinguish skill from luck? (3) TRUE OUT-OF-SAMPLE & POST-PUBLICATION TEST: split the sample at the factor's PUBLICATION date (per McLean-Pontiff) and at a geographic boundary (US-discovered factor tested in international markets is a quasi-OOS test). Apply a default ~50% haircut to the in-sample premium as the working prior for live expected return. A factor that doesn't survive its own post-publication / out-of-country sample is a museum piece, not a live edge. (4) NET-OF-COST, CAPACITY-AWARE BACKTEST: model transaction costs as a function of turnover, spread, and (critically) market impact that SCALES with AUM — not a flat bps assumption. Re-rank all candidate factors by NET Sharpe at the fund's target capital, not gross. Run the backtest at multiple AUM levels to find the capacity cliff. This is where momentum/BAB get demoted and value/profitability get promoted. Use the Chen-Zimmermann or Frazzini-Israel-Moskowitz cost frameworks as templates. (5) SPANNING / INCREMENTAL-ALPHA REGRESSIONS: before adding any factor to the book, regress it on the factors you already hold (FF5 + UMD + QMJ + BAB as the baseline). Keep it ONLY if the intercept (alpha) is significant after multiple-testing correction — significant raw return is not enough. This kills redundancy (e.g. you'll discover your 'new value signal' is just CMA+RMW, à la HML's redundancy in FF5). (6) SPECIFICATION-CURVE / MULTIVERSE ANALYSIS: instead of one hand-picked construction, run the factor across the full grid of reasonable choices (breakpoints, weighting, lag, rebalance, definition) and report the DISTRIBUTION of Sharpes/t-stats. A robust factor is significant across most of the multiverse; a fragile one is significant only in a thin slice. This directly defends against researcher-degree-of-freedom overfitting and is more honest than any single backtest. (7) BENCHMARK AGAINST THE RIGHT MODEL: a 'new' factor's alpha must be tested against q-factor and FF5+momentum, not just CAPM or FF3. Many 'discoveries' are alpha vs CAPM but zero alpha vs q5. Use the global-q.org and Ken French factor data as the null model. If the factor has no alpha against the strongest existing model, it is not new edge. (8) PRE-REGISTER THE HYPOTHESIS AND THE KILL-RULE: write down the economic mechanism (risk premium vs mispricing) and the expected Sharpe BEFORE the backtest; and pre-commit the live monitoring rule that will retire the factor (e.g. rolling Sharpe below threshold over N years AND valuation spread no longer cheap = decayed, cut). This prevents both ex-post storytelling and drawdown-bottom capitulation.

**Data:** "CORE: (1) CRSP-equivalent point-in-time daily/monthly equity returns WITH delisting returns (omitting these biases distress/low-quality short legs upward). (2) Compustat-equivalent point-in-time fundamentals (restated data injects look-ahead) — annual + QUARTERLY (the q-ROE factor and any earnings-momentum need quarterly with announcement dates). Required line items: total assets (AT), book equity (CEQ + adjustments), revenues (REVT/SALE), COGS, SG&A (XSGA), interest expense (XINT), income before extraordinary items (IB), shares outstanding, net issuance, dividends. (3) Market equity at the right dates (Dec t-1 for B/M, June t for size). (4) NYSE-only breakpoint computation (must identify exchange to compute NYSE-median/30-70-percentile breakpoints — this is the single most important data discipline against the microcap mirage). TURNKEY SHORTCUTS (do NOT rebuild from scratch — R4 layer): Ken French data library (FF5+UMD factors + 25/100 sorted portfolios, free); global-q.org (q-factor + q5 factors + 18 benchmark portfolios, free, practitioner-maintained); AQR datasets (QMJ, BAB, value/momentum-everywhere, free); Chen-Zimmermann Open Source Asset Pricing (200+ replicated signals + portfolios + code, free, the single best starting corpus). FOR LIVE TRADING (beyond the academic factors): borrow-cost/short-availability data (to assess short-leg feasibility), bid-ask spreads + ADV for cost/capacity modeling, corporate-action-adjusted prices, and a survivorship-bias-free universe. SAMPLE LENGTH: 1963-present is the academic standard (pre-1963 Compustat is sparse/biased); a meaningful factor backtest needs decades, and Minimum-Backtest-Length math says the more signals you screen, the longer the required clean sample."

**Fits our stack:** "R3 (evidence): This family is where multiple-testing discipline becomes mandatory infrastructure. R3 must enforce a t>3.0 (Harvey-Liu-Zhu) / Deflated-Sharpe gate, log N_trials (including discarded specs) so the deflation is honest, and store the post-publication-decay prior (~50% haircut on any published factor) as a default belief. The economic-mechanism classification (risk premium = durable vs mispricing = decaying) should be a required R3 field per signal. R4 (data): Do NOT rebuild — ingest Ken French + global-q + AQR + Chen-Zimmermann Open Source Asset Pricing as turnkey factor/signal feeds; the in-house build is only the point-in-time CRSP/Compustat pipeline with NYSE breakpoints + delisting returns + value-weighting (the three artifact-killers). R5 (alpha): the deployable signals, ranked by NET-of-cost robustness, are profitability (RMW/GP-to-assets/ROE — highest conviction, large-cap, low decay), composite/intangibles-adjusted value (low turnover, low crowding), and quality (QMJ, defensive diversifier) — with momentum and BAB flagged as high-Sharpe-but-high-turnover-and-crowded (deploy only with serious cost/capacity modeling). Combine via z-score blending + spanning regressions to strip redundancy, not naive factor stacking. RISK: factor-risk-model (Grinold-Kahn / Barra-style) for ex-ante factor exposures and covariance; watch factor valuation spreads (Arnott crowding signal) and multi-year drawdown tolerance as explicit risk parameters. PORTFOLIO: Fundamental Law (IR = IC*sqrt(breadth)) sizing; neutralize unintended bets (sector, size) so you isolate the intended factor; respect capacity cliffs per factor. EXECUTION: turnover is a first-class selection criterion — the gross-to-net gap is where most zoo factors die; cost-aware rebalancing (no-trade bands, crossing/netting across factors) is what preserves the surviving edge. The decay-aware kill-rule (retire a factor when rolling net-Sharpe AND valuation spread both signal structural decay) is a portfolio-governance primitive this family demands."

**Go deeper:** • Harvey & Liu (2020) 'A Census of the Factor Zoo' (SSRN 3341728) + Harvey-Liu-Zhu (2016) full RFS paper — read for the exact multiple-testing math (Bonferroni/Holm/BHY hurdles over time) before building the R3 significance gate. • Lopez de Prado, 'Advances in Financial Machine Learning' (2018) — Ch. on purged/embargoed cross-validation, combinatorial-purged CV, Deflated Sharpe Ratio, and the False Strategy Theorem. This is the implementation manual for the anti-Profit-Mirage validation layer; pair with the Bailey-Lopez de Prado DSR paper. • Hou, Mo, Xue, Zhang (2021) 'An Augmented q-Factor Model with Expected Growth' (q5) at global-q.org — the expected-growth factor (Eg) and why q5 beats q4 and FF5 on the broadest anomaly set; download the factor data and benchmark your signals against it as the null model. • Asness, Frazzini, Israel, Moskowitz (2014-2015) 'Fact, Fiction and Momentum/Value Investing' + 'Trading Costs' (AQR) — the practitioner rebuttal on net-of-cost feasibility and capacity; the most honest gross-vs-net treatment from people who actually trade these at scale. • Novy-Marx & Velikov (2016) 'A Taxonomy of Anomalies and Their Trading Costs' (RFS) + (2018) 'Betting Against Betting Against Beta' — the definitive net-of-cost ranking of the zoo and the BAB deconstruction; essential before deploying any high-turnover factor. • Arnott, Beck, Kalesnik, West (Research Affiliates) 'How Can Smart Beta Go Horribly Wrong?' + 'Forecasting Factor and Smart Beta Returns' — the factor-valuation/crowding-timing case (and Asness's 'The Long Run Is Lying to You' as the rebuttal); read both to calibrate the crowding-vs-cheapness risk overlay. • Israel, Laursen, Richardson (2020) 'Is (Systematic) Value Investing Dead?' (SSRN 3554267) — the intangibles-adjusted value repair and valuation-spread decomposition; the template for modernizing book-to-market and for the 'temporary drawdown vs structural death' kill-rule logic. • Chen & Zimmermann (2022) Open Source Asset Pricing (openassetpricing.com) — clone the repo; it is the fastest path to 200+ replicated signals with portfolios and is the empirical referee in the HXZ-pessimism vs Chen-optimism replication debate. Read their 'Publication Bias and the Cross-Section of Stock Returns' (2020, RFS) for the optimistic side of the decay argument. • Grinold & Kahn 'Active Portfolio Management' (Ch. on Fundamental Law, factor risk models, transfer coefficient) + Qian, Hua & Sorensen 'Quantitative Equity Portfolio Management' — the bridge from 'a factor exists' to 'a risk-managed, cost-aware, capacity-respecting book', i.e. the R5->risk->portfolio->execution path for this whole family. • Cochrane (2011 AFA Presidential Address) 'Discount Rates' — the unifying theoretical frame: every factor premium is a statement about discount-rate variation; read it to keep the risk-premium-vs-mispricing classification (the durability question) economically grounded rather than purely statistical.

---
## carry

**Thesis.** Carry is the return you earn if prices do not move — the ex-ante, model-free yield embedded in an asset's term structure. Koijen-Moskowitz-Pedersen-Vrugt (2018) formalize it as a single primitive across ALL asset classes: E[return] = carry + E[price appreciation], where carry = (S - F)/F for a fully-collateralized futures (income/yield differential per period). Economically, carry is compensation for bearing a state-contingent risk: the carry trade is short a "disaster" or funding-liquidity factor. You collect a small, steady premium in normal times and pay it back violently in coordinated risk-off events (negative skew, fat left tail). The premium persists because (a) it is genuine risk-bearing compensation — high-carry assets crash precisely when marginal utility is highest (recessions, liquidity crunches, vol spikes), so it is NOT a free lunch and can't be fully arbitraged; (b) it embeds institutional/behavioral frictions (UIP failure, hedging pressure of commodity producers, structural demand for portfolio insurance that depresses the price of variance). The same negative skew that compensates the risk-premium story also makes carry vulnerable to crowding-amplified unwinds ("up by the stairs, down by the elevator"). Where carry decays, it is typically because the underlying risk compensation shrank (rate differentials compressed post-GFC, vol-selling got crowded into levered ETPs) rather than because the structure was spurious. Net: carry is one of the most economically grounded premia in the factor zoo, but it is the WORST-behaved on tail risk and the most regime-dependent, so naive Sharpe ratios massively overstate the achievable risk-adjusted return.

**Concrete strategies:**
- **FX carry (cross-sectional HML-FX)**
  - *Signal:* Rank ~10-30 liquid currencies by forward discount (equivalently interest-rate differential vs USD; by covered interest parity F/S-1 = i_foreign - i_domestic). Carry_i = (S_i - F_i)/F_i ~ forward discount. Go long the top-tercile/quintile high-yielders, short the bottom low-yielders, dollar-neutral, vol-scaled. This is Lustig-Roussanov-Verdelhan HML-FX.
  - *Params:* Universe: G10 (developed-only is cleaner) or G10+EM (higher return, higher crash). Rebalance monthly. Forward tenor 1M. 5-6 portfolios for the cross-section; tercile L/S for a tradable book. Vol-target the book (e.g. 10% annualized) and consider scaling DOWN exposure when global FX vol / VIX is high (Menkhoff et al. risk signal). Risk-managing on a vol or drawdown trigger materially improves the tail.  ·  *Assets:* FX (developed G10 forwards; EM forwards/NDFs for the high-octane version)
  - *Evidence (honest):* Pre-GFC gross Sharpe roughly 0.6-0.9 (KMPV report FX carry ~mid-pack; LRV/Menkhoff document the spread). Net-of-cost in liquid G10 forwards: most of the gross survives (forwards are among the most liquid instruments). BUT: returns are strongly negatively skewed; post-2008 performance collapsed — a leveraged carry portfolio fell ~5.4%/yr between pre- and post-GFC periods (Accominotti et al / ScienceDirect 2021). Honest read: real but decayed and tail-heavy.
  - *Decay/crowding:* SUBSTANTIAL DECAY. Crowdedness peaked ~31% (Mar 2008) just before the unwind. Post-GFC: compressed rate differentials (ZIRP everywhere), suppressed FX vol, and crowding all eroded the premium. G10-only carry has been roughly flat-to-negative for stretches of the 2010s. EM carry retains more premium but more crash risk. Treat as a regime-conditional, vol-managed allocation, NOT a standalone return engine.
- **Commodity carry / basis (roll-yield sort)**
  - *Signal:* For each commodity compute the basis/annualized roll: Carry = (F_near - F_deferred)/F_deferred / (time gap), i.e. slope of the futures curve. Backwardation (downward-sloping, near>far) = positive carry; contango = negative carry. Long the most-backwardated, short the most-contangoed, equal-weight, hold 1 month. Economically tied to inventories/convenience yield (theory of storage).
  - *Params:* Universe: ~24-30 liquid commodities across energy/metals/ags. Signal: front-to-next basis annualized (or a smoothed multi-point curve slope to avoid expiry noise). Rebalance monthly; roll futures on a defined schedule to avoid delivery. Equal-weight long/short legs. Sector-neutralize (don't let energy dominate). Vol-scale.  ·  *Assets:* Commodity futures (WTI/Brent, nat gas, gold/copper/aluminum, corn/wheat/soy/sugar, etc.)
  - *Evidence (honest):* Basis-sorted HML spot premia ~5-14%/yr (Szymanowska 2014); basis/carry is one of three robust commodity factors alongside momentum and the average factor (Bakshi-Gao-Rossi 2019). High Sharpe, low correlation to equities. CRITICAL caveat from Gorton-Rouwenhorst: this is compensation for spot-price risk linked to low inventories, NOT a mechanical roll-yield free lunch — naive 'collect the roll' framing is the roll-yield fallacy.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Moderate. Post-2004 financialization compressed some commodity premia and raised cross-commodity comovement (contango/backwardation now co-move with macro/financial shocks, Luisi et al). Basis factor more robust than commodity momentum. Watch crowding in the most-traded contracts (oil, gold) and roll-cost slippage around index-roll dates (the 'Goldman roll').
- **Bond carry (cross-country + within-curve)**
  - *Signal:* Two flavors. (1) Cross-country: rank sovereign bond futures (US, DE, UK, JP, AU, CA...) by carry = yield + roll-down minus financing, per unit duration; long high-carry markets short low-carry, duration-neutral. (2) Within-curve / term-structure: hold the maturity with the best carry-per-unit-duration (term structure of Sharpe ratios is downward-sloping — short maturities earn more Sharpe per duration). Carry here = slope + roll-down.
  - *Params:* Carry_bond ~ (yield - short-rate) + roll-down (price gain from aging down a static upward-sloping curve). Express per unit of duration (DV01-neutral L/S). Universe: 6-10 developed sovereign futures. Rebalance monthly. The 1y bond earns ~2.5x the Sharpe-per-duration of the 30y (term structure of Sharpe ratios). KMPV: US-Treasury carry L/S Sharpe ~0.68.  ·  *Assets:* Government bond futures, sovereign rates
  - *Evidence (honest):* KMPV: Treasury carry-per-duration L/S Sharpe ~0.68; carry beats passive in most classes EXCEPT global bond level/slope. Bond carry is heavily correlated with the term-premium and curve slope — much of the 'edge' is just being long duration when the curve is steep, so it's exposed to rate shocks and inflation surprises.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Lower crowding than FX/vol, but the premium is regime-dependent on the term premium itself, which has been compressed/negative for long stretches post-QE. The roll-down component evaporates when curves are flat or inverted. Honest: bond carry is a duration-timing bet dressed as a carry factor — size accordingly.
- **Volatility carry (short variance / sell the VRP)**
  - *Signal:* Sell the variance risk premium: short a variance swap (or delta-hedged ATM straddle, or short VIX futures, or systematic put-write/strangle-sell) and collect implied-minus-realized variance. VRP = synthetic variance-swap rate (1/K^2 OTM option strip, Carr-Wu) minus realized variance; it is structurally NEGATIVE for equity indices, so the SELLER earns the premium. Can time it: scale exposure UP when VRP is wide (Bollerslev-Tauchen-Zhou: high VRP predicts high returns).
  - *Params:* Instruments: 1M variance swaps / delta-hedged ATM options / short front VIX futures / put-write. Hold ~1M, roll. Position-size to a STRICT vol or tail budget — this strategy's left tail is its defining feature. Use a kill-switch / convexity overlay (own deep OTM puts or long-vol tail hedge) so a single Volmageddon doesn't end the book. Time with VRP level and term-structure (sell when VIX>realized and curve in contango).  ·  *Assets:* Equity index volatility (SPX/VIX); extends to FX vol, rates (swaptions), commodity vol
  - *Evidence (honest):* Index VRP is robustly negative and large; naive backtests show very high Sharpe (academic variance-swap studies report 1.5-3+) — but these are radically overstated because the distribution is the most negatively-skewed of any carry strategy. Carr-Wu: index VRP strongly negative, single-stock VRP near zero (the premium is a SYSTEMATIC/correlation-risk premium, not idiosyncratic).
  - *Decay/crowding:* HIGH crowding risk demonstrated catastrophically. Feb 5 2018 'Volmageddon': crowded short-vol via levered/inverse ETPs (XIV) created a feedback loop — vol spike forced ETPs to BUY VIX futures to stay neutral, amplifying the spike; XIV fell from ~$140 to ~$6 (~-96%) and was terminated. The premium itself persists (structural demand for insurance) but the strategy is uninvestable without hard tail risk controls and capacity awareness.
- **Global multi-asset carry composite**
  - *Signal:* Combine FX, commodity, bond, and (optionally) equity-index and credit carry into one diversified, vol-weighted book — each leg dollar/duration-neutral, then risk-parity across legs. KMPV's core empirical result is that the diversified global carry factor has a much higher Sharpe than any single sleeve BUT all sleeves crash together in global recessions.
  - *Params:* Equal-risk-weight across 4-6 carry sleeves. Rebalance monthly. Overlay a single GLOBAL risk regime filter (global vol, drawdown, funding-liquidity/TED) that de-grosses the WHOLE book in risk-off — because the diversification is fake in the left tail (correlations -> 1 exactly when you need them low). KMPV global carry gross Sharpe ~1.1; carry-timing ~0.9.  ·  *Assets:* FX + commodities + bonds + equity indices + credit (the KMPV universe)
  - *Evidence (honest):* KMPV: across-class diversified carry gross Sharpe ~1.1, far above the ~0.7 within-class average — the headline result of the literature. Net-of-cost lower; the gross figures are pre-2017 and pre-decay.
  - *Decay/crowding:* The diversification benefit is REAL in normal times and ILLUSORY in tail events (the unifying recession-risk exposure). Crowding varies by sleeve (vol and FX most crowded). The honest framing: you are harvesting one global disaster-risk premium dressed up as five diversified ones.

**Canonical sources:**
