(8) Liquidation/margin-call risk in the carry trade: a basis SPIKE forces the short-perp leg into a margin call precisely in euphoria regimes — the BIS paper shows high carry predicts ~22% more short-liquidations next month. The trade can be right at expiry yet liquidate before convergence. Keep a >2x maintenance-margin buffer. (9) Cross-exchange/triangular/MEV are HFT/co-location/builder games with ms-to-block-time lifetimes. A paper-first or slow execution stack will see the opportunity in data and capture ~none of it (the 'Wish or reality?' verdict). Do not let in-sample opportunity counts masquerade as P&L. (10) Short data history & few cycles: crypto has ~3-4 halving cycles and ~5-8 years of liquid derivatives data. On-chain-metric and factor strategies have very low effective sample sizes — extreme overfitting risk. Multiverse/walk-forward validation is mandatory, and many 'edges' are one regime deep. (11) Decay is the default, not the exception: the carry/basis premium is being competed away by ETF inflows and institutional capital (carry Sharpe negative in 2025; CME basis ~5% vs ~4.5% risk-free; CME OI at 14-month low). Backtests on 2020-2022 data massively overstate forward edge. Always re-estimate the premium on the most recent 6-12 months.

**Honest validation:** (1) Build a point-in-time funding-rate and basis dataset: store each venue's 8h funding print and dated-futures basis as-published with exact timestamps, never restated. Backtest the carry strategy with funding accrued ONLY at settlement timestamps and signals lagged by at least one settlement period. This alone kills most phantom funding-carry profit (anti-Profit-Mirage gate #1). (2) Cost everything to the bone, then double it: round-trip taker fees on both legs, funding paid on the short, borrow cost on the spot/long, slippage from real order-book depth (not mid), and a counterparty-risk haircut. Re-run the headline Sharpe net of this stack — He-Manela's 1.8-vs-3.5 split shows the result lives or dies on the cost tier. If the edge survives only at market-maker fees you don't have, it's not your edge. (3) Use a survivorship-bias-free universe: include delisted/dead tokens and exchanges that later failed. For any cross-sectional or carry-across-alts strategy, reconstruct the tradable universe as it existed at each point in time. Expect 17-22%/yr of apparent return to vanish. (4) Filter exchange data for wash trading and data gaps before computing any volume/liquidity/VWAP signal; cross-check prices across >=2 independent venues and reject outliers. Treat single-venue, low-integrity data as unusable for execution modeling. (5) Walk-forward / out-of-sample on the MOST RECENT window: estimate parameters on 2020-2023, then test 2024-2025 untouched — the decay (carry Sharpe 6.45 -> 4.06 -> negative) will appear if you do this honestly and will be hidden if you don't. Demand the strategy still clears its hurdle in the last 6-12 months, not just over the full sample. (6) For DeFi-LP strategies, backtest fees-minus-LVR explicitly using on-chain swap data and a CEX price reference (LVR = integral of sigma^2/8 over the holding period). Reject any LP allocation whose realized fees do not exceed realized LVR + gas out-of-sample. Never accept advertised APR as evidence. (7) For DeFi-lending strategies, decompose yield into organic (borrower-funded: borrow_rate*util*(1-reserve)) vs token-subsidy components; only count the organic part as a durable edge and stress-test withdrawal liquidity at high utilization. Add an explicit smart-contract-loss probability (use historical exploit base rates) to the expected return. (8) Stress / scenario tests as first-class: (a) a venue insolvency that freezes one leg, (b) a stablecoin depeg of 5-15%, (c) a basis spike forcing margin call before convergence, (d) a funding inversion lasting weeks. The strategy must survive each with bounded loss, not just have a nice average Sharpe. (9) Reality-gap test (anti-Profit-Mirage): explicitly enumerate which 'opportunities' are capturable with the fund's ACTUAL execution latency and inventory. Re-run cross-exchange/triangular signals restricted to tradable windows only — expect the 0.68/wk -> 0.04/wk collapse the survey documents. If the edge requires co-location you don't have, mark it un-investable, don't book it. (10) Capacity / market-impact check: estimate how much capital the premium absorbs before it self-compresses (the ETF-basis unwind is the cautionary tale). Validate at the fund's intended AUM, not at toy size.

**Data:** "Point-in-time perpetual funding rates (8h, per-venue: Binance/Bybit/OKX/Deribit/Hyperliquid) with exact settlement timestamps — the single most important dataset; never use restated. Spot and dated-futures prices (CME quarterly + crypto-native quarterlies) for basis computation, tick or 1-min. Full order-book depth snapshots (L2) per venue for realistic slippage/impact modeling — NOT just OHLCV. Survivorship-bias-free token universe including delisted/dead assets with listing/delisting dates (CoinGecko/CoinMarketCap full history). Wash-trading-filtered volume or volume from regulated/high-integrity venues only. Stablecoin price/peg history (USDC/USDT/DAI) for depeg modeling. On-chain data: DeFi money-market rates and utilization (Aave/Compound/Morpho, point-in-time), AMM pool swap-level data + reserves for LVR backtests, and UTXO/address-level metrics (MVRV, SOPR, NVT, exchange flows) from Glassnode/CryptoQuant/Dune. Borrow/lending rates for the spot leg (financing cost). Exchange-event/insolvency timeline for counterparty stress tests. Granularity: 8h for funding-native logic, 1-min/tick for execution and basis-spike modeling, daily/weekly for on-chain-metric and factor signals. Latency note: cross-exchange/MEV strategies additionally require co-located, low-microsecond market data + mempool access — flag as infra-gated, out of scope for paper-first validation."

**Fits our stack:** "R3 (evidence): Carry/basis and lending/LP yield are EXACTLY the family a paper-first fund can validate cleanly — funding rates, basis, on-chain rates, and AMM swaps are all observable point-in-time and gradeable without live execution infra; cross-exchange/triangular/MEV are NOT (infra-gated, mark un-investable). Evidence quality tiers: carry/basis = strong-but-decaying (He-Manela, BIS), lending = mechanistic-and-measurable (PLF), LP = measurable-but-usually-negative-net (LVR), on-chain-metric = weak/overfit-prone. R4 (data): the binding constraints are point-in-time funding-rate storage, survivorship-bias-free universe, L2 depth, and wash-trade filtering — these are concrete ingestion requirements, not optional niceties; without them the backtest lies. R5 (alpha): the durable signals are (a) net-funding-above-hurdle carry with a short-EWMA forward funding signal, (b) basis>risk-free+counterparty-premium cash-and-carry, (c) organic-only DeFi lending yield, (d) fees>LVR stable/correlated-pair LP; momentum (Liu-Tsyvinski) is a cross-asset overlay, on-chain metrics a slow regime tilt. RISK: this family is dominated by tail risks the vol does NOT capture — exchange default, depeg, liquidation, smart-contract exploit — so the risk module must carry per-venue insolvency limits, stablecoin depeg scenarios, margin-buffer rules, and protocol-exploit base rates as first-class constraints, not VaR add-ons. PORTFOLIO: carry/basis/lending yields are positively correlated with each other and with the crypto-leverage cycle (they all spike and unwind together — see the 0.878 basis-ETF-outflow correlation), so treat them as ONE risk factor, not diversifiers; size against a single 'crypto-funding-regime' exposure. EXECUTION: carry/basis/lending/LP are slow-rebalance (8h-daily) and fit a non-HFT execution stack; the moment a sub-strategy needs sub-second latency or builder relationships (cross-exchange, triangular, MEV), it leaves our executable envelope. Net: prioritize funding-carry + cash-and-carry + organic-lending for R3->R5; gate the arb-latency family out; encode decay-monitoring (re-estimate the premium on trailing 6-12mo) as a standing risk check."

**Go deeper:** • Read He, Manela, Ross, von Wachter 'Fundamentals of Perpetual Futures' (arXiv:2212.06888) in full for the random-maturity-arbitrage construction and the exact cost-tier Sharpe decomposition — it is the rigorous backbone of the carry strategy. • Read BIS WP 1087 / Management Science 'Crypto Carry' (Schmeling, Schrimpf, Todorov) for the segmentation/limits-to-arbitrage theory and the carry->liquidation predictive result; pair with the CEPR voxeu summary for the intuition. • Read Milionis, Moallemi, Roughgarden, Zhang 'AMM & Loss-Versus-Rebalancing' (arXiv:2208.06046) + the a16z LVR explainer for the sigma^2/8 derivation and the break-even volume math before ever allocating to LP. • Track the 2025-2026 basis-unwind primary data (The Block, CoinDesk Dec 2025; CME/CryptoQuant annualized-basis charts) to quantify exactly how far the premium has compressed and whether a re-widening window has opened — this is the live decay monitor. • Read Liu, Tsyvinski, Wu (NBER w24877 and the JF C3/C4 factor papers) for the systematic-factor backbone (momentum lookbacks, size, the cross-section) to layer a factor overlay onto the carry book. • Read the Flashbots / MEV literature (Daian et al. 'Flash Boys 2.0' + 'Remeasuring Arbitrage and Sandwich MEV' arXiv:2405.17944) ONLY to confirm why DEX/cross-exchange arb is out of scope — and to understand that as an LP you are the prey, not the predator. • Read Gudgeon et al. 'DeFi Protocols for Loanable Funds' (arXiv:2006.13922) and current Aave v3 / Morpho interest-rate-model docs for the utilization-curve mechanics behind sustainable lending yield, and decompose live APYs into organic vs subsidy. • Read Cong, Li, Tang, Yang 'Crypto Wash Trading' (arXiv:2108.10984) and CoinAPI/StratBase survivorship-bias write-ups before building the data layer — get the data hygiene right before any signal, because every downstream edge inherits these biases. • IG-12 prompt this deepens: the crypto-native strategy-generation prompt — feed it (a) the carry/basis premium as the primary edge with its decay schedule, (b) the explicit infra gate separating slow-rebalance carry/yield from HFT/MEV arb, (c) the tail-risk taxonomy (exchange/depeg/liquidation/exploit) as mandatory risk constraints, and (d) the point-in-time + survivorship-free + wash-filtered data requirements as preconditions for any backtest to count.

---
## portfolio-sizing (portfolio construction & position sizing: from raw signals/covariances to a sized, leveraged book)

**Thesis.** Portfolio sizing is where alpha goes to die or get amplified. The edge here is NOT a market anomaly — it is an *estimation-error management* edge. Markowitz mean-variance is optimal given true moments, but with sampled moments it is an "error-maximizer": it overweights assets whose return looks high and vol looks low precisely *because* those are the largest estimation errors. The whole family (shrinkage, Black-Litterman, risk parity, HRP, constraints, Kelly-fractioning) exists to trade a little bias for a large reduction in variance of the *weights*, because out-of-sample portfolio risk is dominated by weight instability, not by the in-sample objective. This "edge" persists permanently because it is not arbitraged away — it is a structural property of optimizing over noisy inputs, and every new fund re-learns it. It does NOT decay from crowding (estimation error is not a trade). What DOES decay/disappear: any specific claim that a clever optimizer *beats 1/N net of costs* (DeMiguel et al. 2009 — most don't), and any vol-targeting Sharpe boost outside risk-assets with a leverage effect. The honest framing: the goal of good sizing is robustness and survival (not blowing up, staying invested through drawdowns), NOT squeezing an extra basis point from a cleverer objective. Sizing protects the alpha you have; it rarely creates new alpha.

**Concrete strategies:**
- **Ledoit-Wolf linear/nonlinear shrinkage covariance (the default robustifier)**
  - *Signal:* Estimator = delta*F + (1-delta)*S, where S = sample covariance, F = structured target (constant-correlation matrix is the standard choice: keep sample variances on diagonal, replace all correlations with their average rbar; off-diag f_ij = rbar*sqrt(s_ii*s_jj)). Optimal intensity delta* = max(0, min(1, kappa/T)) with kappa = (pi - rho)/gamma: pi = sum of asymptotic variances of sample cov entries, rho = asymptotic covariances between target and sample, gamma = sum of squared misspecification (f_ij - s_ij)^2. Nonlinear (QIS) version individually shrinks each sample eigenvalue toward the bulk instead of one global delta.
  - *Params:* Lookback: 1-3y daily returns typical. delta computed analytically (no tuning). Use nonlinear (QIS) when N/T > ~0.3; linear constant-correlation when N/T small. sklearn: LedoitWolf() or OAS(); for nonlinear use Ledoit's covShrinkage QIS code.  ·  *Assets:* Any. Essential for equity universes where N (assets) approaches or exceeds T (observations). The single highest-ROI fix to plug into Markowitz/min-var/Kelly.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Honest: well-established that shrinkage strictly improves out-of-sample MV portfolio risk vs raw sample cov (Ledoit-Wolf 2004, 2017). BUT Jagannathan-Ma (2003) and Disatnik show a simple no-short constraint or even averaging-target captures most of the benefit — shrinkage's edge over a long-only constraint is modest. Net-of-cost it reduces blowup risk more than it adds return.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Does NOT decay — it's a statistical estimator, not a trade. Commoditized (in every library) but that's fine; it's infrastructure, not alpha.
- **Hierarchical Risk Parity (HRP)**
  - *Signal:* 3 stages, no matrix inversion. (1) Tree clustering: convert correlation to distance d_ij = sqrt(0.5*(1-rho_ij)), then a secondary Euclidean distance over distance-profiles, agglomerative linkage (single-linkage in original; Ward/complete often more stable). (2) Quasi-diagonalization: reorder cov rows/cols per the dendrogram so similar assets are adjacent (block-diagonal-ish). (3) Recursive bisection: split the ordered list in two, weight each half inversely to its cluster variance (w'Vw using inverse-variance within), recurse. Weights stay in [0,1], sum to 1.
  - *Params:* Linkage method (single vs Ward — a real free parameter that changes results), distance metric, lookback for correlation (252d typical). No expected returns needed. mlfinlab / PyPortfolioOpt / skfolio implement it.  ·  *Assets:* Best on large, noisy, ill-conditioned universes (many correlated assets) where Markowitz inversion is unstable. Multi-asset and large equity baskets.
  - *Evidence (honest):* MIXED — flag this honestly. Lopez de Prado (2016) showed HRP < CLA out-of-sample variance via Monte Carlo (synthetic data). BUT multiple independent empirical replications (Jaeger et al., and 2025 out-of-sample studies) find NO evidence HRP beats inverse-variance or vanilla risk parity on real data; results are sensitive to covariance estimator and linkage choice. The Monte Carlo win does not robustly survive contact with real returns. Treat HRP as a stability tool, not a performance edge.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Not crowding-sensitive (allocation method). The 'outperformance' claim is fragile/possibly overfit to the original simulation design — the honest status is 'stable weights, no proven return edge.'
- **Risk Parity / Equal Risk Contribution (ERC)**
  - *Signal:* Choose weights so each asset's marginal contribution to portfolio vol is equal: RC_i = w_i * (Sigma w)_i / sqrt(w'Sigma w) equal for all i. Solve via convex optimization (Spinu/Roncalli formulation) or Newton iteration. Naive version (no correlations): w_i proportional to 1/sigma_i (inverse-vol). Then apply leverage to hit a target portfolio vol.
  - *Params:* Vol lookback (60-252d), rebalance frequency (monthly/quarterly), target portfolio vol (e.g., 10%), leverage cap. Long-only by construction.  ·  *Assets:* Multi-asset (the All-Weather use case: stocks/bonds/commodities/TIPS levered to equal risk). Within-equity too. NOT for assets with no positive risk premium (you'd lever junk).
  - *Evidence (honest):* Honest: risk parity's strong 1990s-2010s record is substantially a levered-bonds bet during a 40-year bond bull market. Maillard et al. (2010) prove the math (unique, between min-var and 1/N) — that part is solid. The PERFORMANCE depended on (a) low/negative stock-bond correlation and (b) falling rates. 2022 (stocks AND bonds down, correlation flipped positive) was brutal for risk parity. The diversification math is real; the historical Sharpe is regime-dependent and partly a leverage-on-bonds artifact.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Decayed as a return story post-2021 (rate regime change, positive stock-bond correlation). Crowded: ~hundreds of billions AUM; forced de-levering on vol spikes (e.g., Feb 2018 'Volmageddon', Mar 2020) is a documented feedback risk. Still valid as a diversification discipline, NOT as a guaranteed Sharpe machine.
- **Fractional Kelly position sizing / volatility targeting**
  - *Signal:* Continuous-time Kelly: optimal leverage f* = mu/sigma^2 (single asset, excess return) or w* = Sigma^-1 * mu (multivariate). Full Kelly maximizes long-run log-growth but has ~50-80% drawdowns and is hypersensitive to mu estimation error. Use FRACTIONAL Kelly: scale by c in [0.25, 0.5]. Half-Kelly retains ~75% of growth rate with ~half the variance/drawdown. Vol targeting is a special case: scale exposure by (target_vol / realized_vol_forecast) — equivalent to assuming constant Sharpe and only re-estimating sigma (the more reliable input).
  - *Params:* Kelly fraction c=0.25-0.5 (never full). Vol estimate: EWMA (lambda~0.94, RiskMetrics) or realized vol over 20-60d. Target vol 10-15% annualized. Need 50-100+ trades to estimate edge; a 5pp win-rate error can move full-Kelly fraction 3x. Cap leverage, floor/ceiling per-trade risk (1-5%).  ·  *Assets:* Vol targeting works best on RISK ASSETS with a leverage effect (equities, credit) where vol is persistent and negatively correlated with returns. Kelly fractioning is universal but most defensible where the edge is well-measured.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Honest and asset-class-specific. Moreira-Muir / Harvey et al.: vol targeting improves Sharpe and cuts drawdowns for EQUITIES, CREDIT, and the MOMENTUM factor — because vol is persistent there. BUT Cederburg, O'Doherty et al. (2020) show it does NOT help most other factors (value, etc.) and can WORSEN drawdowns in global equities; the benefit hinges on the leverage effect. Vol targeting on assets without vol-persistence/leverage-effect just adds turnover. Half-Kelly's 75%-growth/half-variance is a derived mathematical property, robust.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Vol targeting is crowded (everyone de-levers on the same vol spike → synchronized selling, gap risk; implicated in 2018 and 2020 deleveraging cascades). Fractional Kelly itself doesn't crowd. The vol-target Sharpe boost has partially decayed/been-arbitraged in the most-traded equity-index context.
- **Black-Litterman view blending**
  - *Signal:* Reverse-optimize to get equilibrium-implied returns Pi = lambda*Sigma*w_mkt (lambda = market risk aversion ~2.5, w_mkt = cap weights). Combine with views via the master formula: E[R] = [(tau*Sigma)^-1 + P'*Omega^-1*P]^-1 * [(tau*Sigma)^-1*Pi + P'*Omega^-1*Q], where P = view-pick matrix, Q = view returns, Omega = view-uncertainty (diagonal), tau = prior scaler. Feed E[R] into mean-variance — weights tilt smoothly from market toward views, sized by confidence.
  - *Params:* tau in [0.025, 0.05]. lambda ~2.5. Omega: set via Idzorek's confidence% method (0-100% per view) instead of guessing. Views can come from your alpha signals (signal -> Q).  ·  *Assets:* Multi-asset and equity. The institutional way to inject alpha signals into sizing WITHOUT corner solutions / 400% short positions.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Honest: BL reliably FIXES Markowitz's instability and corner-solution pathology (well-documented). Whether it adds RETURN depends entirely on whether your views (Q) have real IC — BL is a blending machine, not an alpha source. Out-of-sample studies (e.g., reverse-optimization-views variant) show modest, conditional gains. The value is robustness and interpretability, not magic alpha.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Method doesn't crowd. Its usefulness is entirely downstream of your signal quality; no decay in the blending mechanism itself.
- **Combining many weak signals (Fundamental Law / signal blending)**
  - *Signal:* IR = IC * sqrt(Breadth) (Grinold-Kahn). To combine K alpha signals: z-score each cross-sectionally, then weight. Equal-weight the standardized signals is the robust default (estimation error in optimal weights usually swamps the gain from optimizing them). Optimal combination (Menchero-Lee 2015): weight by IC and de-correlate — if signals correlated, w proportional to Omega^-1 * IC (Omega = signal correlation matrix), exactly analogous to portfolio optimization on the signals. Independence of signals is the key assumption that inflates effective breadth.
  - *Params:* IC measured via rank correlation (Spearman) of signal vs forward returns, rolling 12-36mo. Signal correlation matrix Omega. Decay/half-life of each signal. Cross-sectional z-score winsorized at +/-3.  ·  *Assets:* Cross-sectional equity (where breadth is large — hundreds of names) is the natural home. Also futures/macro signal blends.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Honest: the Fundamental Law is a useful organizing principle, NOT a precise predictor — the 'breadth' term assumes independence that rarely holds (correlated signals/positions shrink TRUE breadth far below nominal count). Grinold-Kahn 'Redux' (Ding-Martin) and others show real breadth is much lower than name-count once positions/signals correlate. Equal-weighting standardized signals usually beats 'optimal' weights out-of-sample (same estimation-error lesson as Markowitz). More uncorrelated signals genuinely helps; chasing optimal weights on them usually doesn't.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Individual signals decay/crowd (that's the alpha layer). The COMBINATION discipline (z-score, de-correlate, equal-weight) is durable infrastructure.

**Canonical sources:**
- *Optimal Versus Naive Diversification: How Inefficient is the 1/N Portfolio Strategy?* — Victor DeMiguel, Lorenzo Garlappi, Raman Uppal (2009, paper): The benchmark-setting paper of the entire field. Across 7 empirical datasets, NONE of 14 sophisticated mean-variance models consistently beat 1/N out-of-sample net of costs. Estimation window needed for sample MV to beat 1/N: ~3000 months for 25 assets, ~6000 for 50. This is the null hypothesis every sizing method must clear. Anyone who skips this paper builds on sand. — https://academic.oup.com/rfs/article/22/5/1915/1592901
- *Honey, I Shrunk the Sample Covariance Matrix* — Olivier Ledoit, Michael Wolf (2004, paper): Introduced linear shrinkage with an analytically-optimal intensity (no cross-validation needed). The default covariance estimator in scikit-learn (LedoitWolf, OAS) and the most widely-deployed robustification in production. Solves the p>>T ill-conditioning that makes Markowitz explode. — http://www.ledoit.net/honey.pdf
- *Nonlinear Shrinkage Estimation of Large-Dimensional Covariance Matrices* — Olivier Ledoit, Michael Wolf (2012/2020, paper): Successor to linear shrinkage: applies an individualized shrinkage intensity to every eigenvalue (QIS — quadratic-inverse shrinkage is the current best). Materially better than linear when N/T is large. The 'right' answer for large-universe equity covariance in 2026. — https://www.econ.uzh.ch/dam/jcr:ffffffff-935a-b0d6-ffff-ffffb71c25e8/ledoit_wolf_2017.pdf
- *Building Diversified Portfolios that Outperform Out of Sample* — Marcos Lopez de Prado (2016, paper): Introduced Hierarchical Risk Parity (HRP). The key insight: quadratic optimizers require inverting an ill-conditioned covariance matrix (the 'Markowitz curse' — more correlated assets = higher condition number = more instability). HRP avoids inversion entirely via tree-clustering + recursive bisection, producing stable weights even on singular matrices. — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2708678
- *On the Properties of Equally-Weighted Risk Contributions Portfolios* — Sebastien Maillard, Thierry Roncalli, Jerome Teiletche (2010, paper): Formalized risk parity (Equal Risk Contribution) for volatility. Proves the ERC portfolio is unique, sits between min-variance and 1/N, and needs only the covariance matrix (no expected returns — the noisiest input). The theoretical backbone of the trillion-dollar risk-parity industry (Bridgewater All Weather, AQR). — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=1271972
- *Global Portfolio Optimization (The Black-Litterman Model)* — Fischer Black, Robert Litterman (1992, paper): Solved Markowitz's 'corner solution' problem by starting from market-equilibrium implied returns (reverse optimization) and Bayesian-updating with investor views. Turns sizing into a disciplined blend of a sane prior + your alpha. The institutional standard for incorporating views without the optimizer going haywire. — https://www.cfainstitute.org/en/research/financial-analysts-journal/1992/global-portfolio-optimization
- *A Step-By-Step Guide to the Black-Litterman Model* — Thomas Idzorek (2007, paper): The implementable BL reference. Gives the master formula explicitly and — crucially — a method to specify view confidence as an intuitive 0-100% instead of the opaque Omega matrix. This is the doc you code from. — https://people.duke.edu/~charvey/Teaching/BA453_2006/Idzorek_onBL.pdf
- *A New Interpretation of Information Rate (Kelly Criterion) + The Kelly Capital Growth Investment Criterion* — J.L. Kelly Jr (1956); MacLean, Thorp, Ziemba (eds, 2011) (1956/2011, book): Kelly's original info-theory result + the definitive modern treatment. For continuous returns the multivariate Kelly weights are w* = Sigma^-1 * mu (excess returns), identical in form to mean-variance with risk-aversion=1. Establishes WHY fractional Kelly is mandatory in practice: full Kelly maximizes growth but has brutal drawdowns and is destroyed by estimation error in mu. — https://www.worldscientific.com/worldscibook/10.1142/7598
- *Risk Reduction in Large Portfolios: Why Imposing the Wrong Constraints Helps* — Ravi Jagannathan, Tongshu Ma (2003, paper): Proves a no-short-sale constraint on min-variance is mathematically equivalent to shrinking the covariance matrix. 'Wrong' constraints help because they cap the damage the optimizer can do with estimation error. Explains why constrained sample-covariance MVP performs as well as fancy shrinkage estimators. — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=424756
- *The Probability of Backtest Overfitting / The Deflated Sharpe Ratio* — Bailey, Borwein, Lopez de Prado, Zhu (2014/2016, paper): The anti-Profit-Mirage canon. CSCV estimates Probability of Backtest Overfitting; Deflated Sharpe Ratio corrects an observed Sharpe for the number of trials you ran, sample length, skew and kurtosis. Mandatory gate before believing ANY sizing-method comparison — most 'X beats Y' results are selection bias. — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2326253
- *Active Portfolio Management (esp. The Fundamental Law of Active Management)* — Richard Grinold, Ronald Kahn (1999, book): IR = IC * sqrt(Breadth). The governing equation for combining many weak signals: value comes from skill (IC) times number of independent bets. Tells you that more uncorrelated signals dominates a better single signal, and motivates signal-blending and broad cross-sectional books. Menchero-Lee (2015) extends it to the optimal combination of correlated alphas. — https://www.mhebooklibrary.com/doi/book/10.1036/0070248826
- *Machine Learning for Asset Managers* — Marcos Lopez de Prado (2020, book): Compact operational manual for the modern stack: covariance denoising via Marchenko-Pastur (cut eigenvalues below the random-matrix bound), detoning, Nested Clustered Optimization (NCO), and the distinction between noise-instability and signal-instability. The 2026-relevant recipe book. — https://www.cambridge.org/core/elements/machine-learning-for-asset-managers/6D9211305EA2E425D33A9F38D0AE3545

**Pitfalls:** (1) Optimizing weights on the same data you estimated moments from. In-sample min-variance is meaningless; the only number that matters is OUT-OF-SAMPLE realized risk/return on a walk-forward basis. Markowitz is an error-MAXIMIZER on sampled inputs. (2) Believing the 'X beats Y' Monte Carlo / single-backtest. HRP's original outperformance came from synthetic data and does not robustly replicate on real returns (multiple 2018-2025 studies). Always ask: in-sample or OOS? synthetic or real? how many trials? net of costs? (3) Trusting expected-return inputs (mu). mu is FAR noisier than covariance — that's why risk parity, min-var, and HRP avoid mu entirely. If you must use mu (Kelly, BL, MV), shrink it hard or you get garbage weights. (4) Full Kelly. It maximizes log-growth in theory but with estimated mu it routinely over-bets into ruinous drawdowns. Fractional (0.25-0.5x) is not optional — it's the only safe operating point, and it ALSO hedges the estimation error in your edge. (5) Vol targeting where vol isn't persistent / no leverage effect. It boosts Sharpe for equities, credit, momentum; for many other factors (Cederburg et al.) it adds turnover and can deepen drawdowns. Don't cargo-cult it onto every strategy. (6) Risk parity as a guaranteed Sharpe machine. Much of its golden-age record was levered bonds in a 40-year bull. 2022 (positive stock-bond correlation, both down) broke that. The diversification math is sound; the historical performance is regime-conditional. (7) Ignoring transaction costs and turnover. DeMiguel et al.: costs significantly erode any optimizer's edge over 1/N. Shrinkage/BL/risk-parity all generate turnover on rebalance; a method that 'wins' gross can lose net. Always evaluate net-of-cost with realistic turnover penalties. (8) Crowding/synchronized de-leveraging. Vol-target and risk-parity funds de-lever on the SAME vol spikes — a documented feedback loop (Feb 2018 Volmageddon, Mar 2020). Your sizing rule can become a systemic gap-risk amplifier. (9) Ill-conditioned covariance (the Markowitz curse). More-correlated assets => higher condition number => tiny input errors => giant weight swings. Must denoise (Marchenko-Pastur eigenvalue cut) or shrink BEFORE inverting, or skip inversion (HRP/NCO). (10) Fake breadth in the Fundamental Law. IR = IC*sqrt(Breadth) assumes independent bets; correlated signals/positions collapse effective breadth far below the name count. Counting 500 stocks as 500 independent bets is a classic overstatement of skill.

**Honest validation:** (1) Walk-forward / rolling out-of-sample ONLY. Estimate moments on window [t-L, t], hold over [t, t+h], roll. Report OOS realized vol, Sharpe, max drawdown, turnover — never in-sample objective values. This is the single most important discipline. (2) Always benchmark against 1/N (equal weight) AND inverse-volatility, net of transaction costs. Per DeMiguel et al., these are the null hypotheses. If your fancy optimizer doesn't beat 1/N net of costs OOS, it adds nothing. (3) Deflated Sharpe Ratio (Bailey-Lopez de Prado): correct any reported Sharpe for the NUMBER of configurations you tried (linkage methods, lookbacks, shrinkage targets, Kelly fractions), sample length, skew, and kurtosis. A Sharpe that survives deflation across your search is real; one that doesn't is selection bias. (4) Probability of Backtest Overfitting via CSCV (Combinatorially Symmetric Cross-Validation): split the backtest into N submatrices, test every combinatorial in-sample/out-of-sample split, measure how often the in-sample-best config underperforms OOS median. High PBO => your sizing-method ranking is overfit noise. (5) Parameter-perturbation / robustness check: jitter the covariance lookback, linkage method, Kelly fraction, vol-target by +/-20%. A genuine sizing improvement is stable under perturbation; a fragile one's ranking flips. (This is how you catch HRP's linkage-sensitivity.) (6) Cost and turnover stress test: re-run with 2x-5x assumed transaction costs and realistic market-impact. Many sizing 'wins' are turnover artifacts that vanish under honest costs. (7) Regime / subperiod split: report performance in distinct regimes (e.g., positive vs negative stock-bond correlation, high vs low vol). Risk parity and vol targeting are regime-conditional — a single full-sample Sharpe hides this. (8) Bootstrap the weight stability itself: resample returns, re-estimate weights, measure weight dispersion. Markowitz weights will swing wildly; shrunk/HRP/risk-parity weights should be tight. Unstable weights => high turnover => cost drag => the real OOS killer.

**Data:** "Core: asset return time series (daily preferred; 1-3y minimum for covariance, longer for stable correlations). For covariance estimation you need N assets x T observations with T comfortably > N or you MUST denoise/shrink (Marchenko-Pastur cut needs the ratio q=N/T). For Black-Litterman: market-cap weights (for equilibrium prior), a risk-aversion estimate. For Kelly/MV: expected excess returns (the noisy input — source from your R5 alpha layer, shrink hard). For risk parity / vol targeting: realized-vol estimates (EWMA needs intraday or daily; RiskMetrics lambda=0.94). For signal combination: per-signal forward-return IC time series (rolling), signal cross-correlation matrix. For honest validation: transaction-cost/market-impact model, multiple rebalance-frequency variants. Critical data-quality needs: survivorship-bias-free universe, point-in-time membership, corporate-action-adjusted returns — covariance/correlation estimates are corrupted by survivorship and look-ahead just like alpha backtests."

**Fits our stack:** "Maps to the PORTFOLIO + RISK layers, sitting between R5 (alpha/signals) and execution. R5 produces raw signals/expected returns; this family turns them into a sized, risk-controlled book. Concrete pipeline placement: (1) R4 DATA provides survivorship-free returns + cap weights + cost model. (2) RISK layer owns the covariance estimator — make Ledoit-Wolf nonlinear (QIS) shrinkage or Marchenko-Pastur denoising the DEFAULT, never raw sample cov; expose condition-number as a health metric. (3) PORTFOLIO layer owns the sizing method — recommend a default of risk-parity/ERC or HRP for the *risk allocation* (no mu needed, stable), with Black-Litterman as the bridge for injecting R5 signals as 'views' (Q) so the optimizer never produces corner solutions. (4) Apply FRACTIONAL Kelly (0.25-0.5x) + vol-targeting as the final leverage/scaling step, but ONLY where the leverage-effect holds (gate by asset class). (5) EXECUTION layer must see the turnover this generates — sizing and execution are coupled via cost. CRITICAL stack discipline: the anti-Profit-Mirage validation (R3 EVIDENCE layer) must wrap every sizing-method choice in Deflated-Sharpe + PBO/CSCV + 1/N-net-of-cost benchmark, because the literature is littered with sizing 'wins' that are selection bias. This deepens the IG-12 'position sizing & portfolio construction' prompt: the honest message to encode is that sizing is RISK MANAGEMENT and ESTIMATION-ERROR CONTROL (survival, robustness, not blowing up), not a second alpha source — and that 1/N is the benchmark to beat, not assume you've beaten."

**Go deeper:** • Ledoit & Wolf nonlinear shrinkage (QIS) reference code: http://www.econ.uzh.ch/en/people/faculty/wolf/publications.html — drop-in covariance estimator; benchmark it against sklearn LedoitWolf and raw sample cov on your universe • Lopez de Prado, 'Machine Learning for Asset Managers' (2020) — Ch. on covariance denoising (Marchenko-Pastur), detoning, and Nested Clustered Optimization (NCO); NCO is the principled successor to HRP that separates noise-instability from signal-instability • Bailey & Lopez de Prado, 'The Deflated Sharpe Ratio' (2016) + 'Probability of Backtest Overfitting' (2014) — implement CSCV/PBO and DSR as a mandatory gate in the R3 evidence layer before accepting any sizing comparison • DeMiguel, Garlappi, Uppal (2009) full paper — read the appendix tables for the exact estimation-window lengths needed for each model to beat 1/N; sobering and load-bearing • Moreira & Muir 'Volatility-Managed Portfolios' (2017, J. Finance) vs Cederburg, O'Doherty, Wang, Yan 'On the Performance of Volatility-Managed Portfolios' (2020, JFE) — read these two head-to-head; the disagreement IS the honest state of vol-targeting evidence • Roncalli, 'Introduction to Risk Parity and Budgeting' (2013, free PDF on thierry-roncalli.com) — the definitive risk-parity / risk-budgeting algorithm reference with the convex-optimization formulation and code • Grinold-Kahn 'Active Portfolio Management' Ch.6 + Ding & Martin 'The Fundamental Law of Active Management: Redux' (2017) — for the honest, deflated view of breadth and signal combination (real breadth << name count) • Menchero & Lee, 'Efficiently Combining Multiple Sources of Alpha' (J. Investment Management, 2015) — the optimal-signal-combination math (de-correlate signals via Omega^-1), the extension of the Fundamental Law to correlated alphas • PyPortfolioOpt + skfolio + mlfinlab libraries — production implementations of shrinkage, HRP, NCO, BL, risk parity; read their source to see the exact recipes, but VALIDATE independently (don't trust library defaults blindly, esp. HRP linkage) • Jagannathan & Ma (2003) 'Why Imposing the Wrong Constraints Helps' — the proof that long-only constraints ≈ shrinkage; the cheapest robustification you can apply before reaching for fancy estimators

---
## backtest-rigor-deep — Anti-overfitting and backtest-rigor gating (CPCV, Deflated Sharpe, PBO/CSCV, Harvey-Liu multiple-testing haircuts, Minimum Backtest Length, the seven sins). This is NOT an alpha family; it is the meta-layer that decides whether any alpha in R5 is allowed to graduate to paper/live. It deepens IG-12 (the backtest-validation / overfitting-gate prompt) and supplies the quantitative acceptance gates that sit between R5 (alpha) and risk/portfolio/execution.

**Thesis.** The "edge" here is not a market edge — it is an epistemic edge: the structural reason most published/backtested strategies fail live is that the in-sample Sharpe is a maximum over a (usually undeclared) number of trials N, and the maximum of N noise draws is large even when every true Sharpe is zero. Bailey-Borwein-López de Prado-Zhu prove that E[max Sharpe] over N independent zero-edge trials grows ~ sqrt(2·ln N): with ~1000 trials on pure random walks you expect a best in-sample Sharpe near 3.0, and with only ~45 independent configs on 5 years of data you can almost guarantee an annualized in-sample SR of 1.0 whose true out-of-sample SR is 0. This persists because (1) compute makes trying millions of configs trivial, (2) the number of trials is almost never reported, so investors cannot deflate, and (3) selection-under-multiplicity bias is invisible in a single pretty equity curve. The family does NOT decay — it is a mathematical identity, not a traded anomaly. What DOES decay is the alpha it gates: McLean-Pontiff (2016) show post-publication anomaly returns fall ~26% in-sample-to-OOS and ~58% post-publication (32% pure publication/crowding effect on top of statistical bias); Hou-Xue-Zhang (2020) fail to replicate ~65% of 452 anomalies under microcap-robust methods. The honest counterweight: Jensen-Kelly-Pedersen (2023) replicate ~82% of 153 factors using hierarchical Bayesian shrinkage and economic-theme clustering — i.e. the 'replication crisis' is partly a multiple-testing-correction artifact itself, and over-aggressive haircuts (naive Bonferroni) destroy real factors. The discipline is therefore two-sided: deflate hard enough to kill data-mined ghosts, but use FDR (not FWER) and effective-N (not literal-N) so you do not also kill the real, economically-motivated signals.

**Concrete strategies:**
- **Deflated Sharpe Ratio (DSR) gate — the primary graduation metric**
  - *Signal:* Compute observed (non-annualized, per-period) Sharpe SR_hat over T periods. Estimate the expected maximum Sharpe under the null of zero true edge across the trials you ran: SR0 = sqrt(V) · [ (1-γ)·Z^{-1}(1 - 1/N) + γ·Z^{-1}(1 - 1/(N·e)) ], where V = cross-trial variance of the trial Sharpes, γ≈0.5772 (Euler-Mascheroni), N = effective number of independent trials, Z^{-1} = inverse normal CDF, e≈2.718. Then DSR = Φ[ (SR_hat - SR0)·sqrt(T-1) / sqrt(1 - skew·SR_hat + ((kurt-1)/4)·SR_hat^2) ]. PASS if DSR > 0.95. The denominator is the non-normality-corrected standard error of the Sharpe (Mertens/Lo); skew<0 and fat tails INFLATE the SE and LOWER DSR — correctly penalizing option-like return profiles.
  - *Params:* Threshold DSR>0.95 (one-sided 95%). T = number of return observations (more is better; this is the sample-length term). N = EFFECTIVE independent trials — NOT the literal config count; estimate via clustering (K-means / correlation-distance hierarchical clustering on the trial PnL matrix) to collapse e.g. 150 correlated configs to K≈10-20 effective. V from the trial Sharpe dispersion. skew, kurt from the chosen strategy's own returns.  ·  *Assets:* Asset-class agnostic — operates on the PnL/return series, not prices. Works for equity factors, futures trend, crypto stat-arb, options. The non-normality terms matter MOST for negatively-skewed strategies (carry, short-vol, short-gamma) where naive Sharpe most overstates.
  - *Evidence (honest):* In-sample-correction method, not a traded result — its 'evidence' is the False Strategy Theorem proof (Bailey-LdP 2014) and the 2024 synthetic-environment study (Arian-Norouzi-Seco, Knowledge-Based Systems / SSRN 4686376) showing CPCV+DSR achieves the lowest PBO and highest valid DSR among OOS methods. Honest caveat: DSR is only as good as the N you feed it — understate N (the universal temptation) and DSR is meaninglessly inflated. This is the metric's single biggest failure mode.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Does not decay (mathematical identity). The risk is not crowding but mis-application: practitioners report literal-N or N=1, which defeats the deflation. The honest discipline is logging EVERY trial (including abandoned ones) so N is truthful.
- **Probability of Backtest Overfitting (PBO) via CSCV — the selection-process gate**
  - *Signal:* Build the T×N matrix M of per-period PnL across all N candidate configs. Partition rows into S disjoint equal blocks (even S). Form all C(S, S/2) ways to pick S/2 blocks as IS (train) and the complement as OOS (test). For each split: rank the N configs IS, pick the IS-best n*, find its OOS relative rank ω = rank(n*)/(N+1) ∈ (0,1), compute logit λ = ln(ω/(1-ω)). PBO = fraction of splits with λ<0 (i.e. IS-best fell below OOS median). Also yields a performance-degradation scatter (IS vs OOS Sharpe slope — negative slope = overfit) and probability-of-loss OOS.
  - *Params:* S = 16 is the canonical choice (gives C(16,8)=12,870 splits — the paper says 12,780, modern implementations use 12,870). Each IS/OOS half is T/2 long. PASS rule of thumb: PBO < 0.5 minimally, PBO < 0.2 for confidence. Requires N≥2 configs (it judges the selection among configs, so single-strategy work uses DSR instead). Embargo/purge the block boundaries if labels overlap.  ·  *Assets:* Agnostic; needs only the multi-config PnL matrix. Best when you genuinely swept many configs (the thing it audits). For a single hand-built strategy with no sweep, PBO is undefined/uninformative — use DSR + MinBTL.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Model-free framework (Bailey-Borwein-LdP-Zhu 2017, J. Computational Finance). CRAN 'pbo' package and mlfinlab implement it. 2024 Arian et al. synthetic study confirms PBO discriminates overfit from genuine signals better than walk-forward. Caveat: CSCV blocks assume returns are reshuffleable across blocks (IID-ish within performance metric) — for path-dependent metrics (max drawdown) block ORDER matters and naive CSCV understates risk.
  - *Decay/crowding:* No decay. Critique (2024-2025): the C(S,S/2) paths heavily OVERLAP, so they are not independent draws — PBO's sampling distribution is narrower than it looks, and the method assumes the future recombines the past (cannot detect a genuine regime break). Treat PBO as a relative overfitting score, not a calibrated probability.
- **Combinatorial Purged Cross-Validation (CPCV) — the OOS-estimation engine that feeds DSR/PBO**
  - *Signal:* Split the timeline into N sequential groups; choose k as the test groups per split. Generate all C(N,k) train/test combinations. In each: PURGE from train any observation whose label horizon overlaps the test window, and EMBARGO a buffer of observations immediately AFTER each test block (to kill serial-correlation leakage). Each group ends up tested in φ = (k/N)·C(N,k) distinct combinations, so you reconstruct φ full-length backtest PATHS instead of one. Distribution of path Sharpes → feed mean/variance into DSR and the PnL matrix into PBO.
  - *Params:* N total groups (e.g. 6–10), k test groups (e.g. 2). Worked example: N=6, k=2 → C(6,2)=15 combinations → φ=(2/6)·15 = 5 backtest paths. Embargo h ≈ 1% of bars (LdP default; some use 5%·T illustratively) — set h ≥ the label/feature decay horizon. Purge window = max label-formation horizon (e.g. triple-barrier vertical-barrier length). More paths = tighter OOS Sharpe distribution but factorially more compute.  ·  *Assets:* Designed for ML-on-financial-features (overlapping labels are the core problem it solves) but applies to any strategy with overlapping signal/holding windows. Requires LONG history — impractical for short-lived assets (new ETFs, young crypto) where N groups would be too short.
  - *Evidence (honest):* LdP (2018) AFML Ch.12; skfolio.CombinatorialPurgedCV and mlfinlab implement it. 2024 synthetic-controlled study (Arian-Norouzi-Seco) finds CPCV materially beats walk-forward and standard k-fold on overfitting metrics. Honest caveat: walk-forward remains the industry standard for REALISTIC sequential trading simulation (CPCV trains on future-then-past blocks, which is fine for parameter robustness but is NOT a deployable trading sequence). Use CPCV for robustness/validation, walk-forward for the deployment dry-run.
  - *Decay/crowding:* No decay (a validation method). Limitation: cannot anticipate structural breaks unseen in history; overlapping paths inflate apparent path-count; high compute cost. Purge/embargo must be tuned to the actual label horizon or leakage persists.
- **Harvey-Liu multiple-testing haircut — the cross-strategy / factor-zoo gate**
  - *Signal:* For a candidate with Sharpe SR over T periods: t = SR·sqrt(T), single-test p = 2·(1-CDF_t(t)). Adjust for M total tests: Bonferroni p_adj = min(M·p, 1); Holm (step-down, less harsh); BHY/Benjamini-Hochberg-Yekutieli (FDR, controls false-discovery proportion, uses c(M)=Σ_{j=1..M}(1/j) normalizer for arbitrary dependence). Convert adjusted p back: t_adj = CDF_t^{-1}(1 - p_adj/2), haircut Sharpe HSR = t_adj/sqrt(T), haircut% = (SR - HSR)/SR. PASS if HSR survives at your chosen significance.
  - *Params:* M = number of strategies/factors tested (HLZ document 316+ equity factors → use M in the hundreds for a crowded space). Significance 0.05 (or 0.10). Harvey-Liu ADVOCATE BHY (FDR) over Bonferroni/Holm (FWER) for finance — FWER is too punitive when you tolerate some false discoveries. Recommended bar for a NEW factor given the zoo: t-stat > 3.0 (≈ HLZ), not the classic 2.0. Empirical anchors (Exhibit 1): E/P SR=0.43 → haircut 26.6% @M=10, 50.0% @M=50, 61.6% @M=100; BAB SR=0.78 → 4.6%/7.9%/9.3%.  ·  *Assets:* Cross-sectional equity factors are the native domain (the 'factor zoo'), but applies to any family where many candidates were screened. Use it at the PORTFOLIO/family level (how many strategies did the whole shop test?), distinct from DSR which deflates within one research project's trials.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Harvey-Liu (2015) + Harvey-Liu-Zhu (2016, RFS '…and the Cross-Section of Expected Returns'). The NONLINEARITY is the key empirical finding: 50% rule-of-thumb is too lenient for SR<0.4 and too harsh for SR>1.0 — marginal strategies are killed, exceptional ones barely touched. R code at people.duke.edu/~charvey/backtesting. Honest tension: Jensen-Kelly-Pedersen (2023) show naive haircuts over-reject — use effective-M with theme clustering, not literal-M.
  - *Decay/crowding:* The METHOD does not decay. The factors it gates DO: McLean-Pontiff (2016) ~58% post-publication decay, ~32% pure crowding. So a factor that barely clears the haircut today is the MOST likely to be crowded away — the haircut and the decay literature should be applied together: require margin above the bar, not bare passage.
- **Minimum Backtest Length (MinBTL) gate + the 7 sins data-hygiene checklist**
  - *Signal:* Before trusting any IS Sharpe, check the sample is long enough for the number of trials run: MinBTL (in years) ≈ [ ( (1-γ)·Z^{-1}(1-1/N) + γ·Z^{-1}(1-1/(N·e)) ) / E[max SR_target] ]^2, where γ≈0.5772, N=independent trials, E[max SR] grows ≈ sqrt(2·ln N). Interpretation: with 5 years of data, trying >~45 independent configs almost guarantees a spurious IS SR=1 with true OOS SR=0; on pure random walks, ~1000 trials yields expected best IS Sharpe ≈ 3.0. Separately, run the 7-sins pre-flight: survivorship, look-ahead, storytelling, data-snooping, turnover/cost, outlier-dependence, shorting-cost/asymmetry.
  - *Params:* Inputs: N (effective trials), target annualized Sharpe. Decision: if your actual backtest length < MinBTL(N, SR), the result is statistically uninterpretable — REJECT regardless of how good it looks. The 7 sins each map to a binary gate: CRSP/delisting-inclusive universe (survivorship), point-in-time data with release lags (look-ahead), pre-registered hypothesis (storytelling), train/test isolation + DSR (snooping), cost model proportional to turnover (cost), winsorize/trim + jackknife on top contributors (outliers), realistic borrow cost & availability (shorting).  ·  *Assets:* Universal. The 7 sins are most violated in equities (survivorship via delistings, microcap illiquidity, short-borrow) and crypto (look-ahead via revised/backfilled data, survivorship via dead tokens, fake volume).
  - *Evidence (honest):* MinBTL from Bailey-Borwein-LdP-Zhu (2014, AMS). The '45 configs / 5 years' and 'best of 1000 random walks ≈ Sharpe 3' numbers are the paper's headline results — used to scare researchers into reporting N. 7 sins from Deutsche Bank (Luo et al. 2014). Honest caveat: MinBTL assumes independent trials and zero true edge — it is a NECESSARY (not sufficient) condition; passing it does not certify the strategy, it only rules out the most obvious data-mining ghost.
  - *Decay/crowding:* No decay. The 7 sins are perennial; survivorship and look-ahead in particular re-enter every time a new data vendor or asset class is onboarded (R4). The MinBTL bound tightens automatically as compute lets you try more configs — the more you search, the longer the history you need.

**Canonical sources:**
- *Pseudo-Mathematics and Financial Charlatanism: The Effects of Backtest Overfitting on Out-of-Sample Performance* — David H. Bailey, Jonathan Borwein, Marcos López de Prado, Qiji Jim Zhu (2014, paper): Notices of the AMS, May 2014. The mathematical proof that backtest overfitting is near-certain under modest trial counts. Source of the Minimum Backtest Length (MinBTL) result and the 'best of N random walks gives Sharpe ~ sqrt(2 ln N)' identity. The canonical '5 years / 45 configs / SR=1 IS but 0 OOS' bound. Read this first to internalize WHY the gate exists. — https://www.ams.org/notices/201405/rnoti-p458.pdf
- *The Deflated Sharpe Ratio: Correcting for Selection Bias, Backtest Overfitting and Non-Normality* — David H. Bailey, Marcos López de Prado (2014, paper): Journal of Portfolio Management 40(5). Defines the Probabilistic Sharpe Ratio (PSR) and Deflated Sharpe Ratio (DSR) with the False Strategy Theorem for E[max Sharpe]. The single most implementable gate: one number in [0,1], DSR>0.95 to pass. Folds in sample length, skew, kurtosis AND number/variance of trials. This is the workhorse acceptance metric. — https://www.davidhbailey.com/dhbpapers/deflated-sharpe.pdf
- *The Probability of Backtest Overfitting* — David H. Bailey, Jonathan Borwein, Marcos López de Prado, Qiji Jim Zhu (2017, paper): Journal of Computational Finance 20(4). Defines PBO via Combinatorially Symmetric Cross-Validation (CSCV): split the T×N performance matrix into S row-blocks, form all C(S,S/2) IS/OOS swaps, compute the logit λ of the IS-best strategy's OOS rank, and PBO = P(λ<0) = fraction of swaps where the IS-best lands below the OOS median. Model-free, needs only the per-trial PnL matrix. Complements DSR: DSR judges the winner, PBO judges the selection process. — https://www.davidhbailey.com/dhbpapers/backtest-prob.pdf
- *Backtesting (the Haircut Sharpe Ratio paper)* — Campbell R. Harvey, Yan Liu (2015, paper): Journal of Portfolio Management 42(1). The multiple-testing haircut: converts SR→t-stat→p-value, applies Bonferroni / Holm / Benjamini-Hochberg-Yekutieli (BHY) adjustment for M tests, converts back to a haircut Sharpe. Proves the '50% haircut rule of thumb' is wrong — the haircut is NONLINEAR (>50% for SR<0.4, <25% for SR>1.0). Advocates BHY (FDR) over Bonferroni (FWER) for finance. Worked Exhibit 1: E/P SR=0.43 gets 26.6% haircut at N=10 but 61.6% at N=100; BAB SR=0.78 only 9.3% even at N=100. — https://people.duke.edu/~charvey/Research/Published_Papers/P120_Backtesting.PDF
- *Evaluating Trading Strategies* — Campbell R. Harvey, Yan Liu (2014, paper): Journal of Portfolio Management 40(5), 40th-anniversary issue. The practitioner-facing companion to Backtesting: a step-by-step protocol for real-time evaluation of candidate strategies under multiplicity. Pairs with the '...and the Cross-Section of Expected Returns' (HLZ 2016, RFS) which documents 316+ factors and argues a new factor needs t>3.0 (not 2.0) to clear the multiple-testing bar. — https://people.duke.edu/~charvey/Research/Published_Papers/P116_Evaluating_trading_strategies.pdf
- *Advances in Financial Machine Learning (Ch. 7, 11, 12, 14)* — Marcos López de Prado (2018, book): The implementation bible. Ch.7 = Purged K-Fold + embargo; Ch.11/12 = Combinatorial Purged Cross-Validation (CPCV) and the multiple-backtest-paths idea; Ch.14 = backtest statistics incl. DSR/PSR. Also the '10 Reasons Most ML Funds Fail' / '7 reasons backtests fail' taxonomy (walk-forward fragility, stationarity-vs-memory via fractional differentiation d≈0.35, sample uniqueness weighting for non-IID data, triple-barrier labeling, CV leakage). The reference for code-level CPCV/purge/embargo. — https://www.wiley.com/en-us/Advances+in+Financial+Machine+Learning-p-9781119482086
- *The Seven Sins of Quantitative Investing* — Luo, Alvarez, Wang, Jussa, Wang, Rohal (Deutsche Bank Markets Research) (2014, paper): The practitioner checklist that predates and complements LdP: (1) survivorship bias, (2) look-ahead bias, (3) storytelling, (4) data-snooping/overfitting, (5) ignoring turnover & transaction cost, (6) outlier dependence, (7) asymmetric/shorting-cost assumptions. Each maps to a concrete data-pipeline guardrail in R4/execution. Note: this is a different list from McLean-Pontiff or LdP — cite Deutsche Bank Luo et al. as the origin, do not conflate. — https://portfoliooptimizationbook.com/book/8.2-seven-sins.html
- *Is There a Replication Crisis in Finance?* — Theis Ingerslev Jensen, Bryan Kelly, Lasse Heje Pedersen (2023, paper): Journal of Finance 78(5). The essential counterweight to overfitting-paranoia: hierarchical Bayesian model on 153 factors × 93 countries replicates ~82% (vs Hou-Xue-Zhang's ~35%). Shows that naive multiple-testing haircuts (and microcap-equal-weighting) can OVER-reject real factors, and that economic-theme clustering + shrinkage is the right way to estimate effective-N. Open data/code at jkpfactors.com — directly usable as an R4 factor-data source AND as a sanity benchmark for the gate's false-negative rate. — https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/jofi.13249

**Pitfalls:** (1) Understating N is the cardinal sin and it silently destroys every gate. DSR, PBO and the haircut all need an HONEST trial count. Reporting N=1 (or 'the configs I kept') inflates DSR toward 1.0 and shrinks the haircut toward 0. The fix is process, not math: log EVERY trial — including abandoned sweeps, manual tweaks, and 'I just eyeballed a better lookback' — into R3 evidence. The thing the operator most wants to hide is the thing the gate most needs. (2) Confusing literal-N with effective-N over-deflates real signals. 150 configs that are 90% correlated are NOT 150 independent trials. Naive deflation here produces false negatives and kills genuine alpha (the Jensen-Kelly-Pedersen failure mode of crude haircuts). Always collapse correlated trials via clustering (correlation-distance hierarchical or K-means on the trial PnL matrix) before computing N for DSR or M for the haircut. (3) Using FWER (Bonferroni) when you should use FDR (BHY). Bonferroni is designed for 'one false positive is catastrophic' (space missions). In a fund you tolerate some false discoveries to avoid missing real ones — Harvey-Liu explicitly advocate BHY. Bonferroni at M=300 will reject almost everything including true factors. (4) Treating PBO and DSR as interchangeable. DSR judges whether the SELECTED winner is real given the trials; PBO judges whether the SELECTION PROCESS is overfit (does IS-best generalize OOS?). A strategy can pass DSR but the research process still have high PBO (you got lucky on the winner). Run both; they answer different questions. (5) Forgetting non-normality flips the verdict on skewed strategies. The DSR/PSR denominator inflates with negative skew and excess kurtosis. Carry, short-vol, mean-reversion and most 'smooth equity curve' strategies have exactly the toxic skew that naive Sharpe ignores — they look best on raw Sharpe and worst on DSR. That is the metric working correctly; do not 'fix' it. (6) CPCV is a robustness tool, not a deployment simulation. It trains on future-then-past blocks, so its paths are not a tradeable sequence. Walk-forward (despite single-path fragility) is still needed for the realistic sequential dry-run, cost/slippage accrual, and capacity. Use CPCV for parameter robustness + feeding DSR/PBO; use walk-forward for the go-live rehearsal. Reporting only one is incomplete. (7) Purge/embargo mis-set = leakage survives. If the embargo is shorter than the feature/label decay horizon, serial-correlation leakage inflates OOS performance even with 'purged' CV. Set purge ≥ max label-formation horizon and embargo ≥ the autocorrelation/decay length of the features — do not blindly use the 1% default. (8) MinBTL and the haircut are NECESSARY, not SUFFICIENT. Passing them only rules out the obvious data-mining ghost. They say nothing about regime-dependence, capacity, crowding, or a structural break the history never saw. A strategy can clear every statistical gate and still die live because the world changed (the CPCV 'future recombines the past' assumption). (9) The decay literature must be applied WITH the gate, not after. A strategy that BARELY clears the haircut is, by McLean-Pontiff, the most likely to already be crowded and decaying. Require margin above the bar (e.g. DSR>0.95 AND haircut-survived SR > some economic floor), not bare passage — bare passage is where the false positives and the decayed signals both cluster.

**Honest validation:** (1) The anti-Profit-Mirage protocol, in order: (1) PRE-REGISTER the hypothesis and the full config grid before touching test data (defeats storytelling + snooping). (2) Run CPCV with purge+embargo tuned to the label horizon to get a DISTRIBUTION of OOS path-Sharpes, not a point estimate. (3) Compute PBO via CSCV (S=16) on the full trial matrix — require PBO < 0.2. (4) Compute DSR on the selected strategy using EFFECTIVE-N (cluster-collapsed) — require DSR > 0.95. (5) Apply the Harvey-Liu BHY haircut at the family level using effective-M — require the haircut-survived Sharpe to clear an economic floor with MARGIN. (6) Check the backtest length exceeds MinBTL(N). Only a strategy clearing ALL SIX graduates from R5 to risk/portfolio. (2) Net-of-everything before any gate: compute every Sharpe on returns net of realistic transaction cost (proportional to turnover), market impact, borrow cost, and financing. An IS Sharpe gate on gross returns is theater — the 7-sins 'turnover & cost' and 'shorting cost' sins kill more strategies than statistics do. Build the cost model in execution (R6) and pipe net returns back to R3/R5. (3) Deflate against the TRUE trial count by logging trials as first-class R3 evidence. Every backtest run (kept or discarded, scripted or manual) writes a row: config hash, date, IS Sharpe, OOS Sharpe, PnL series pointer. N for DSR and M for the haircut are read from this ledger, not from memory. This is the single highest-leverage control — it makes the deflation honest. (4) Hold a true, never-touched lockbox of the most RECENT data. Despite CPCV's no-holdout philosophy, keep a final out-of-time slice (most recent ~10-20%) that the research process literally cannot see until the strategy is otherwise approved. If OOS-on-lockbox Sharpe collapses vs CPCV-path Sharpe, that gap IS the overfitting estimate. Recent data is the most representative going forward (LdP's argument), so lockbox the END, not the beginning. (5) Benchmark the gate's false-negative rate against known-good factors. Periodically run the full gate on the Jensen-Kelly-Pedersen replicated themes (open data at jkpfactors.com) — momentum, value, quality, low-risk. If your gate rejects factors that JKP show replicate out-of-sample across 93 countries, your N/M is too aggressive (over-deflation). This calibrates the gate between Type-I (ghosts) and Type-II (killing real alpha). (6) Stress the gate against synthetic nulls. Generate random-walk / bootstrapped-null PnL matrices with the SAME T, N, and autocorrelation as your real research, run them through DSR+PBO, and confirm the gate rejects them at the expected rate. This is the 'does my gate actually catch overfitting' meta-test (mirrors the 2024 Arian-Norouzi-Seco synthetic-environment methodology). A gate that passes pure noise is mis-calibrated. (7) Report decay-adjusted expectations, not backtest Sharpe. After graduation, haircut the EXPECTED live Sharpe by the McLean-Pontiff factor (~26% IS→OOS for un-published edges, up to ~58% for anything resembling a published anomaly) when sizing in portfolio (R5→portfolio). The number you trade on should already assume decay.

**Data:** "Inputs the gate consumes (mostly derived, not raw market data): (1) Per-trial PnL/return SERIES for every config tested — a T×N matrix — this is the core artifact for CPCV, PBO/CSCV, and DSR's trial-variance V. (2) A complete TRIAL LEDGER (config, date, IS/OOS Sharpe) to recover honest N and M. (3) The selected strategy's return moments: mean, std, skewness, kurtosis (DSR denominator). (4) Label/feature decay horizons to set purge and embargo windows. Underlying raw-data quality is upstream in R4 and is what the 7 sins police: point-in-time / as-released data with vendor release lags (kills look-ahead); survivorship-free universe including delistings/dead-tokens (CRSP-equivalent for equities, full token history for crypto); realistic borrow availability & cost and short-locate data; turnover-proportional transaction-cost and market-impact model (lives in R6/execution, fed back). Long history is mandatory for CPCV (enough groups N) and to clear MinBTL — short-lived assets (new ETFs, young crypto) are structurally hard to validate and should carry a flag. For benchmarking the gate's false-negative rate, the open JKP global factor dataset (jkpfactors.com, 153 factors × 93 countries) is directly usable in R4."

**Fits our stack:** "This family is the GOVERNANCE GATE between R5 (alpha) and risk/portfolio/execution — it is the quantitative implementation of the project's own 'anti-Profit-Mirage' / 'verify before claiming done' discipline applied to strategies. Concretely: R3 (evidence) gains a TRIAL LEDGER as a first-class object — every backtest, kept or discarded, is logged so N and M are honest; this is the same 'log every trial, deflate against true count' move as the codebase's memory-graph-everything rule, and it is where DSR/PBO/haircut read their inputs. R4 (data) owns the 7-sins guardrails (point-in-time, survivorship-free, borrow/cost data) and can mount the JKP open factor dataset both as a data source and as the gate's false-negative calibration benchmark. R5 (alpha) is where CPCV runs to produce OOS path distributions instead of single equity curves, and where a strategy must clear the six-gate protocol (CPCV → PBO<0.2 → DSR>0.95 on effective-N → BHY haircut with margin → MinBTL → net-of-cost) before it may graduate. Risk/portfolio consumes DECAY-ADJUSTED expected Sharpe (McLean-Pontiff haircut) for sizing, not the rosy backtest number. Execution (R6) supplies the cost/borrow model that makes every gate net-of-everything and runs the walk-forward deployment rehearsal that CPCV deliberately is NOT. This deepens IG-12 (the backtest-validation / overfitting-gate prompt): IG-12 should no longer ask 'is the backtest good?' but enforce the concrete pipeline — demand the trial ledger, require effective-N via clustering, compute DSR+PBO+haircut+MinBTL with stated thresholds, mandate a never-touched recent lockbox, and report decay-adjusted live expectations. The whole family is the embodiment of the stack's verification-loop-as-load-bearing-primitive principle: a strategy is 'done' only when the gate returns green, not when the equity curve looks good."

**Go deeper:** • Implement and unit-test the four gates from canonical code: mlfinlab (CPCV, PurgedKFold, embargo, PBO) and skfolio.model_selection.CombinatorialPurgedCV; cross-check DSR/PSR against rubenbriones/Probabilistic-Sharpe-Ratio and braverock/quantstrat SharpeRatio.deflated / SharpeRatio.haircut (R). Build a single dharma_capital gate module that takes the T×N PnL matrix and returns {DSR, PBO, haircut_SR, MinBTL_pass}. • Read Harvey-Liu-Zhu (2016) '…and the Cross-Section of Expected Returns' (RFS) for the t>3.0 new-factor bar and the structural model of the factor-test distribution that underlies the haircut; pair with Harvey's 2017 AFA Presidential Address 'The Scientific Outlook in Financial Economics' (p-hacking, pre-registration for finance). • Resolve the replication-crisis tension empirically: Hou-Xue-Zhang (2020, RFS) 'Replicating Anomalies' (~35% survive, microcap-robust) vs Jensen-Kelly-Pedersen (2023, JF) (~82% via Bayesian shrinkage). Decide the house policy on effective-M, microcap weighting, and theme clustering — this directly sets how aggressive the gate is. • Study the effective-number-of-trials problem in depth: López de Prado & Lewis on clustering trials to estimate independent N (the input DSR is most sensitive to). Implement correlation-distance hierarchical clustering on the trial PnL matrix as the canonical effective-N estimator. • Read the 2024 synthetic-controlled comparison (Arian, Norouzi M., Seco — Knowledge-Based Systems / SSRN 4686376) for the empirical case that CPCV+DSR beats walk-forward and k-fold on PBO/DSR, AND for the honest limitations (overlapping-path non-independence, regime-break blindness, compute cost). Use its methodology to build the stack's own synthetic-null calibration harness. • Internalize the McLean-Pontiff (2016, JF) 'Does Academic Research Destroy Stock Return Predictability?' decomposition (~26% IS→OOS statistical decay, ~32% additional post-publication crowding) to set the decay haircut used in portfolio sizing — and Chordia-Goyal-Saretto (2020) on data-mining the cross-section as a further skeptic anchor. • For the implementation-rigor layer, work through AFML chapters 7/11/12/14 directly (purge/embargo code, multiple backtest paths, backtest statistics) and the AlgoXpert / interpretable walk-forward arXiv frameworks (2603.09219, 2512.12924) as worked end-to-end IS→WFA→OOS protocols to template R5's pipeline.

---
## canonical-reading-list

**Thesis.** This family is not a strategy — it is the prior-distribution that determines whether every downstream strategy is real or noise. The economic "edge" here is meta: most published systematic edges are partly or wholly illusory (data-mined, in-sample, gross-of-cost), and the operators who survive are the ones who internalized the validation literature BEFORE building. The canon splits cleanly into two layers that must be read in the right order. Layer 1 (the WHY edges exist and decay): Ilmanen's "Expected Returns" and Grinold-Kahn's "Active Portfolio Management" supply the risk-premia/behavioral economics (why value, momentum, carry, defensive, trend earn returns — some rational risk compensation, some behavioral under/over-reaction) and the algebra of skill (IR = IC x sqrt(breadth)). Layer 2 (the HOW NOT to fool yourself): Lopez de Prado's three books + his meta-papers (Deflated Sharpe Ratio, Probability of Backtest Overfitting, "10 Reasons ML Funds Fail") plus Harvey-Liu-Zhu (factor zoo, t>3.0 not t>2.0) and McLean-Pontiff (returns 26% lower out-of-sample, 58% lower post-publication) are the antidote to the entire factor zoo. The deepest economic claim of the canon: published alpha decays because (a) ~one-third of in-sample edge was statistical mining (vanishes out-of-sample) and (b) another chunk is arbitraged away by investors who read the paper (vanishes post-publication). Anything you build on a famous published signal is, on average, trading a half-strength version of a result that was already optimistic. The canon's job is to make that fact reflexive, not surprising.

**Concrete strategies:**
- **Cross-sectional equity value (HML / book-to-market and beyond)**
  - *Signal:* Rank a cross-section on a cheapness metric; long the cheap decile, short the expensive. Original: book-to-market (Fama-French HML). Modern composite value blends B/P, E/P, FCF/EV, sales/EV to reduce single-ratio fragility and the 'value trap' from accounting distortions. Economic basis (Ilmanen): part rational (distress/risk premium) part behavioral (extrapolation of past growth).
  - *Params:* Universe: large/mid-cap, exclude micro-caps and financials/utilities for clean accounting. Rebalance monthly-to-quarterly. Form deciles or quintiles; often long-only tilt or 130/30 rather than full short. Lag fundamentals 3-6 months to avoid look-ahead on filing dates.  ·  *Assets:* Equities (cross-section); generalizes cross-asset as 'value' = mean-reversion to fundamental anchor (e.g. real exchange rate, real bond yield, commodity 5yr-normalized price).
  - *Evidence (honest):* Decades of in-sample support; BUT 2010s 'value drought' (~2007-2020) was the worst on record and triggered serious 'is value dead?' debate. Honest read: value's long-run premium is real but its Sharpe is modest (~0.3-0.4 standalone, gross), drawdowns are decade-long, and net-of-cost the small/illiquid stocks that carried much of the premium are hard to trade at scale.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Partially decayed/repriced; the simple B/P version is heavily crowded and arguably impaired by intangibles mismeasurement (R&D/brand not on balance sheet). Composite and 'value-spread-timed' versions hold up better. Treat single-ratio value as a teaching example, not a live edge.
- **Cross-sectional momentum (12-1)**
  - *Signal:* Rank assets on trailing 12-month return SKIPPING the most recent month (the 12-1 window — the skip avoids the 1-month short-term reversal). Long winners, short losers. Behavioral basis: under-reaction then delayed over-reaction to news.
  - *Params:* Formation: t-12 to t-2 cumulative return. Holding: 1 month, monthly rebalance. Skip-month is load-bearing — including the last month flips sign for short-horizon reversal. Vol-target each leg. Momentum crashes (2009, 2003) are severe; a dynamic-scaling or 'momentum turbulence' overlay materially improves the Sharpe.  ·  *Assets:* Equities (Jegadeesh-Titman); also industries, countries, FX, commodities, bonds (cross-asset momentum, AQR 'everywhere').
  - *Evidence (honest):* One of the most robust anomalies across markets and time, strong t-stats even post-multiple-testing-adjustment. BUT: negatively skewed (rare violent crashes eat years of gains), turnover is high so net-of-cost erosion is real, and the short leg is the hardest/most-expensive to implement.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Premium survives post-publication better than most (McLean-Pontiff: momentum decayed less than average), but it is crowded and its crash risk is a feature not a bug — the risk is why the premium persists. Net Sharpe after realistic costs and borrow is well below gross.
- **Time-series momentum / trend following (TSMOM)**
  - *Signal:* For each instrument independently: if trailing-k-month return > 0 go long, else short, sized to a constant volatility target. Aggregate equally across a diversified futures universe. The diversification across uncorrelated trends + vol-scaling is the engine.
  - *Params:* Lookback: 12m canonical (MOP 2012); production blends 1/3/12m (fast+slow). Vol target per instrument ~ constant (e.g. 40% annualized pre-aggregation, scaled to ~10% portfolio). Universe: 50-100+ liquid futures (equity indices, govies, FX, commodities). Rebalance daily-to-monthly.  ·  *Assets:* Futures/forwards across all liquid asset classes — the classic CTA/managed-futures product.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Strong in-sample, and notably positive 'crisis alpha' (long-vol-like payoff in sustained selloffs, e.g. 2008). HONEST CAVEAT: published critiques show a large share of the headline alpha is mechanical — the vol-scaling acts like dynamic leverage, and without it TSMOM looks much closer to diversified buy-and-hold. Live CTA index Sharpes have been ~0.3-0.5 net over the last decade, well below backtest.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Materially decayed since ~2010; the 2010s were a poor decade for trend (whipsaw, central-bank suppression of trends). Heavily crowded by CTAs (~$300B+). Still valued for the convex crisis-alpha diversification, not standalone Sharpe. The cleanest live case study of backtest-vs-reality gap.
- **Carry (cross-asset)**
  - *Signal:* Go long high-yielding / high-carry instruments, short low-carry, where carry = the return earned if prices don't move. FX: interest-rate differential. Bonds: term-structure roll-down + yield. Commodities: backwardation (futures curve slope). Equities: dividend yield.
  - *Params:* FX: rank ~10-20 currencies by short-rate differential, long top / short bottom, vol-scaled. Commodities: rank by front-vs-deferred futures slope. Rebalance monthly. Carry is notoriously crash-prone (long high-carry FX = short-vol exposure that blows up in risk-off).  ·  *Assets:* FX (the classic), rates/bonds, commodities, equities, credit — Ilmanen treats carry as a unifying lens across all of them.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Robust long-run premium across asset classes (Koijen-Moskowitz-Pedersen-Vrugt 'Carry' 2018). BUT carry is short-volatility / negatively-skewed: it earns small steady gains then suffers sharp drawdowns in crises (2008 FX carry unwind). The premium is substantially compensation for that crash risk, not free.
  - *Decay/crowding:* FX carry specifically has weakened in the post-2008 low-rate-dispersion world (when all rates are near zero, there is little carry to harvest). Crowded. Commodity carry (curve structure) is more idiosyncratic and less decayed. Treat as a risk premium to be sized small, not an alpha.
- **Statistical-arbitrage pairs / cointegration mean-reversion (Chan recipe)**
  - *Signal:* Find a stationary linear combination of 2+ assets (cointegrated spread). Trade the z-score of the spread: short when z high, long when z low, exit near zero. Hedge ratio from Johansen eigenvector (or OLS for a pair). Size position proportional to -z-score.
  - *Params:* Test: ADF/CADF for a pair, Johansen for a basket; confirm with Hurst < 0.5 and variance-ratio. Half-life = -log(2)/lambda from regressing dSpread on lagged Spread — sets your holding horizon and lookback (use ~1 half-life for the rolling mean/std). Entry/exit z thresholds e.g. +/-1 to +/-2 / 0. Re-estimate cointegration on a rolling window — the relationship DOES break.  ·  *Assets:* Equities (sector pairs, ETF-vs-basket), futures calendar spreads, ETF pairs. Best where an economic linkage justifies the cointegration (not pure data-mined pairs).
  - *Evidence (honest):* Gatev-Goetzmann-Rouwenhorst (2006) documented the classic distance-method profitability — which has since LARGELY DECAYED to near-zero net-of-cost after ~2002 as the trade got crowded by stat-arb desks. Cointegration variants and intraday/higher-frequency versions persist but with thin margins.
  - *Decay/crowding:* Heavily decayed and crowded at daily frequency — the textbook example. Surviving edge is execution/cost-driven (lower latency, better borrow) or in less-trafficked universes. Chan himself stresses cointegration breaks down without warning; a live break is a loss, not a richer entry. Trade small, watch for regime breaks, never average down blindly.
- **Defensive / low-volatility (betting-against-beta)**
  - *Signal:* Long low-beta/low-volatility assets, short high-beta, leverage the long leg to beta-neutral (BAB, Frazzini-Pedersen 2014). Or simply overweight low-vol, high-quality, profitable names. Economic basis: leverage-constrained investors overpay for high-beta lottery exposure, leaving low-beta underpriced.
  - *Params:* Rank by trailing beta or realized vol; long-bottom/short-top, lever long leg to neutralize beta. Or quality composite (profitability, low accruals, low leverage, stable earnings) per Asness-Frazzini-Pedersen 'Quality Minus Junk'. Monthly rebalance.  ·  *Assets:* Equities primarily; the low-beta effect also appears across asset classes.
  - *Evidence (honest):* Strong and theoretically grounded (one of the better-replicating anomalies). BUT BAB's leverage assumptions and the long-short construction are sensitive to implementation; the long-only low-vol version is a valuation-dependent bet that became expensive (crowded into 'bond-proxy' low-vol stocks ~2016).
  - *Decay/crowding:* The pure premium persists but low-vol got crowded/expensive as a smart-beta product in the mid-2010s; valuation-aware ('low-vol cheap') versions hold up. Quality is among the most durable and least-crowded of the style premia. A good defensive/diversifying sleeve, not a high-Sharpe standalone.

**Canonical sources:**
- *Advances in Financial Machine Learning* — Marcos Lopez de Prado (2018, book): The single most important book for an ML-driven systematic fund. Introduces the operational toolkit that is now industry-standard: information-driven bars (tick/volume/dollar/imbalance bars instead of time bars), the triple-barrier labeling method, meta-labeling (separate the SIDE model from a SIZE/precision model), fractional differentiation (make a series stationary while keeping maximal memory — most log-price series reach stationarity at d<0.6, so the standard first-difference wipes out memory unnecessarily), sample uniqueness / sequential bootstrap for overlapping labels, purged-and-embargoed k-fold CV, and Combinatorial Purged CV (CPCV).
