Its core thesis: 'backtesting is not a research tool; feature importance is.' Read this second, after you can already validate. — https://www.wiley.com/en-us/Advances+in+Financial+Machine+Learning-p-9781119482086
- *Machine Learning for Asset Managers* — Marcos Lopez de Prado (2020, book): The compact (~140pp) distillation. Best single source on denoising/detoning covariance matrices (Marchenko-Pastur eigenvalue clipping), distance metrics (variation of information vs correlation), optimal clustering for portfolio construction (precursor to HRP/NCO), and the Deflated Sharpe Ratio applied. Cheapest path to the López de Prado worldview; read it FIRST of his three as the on-ramp. — https://www.cambridge.org/core/elements/machine-learning-for-asset-managers/
- *Active Portfolio Management: A Quantitative Approach for Producing Superior Returns and Controlling Risk (2nd ed)* — Richard Grinold & Ronald Kahn (1999, book): The algebra of active management. The Fundamental Law of Active Management — IR = IC x sqrt(BR), refined to IR ≈ TC x IC x sqrt(BR) with the transfer coefficient TC for real-world constraints — tells you WHERE to spend effort: a small edge (IC ~0.05) applied with high breadth (thousands of independent bets/year) beats a large edge applied rarely. This is the back-of-envelope that justifies a systematic fund's existence over a discretionary one. Also the source for residual return/risk, characteristic portfolios, and the alpha = volatility x IC x score scaling. — https://www.amazon.com/Active-Portfolio-Management-Quantitative-Controlling/dp/0070248826
- *Expected Returns: An Investor's Guide to Harvesting Market Rewards* — Antti Ilmanen (2011, book): The encyclopedia of WHY returns exist. ~570pp organized by return drivers rather than asset classes: the equity premium, term/credit/liquidity premia, and the four workhorse style premia (value, momentum, carry, defensive/low-vol). Distinguishes rational (risk, liquidity) from behavioral (extrapolation, overconfidence, lottery preference) sources. The intellectual foundation for cross-asset, multi-style diversification — 'diversify across styles, not just assets, because style premia historically rival the equity premium with low mutual correlation.' Ilmanen's 2022 follow-up 'Investing Amid Low Expected Returns' updates it for the low-yield regime. — https://www.aqr.com/Insights/Research/Book/Expected-Returns-An-Investors-Guide-to-Harvesting-Market-Rewards
- *Algorithmic Trading: Winning Strategies and Their Rationale* — Ernest P. Chan (2013, book): The most implementable of the canon — actual code-able recipes with rationale, not just theory. Definitive practitioner treatment of mean-reversion plumbing: ADF/Hurst/variance-ratio stationarity tests, the half-life of mean reversion via Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (half-life = -log(2)/lambda from regressing dSpread on lagged Spread), Johansen vs CADF cointegration for baskets, and z-score-proportional position sizing. Plus a momentum chapter and an honest 'why strategies stop working' (regime change, crowding) section. Read alongside his earlier 'Quantitative Trading' (2009, the on-ramp) and 'Machine Trading' (2017). — https://www.wiley.com/en-us/Algorithmic+Trading:+Winning+Strategies+and+Their+Rationale-p-9781118460146
- *Inside the Black Box: A Simple Guide to Quantitative and High-Frequency Trading (2nd ed)* — Rishi K. Narang (2013, book): The org-chart of a real quant fund, non-technical. Decomposes a systematic operation into its true modules: alpha model (theory-driven vs data-driven; trend/reversion/value/carry/quality), risk model, transaction-cost model, portfolio-construction model (optimizer vs heuristic), and execution algos — plus the data layer and the three risk classes (model risk, regime-change risk, exogenous-shock risk). The clearest map of how the pieces fit; read FIRST of the whole canon to get vocabulary before depth. — https://www.amazon.com/Inside-Black-Box-Quantitative-Frequency/dp/1118362411
- *...and the Cross-Section of Expected Returns* — Campbell Harvey, Yan Liu, Heqing Zhu (2016 (RFS), paper): The 'factor zoo' paper. Documents 316+ published factors and shows that with that many trials, a t-stat of 2.0 is meaningless — the multiple-testing-adjusted hurdle for a NEW factor is t > ~3.0. The reason your fund must treat any single published anomaly as guilty-until-proven. Pair with Harvey's 'Presidential Address: The Scientific Outlook in Financial Economics' (2017) on p-hacking, and Hou-Xue-Zhang 'Replicating Anomalies' (2020) which finds ~65% of 452 anomalies fail to replicate at standard significance. — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2249314
- *Does Academic Research Destroy Stock Return Predictability?* — R. David McLean & Jeffrey Pontiff (2016 (J. Finance), paper): Quantifies decay with hard numbers on 97 predictors: out-of-sample returns are 26% lower than in-sample, and post-publication returns are 58% lower — implying ~32% of decay is investors trading away the edge after it's published (the rest is mining bias). The empirical backbone of every 'is this signal still alive?' conversation. Decay is worse for high-in-sample-return, high-turnover, liquid-stock signals. — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2156623
- *The Deflated Sharpe Ratio + The Probability of Backtest Overfitting* — David Bailey & Marcos Lopez de Prado (+ Borwein, Zhu for PBO) (2014 / 2015, paper): The two anti-overfitting instruments your validation layer must implement. DSR corrects an observed Sharpe for (a) the number of trials run, (b) variance of trial Sharpes, (c) skew and kurtosis of returns — 'how many backtests did you run to find this?' is a first-class input. PBO uses combinatorially-symmetric CV to estimate the probability the in-sample best config underperforms the median out-of-sample. The Minimum Backtest Length / 'most backtests are false' result (Bailey et al., 2014) is the companion. Open-source impl: esvhd/pypbo. — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2460551
- *Time Series Momentum* — Tobias Moskowitz, Yao Hua Ooi, Lasse Pedersen (2012 (JFE), paper): The canonical trend-following paper and a perfect teaching case in fragility. 12-month-lookback trend on 58 futures across equities/FX/commodities/bonds, vol-scaled to constant risk. BUT subsequent work (e.g., the volatility-scaling critique) shows much of the headline 'alpha' comes from the vol-scaling leverage mechanism, not pure trend — without it, returns resemble buy-and-hold. Include it precisely so the operator sees how a famous result can be real and partly-mechanical at the same time. Pair with Jegadeesh-Titman (1993) for the original cross-sectional momentum, and AQR's 'Value and Momentum Everywhere' (Asness-Moskowitz-Pedersen 2013). — https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2089463

**Pitfalls:** (1) Backtest overfitting / multiple testing — the master pitfall. If you try N configurations and report the best Sharpe, that Sharpe is inflated by selection. Lopez de Prado: with enough trials you can manufacture an arbitrary in-sample Sharpe on random data. ALWAYS track the number of trials and deflate (DSR). t>2.0 is not significance; after the factor zoo, demand t>3.0 (Harvey-Liu-Zhu). (2) Look-ahead / data leakage — using information not available at decision time. Subtle forms: fundamentals dated to period-end rather than filing date; survivorship-biased universes (dead tickers removed); restated financials; CV folds that leak across the label horizon. Lopez de Prado's purging+embargo exists specifically because triple-barrier labels OVERLAP in time and naive k-fold leaks adjacent information. (3) Gross-of-cost / Profit-Mirage — the single most common way retail and naive quant backtests lie. Spreads, commissions, market impact (nonlinear in size), borrow cost and availability for shorts, and slippage often exceed the gross edge. Many famous anomalies (pairs distance method, much of micro-cap value/momentum) are profitable gross and dead net. Always model costs FIRST, before celebrating a Sharpe. (4) Post-publication decay & crowding — McLean-Pontiff: ~26% lower OOS, ~58% lower post-publication. Any signal you read in a paper is on average a half-strength version of an already-optimistic result. Worse for high-turnover, liquid, high-in-sample-return signals. Assume decay; build a live monitor for it. (5) Regime change / non-stationarity — financial series are non-stationary; relationships break (cointegration pairs decouple, trend regimes flip to chop). Fixed parameters tuned to one regime fail in the next. Lopez de Prado's fractional differentiation addresses the stationarity-vs-memory tradeoff; but no amount of differencing fixes a structurally broken relationship. (6) Mistaking mechanical leverage for alpha — the TSMOM lesson. Vol-scaling, leverage, and short-vol carry can manufacture attractive backtest stats that are really just risk-taking / dynamic leverage, not skill. Decompose returns into beta, leverage effect, and residual alpha before claiming an edge. (7) Negative skew / hidden tail risk — carry, short-vol, and pairs strategies earn small steady gains then suffer rare catastrophic losses. Sharpe ratio flatters negatively-skewed strategies; a 2.0 Sharpe that blows up once a decade is not a 2.0 Sharpe. Report skew, kurtosis, max drawdown, and tail metrics alongside Sharpe. (8) Overfit portfolio optimization — Markowitz mean-variance is an error-maximizer: it loads on estimation noise in the covariance/expected-return inputs. Use denoised covariances (Marchenko-Pastur clipping), shrinkage (Ledoit-Wolf), HRP/NCO, or robust/equal-risk methods. A beautiful optimizer on garbage inputs is garbage. (9) Library trust without audit — mlfinlab is the canonical example: it went CLOSED-SOURCE/paid in ~Aug 2020 (all-rights-reserved license), so the open GitHub version is frozen and unmaintained; the живой code is behind a subscription. Open forks (mlfinpy) exist but are not feature-complete or equally vetted. Zipline is effectively abandoned (use zipline-reloaded). Never assume a popular repo is current, correct, or license-clean. (10) Confusing breadth with independence — the Fundamental Law's sqrt(BR) assumes INDEPENDENT bets. 500 stocks driven by one macro factor is closer to 1 bet than 500. Crowding and common-factor exposure collapse effective breadth, so realized IR is far below the formula's promise.

**Honest validation:** (1) Combinatorial Purged Cross-Validation (CPCV) with embargo — the gold standard from Lopez de Prado. Generate many train/test splits (not one walk-forward path), PURGE training observations whose labels overlap the test set in time, and EMBARGO a buffer after each test block. Yields a DISTRIBUTION of out-of-sample Sharpes, not a single point estimate. Anti-Profit-Mirage because a single lucky path can't hide. (2) Deflated Sharpe Ratio (DSR) — record EVERY backtest run for a strategy (the trial count and the variance of trial Sharpes), then deflate the selected Sharpe for selection bias + non-normality (skew, kurtosis). A strategy that survives DSR with the honest trial count is one of the few that should reach paper trading. Open-source: esvhd/pypbo. (3) Probability of Backtest Overfitting (PBO) — via combinatorially-symmetric CV, estimate the probability that the in-sample best config underperforms the median config out-of-sample. PBO > ~0.5 means your selection process is essentially overfitting. Report it for every candidate. (4) Cost-and-capacity stress test BEFORE statistical significance — re-run net of realistic spreads, commissions, modeled market impact (scaled to your AUM), borrow cost/availability, and slippage. Then sweep position size to find the capacity at which the edge dies. Many strategies pass statistics and fail economics; check economics first to avoid wasting validation budget. (5) Multiple-testing / FDR control on the signal search — when scanning many features or parameter sets, apply a multiple-testing correction (Harvey's recommended t>3.0, or explicit FDR/Bonferroni-Holm). Pre-register the hypothesis economically ('why should this work?') BEFORE the backtest — theory-first beats mining-first. (6) Out-of-sample + post-publication decay monitor — reserve a true holdout that the research process never touched, and once live, track realized vs backtested performance with a decay alarm (McLean-Pontiff says to EXPECT ~50% haircut). A live IR that drifts below a floor triggers de-allocation, not parameter re-tuning. (7) Variance-of-trials / Minimum Backtest Length check — Bailey et al.: given the number of trials, there is a minimum backtest length below which an expected-Sharpe-of-2 is statistically meaningless. Confirm your sample length supports the claimed Sharpe at the trial count used. (8) Robustness / multiverse sweep — verify the edge is not a knife-edge of one lookback or threshold. Perturb lookbacks, rebalance dates, universe definitions, and cost assumptions; a real edge degrades gracefully, an overfit one collapses. Plot the Sharpe surface, not a single point.

**Data:** "For the canon's strategies and validation: (1) Survivorship-bias-FREE security master with delisted/dead tickers and point-in-time membership — this is non-negotiable and the single most expensive/important dataset (CRSP for US equities is the academic standard; commercial vendors for live). (2) Point-in-time fundamentals dated to FILING date not period-end (Compustat PIT or equivalent) to kill look-ahead. (3) Adjusted prices with corporate-action handling (splits, dividends, symbol changes). (4) For cross-asset trend/carry: a broad liquid-futures continuous-contract dataset (roll-adjusted) across equities/rates/FX/commodities, plus the term-structure (front vs deferred) for carry. (5) Borrow/short-availability and financing-rate data to model the short leg honestly. (6) Realistic transaction-cost inputs: historical spreads, volume/ADV for impact modeling. (7) Daily data suffices for the canonical book strategies (monthly/daily rebalance); intraday/tick only needed if pursuing the surviving stat-arb edges. Free sources (yfinance, Quandl/Nasdaq Data Link) are fine for LEARNING the methods but carry survivorship and accuracy issues that will produce Profit-Mirage on real research — budget for institutional data before allocating capital. Validation itself needs you to LOG every backtest run (trial count, per-trial returns) — that is a data-engineering requirement, not optional."

**Fits our stack:** "This family is the doctrinal spine that should be loaded into EVERY layer before a single strategy is coded.\n\nR3 (evidence): This is R3's foundation. The Harvey-Liu-Zhu t>3.0 rule, McLean-Pontiff decay numbers, and the DSR/PBO toolkit ARE the R3 evidence standard. Encode them as gates: no strategy advances from R5 to risk without (a) a logged trial count + DSR, (b) a PBO estimate, (c) a net-of-cost backtest, (d) an economic 'why this works' prior. This is the anti-Profit-Mirage immune system the operator asked for.\n\nR4 (data): Drives the data spec — survivorship-free + point-in-time fundamentals + borrow data are the hard requirements the canon implies. The library audit (mlfinlab closed/paid, zipline abandoned → zipline-reloaded, prefer nautilus_trader for execution-faithful backtests, vectorbt for fast research sweeps, qlib for ML pipelines) belongs in R4 tooling decisions.\n\nR5 (alpha): Supplies the actual signal formulas — the six concrete strategies above are R5-ready templates with lookbacks/parameters. Lopez de Prado's triple-barrier + meta-labeling is the R5 labeling/ML methodology; Grinold-Kahn IR=IC*sqrt(BR) is the R5 'is this worth building?' filter.\n\nRisk: Ilmanen's risk-premia taxonomy and the negative-skew/tail warnings map to risk-model design (carry/trend/pairs all need tail-aware sizing, not just vol-targeting).\n\nPortfolio: Lopez de Prado's denoising + HRP/NCO and Grinold-Kahn's characteristic portfolios are the portfolio-construction layer; the 'optimizer is an error-maximizer' pitfall mandates robust methods.\n\nExecution: Narang's execution-algo chapter + nautilus_trader's execution-faithful design address the implementation gap (backtest-to-live).\n\nIG-12 prompt deepened: This is the 'reading list / canon' prompt — the meta-prompt that should gate all other strategy-family prompts. It deepens IG-12 by converting a flat book list into a PRIORITY-ORDERED, skeptically-annotated curriculum with explicit validation gates, so the fund internalizes 'most edges are fragile' as a prior rather than learning it by losing money."

**Go deeper:** • READING ORDER (vocabulary → why → how-not-to-fool-yourself → ML): (1) Narang 'Inside the Black Box' for the map. (2) Ilmanen 'Expected Returns' for WHY premia exist. (3) Grinold-Kahn for the algebra (IR=IC*sqrt(BR)). (4) Chan 'Algorithmic Trading' for implementable recipes. (5) Lopez de Prado 'Machine Learning for Asset Managers' (short on-ramp) then 'Advances in Financial Machine Learning' (the deep one). Then the meta-papers below. • Meta-papers to read as a block (the validation canon): Harvey-Liu-Zhu '...and the Cross-Section of Expected Returns' (2016); Harvey 'Presidential Address: The Scientific Outlook in Financial Economics' (2017); Hou-Xue-Zhang 'Replicating Anomalies' (2020, ~65% fail); McLean-Pontiff (2016); Bailey-Lopez de Prado 'Deflated Sharpe Ratio' (2014) + 'Probability of Backtest Overfitting' (2015) + 'Pseudo-Mathematics and Financial Charlatanism' (2014, AMS Notices). • Strategy-foundation papers per style: Jegadeesh-Titman (1993, momentum); Moskowitz-Ooi-Pedersen 'Time Series Momentum' (2012) READ WITH its vol-scaling critiques; Asness-Moskowitz-Pedersen 'Value and Momentum Everywhere' (2013); Koijen-Moskowitz-Pedersen-Vrugt 'Carry' (2018); Frazzini-Pedersen 'Betting Against Beta' (2014); Asness-Frazzini-Pedersen 'Quality Minus Junk'; Gatev-Goetzmann-Rouwenhorst 'Pairs Trading' (2006) READ WITH its post-2002 decay. • Blog ecosystem (free, high-signal): Quantocracy (the aggregator/mashup — start here daily), Alpha Architect (factor research, evidence-based, top clickthrough), Robot Wealth (algo/ML/FX, practitioner), QuantStart (Halls-Moore, Python implementation tutorials), Hudson & Thames blog (Lopez de Prado methods, but note the commercial bias), Quantitative Trading (Ernie Chan's own blog, epchan.blogspot.com), Robert Carver / Investment Idiocy ('Systematic Trading' author, excellent on cost/sizing realism). • Open-source library landscape (audit before trusting): nautilus_trader (execution-faithful backtest→live, Rust core, best for the implementation gap); vectorbt (Numba-vectorized, millions of trades/sec, best for research parameter sweeps — but easy to overfit fast); qlib (Microsoft, AI/ML quant pipeline with built-in models); zipline-reloaded (community fork; original zipline is abandoned); backtrader (mature event-driven, good for retail/learning); mlfinlab (NOW CLOSED/PAID since Aug 2020 — open GitHub is frozen; use mlfinpy fork or implement from the book for license-clean code). Supporting: QuantStats (tearsheets), PyPortfolioOpt (optimization), pypbo (overfitting tests). • Courses & repositories: arXiv q-fin (quant finance preprints — q-fin.PM portfolio mgmt, q-fin.TR trading), SSRN q-fin/financial-economics network (working papers, often pre-publication so READ WITH decay-skepticism), WorldQuant University (free accredited MSc in Financial Engineering), Coursera Columbia Financial Engineering specialization, Hudson & Thames + QuantInsti EPAT (paid, practitioner). GitHub: paperswithbacktest/awesome-systematic-trading and stefan-jansen/machine-learning-for-trading (the best free code companion to the canon). • Robert Carver's 'Systematic Trading' (2015) and 'Advanced Futures Trading Strategies' (2023) deserve a slot the operator's list omits — Carver is the most honest practitioner voice on position sizing, cost-aware strategy selection, and the discipline of NOT over-engineering. Strong complement to the academic canon for an actually-trading fund. • Two more validation-critical reads not on the original list: Suhonen-Lennkh-Perez 'Quantifying Backtest Overfitting in Alternative Beta Strategies' (2017, real-money decay evidence) and 'Why and how systematic strategies decay' (Hamill et al., arXiv 2105.01380) — both give live, money-managed decay numbers that complement the academic McLean-Pontiff result.
